# Glutter: Specification

| | |
|---|---|
| Status | Draft 0.1 |
| Date | 2026-10-18 |
| Owner | Manos Ragiadakos |
| Module | `github.com/rm4n0s/glutter` |
| License | BSD-3-Clause |

Glutter is a GUI toolkit for Go that reproduces Flutter's framework API on its own Go engine. An app is a normal Go program built with `CGO_ENABLED=0 go build`: no Dart, no Skia, no C compiler, no development packages (Android, which needs the NDK's C compiler, is the one exception, §24). Version 1.0 is a complete desktop toolkit for Linux (Wayland and X11), including a system tray. Version 1.1 adds a Vulkan renderer on Linux, used when the machine has Vulkan, with OpenGL ES as the fallback, and the web: WebAssembly drawn with WebGPU, or with WebGL 2 where the browser has no WebGPU (§23). Version 1.2 adds what a code editor built on Glutter needs: fonts loaded at run time, fonts in `gluttertest`, and a fallback order that tries the listed font families before the system default (§10). Version 1.3 adds font queries and the desktop's font settings, a glyph cache that drops the oldest entry, `TreeSliver` and `ReorderableListView`. Version 1.4 adds tab stops in paragraph layout and a cache of shaped runs (§10). Version 1.5 lets an app hide a window and show it again, so that an app with a tray icon closes its window to the tray (§8.7), and gives the desktop app lifecycle from the windows (§13). Version 1.6 adds Android: an APK built by `glutter-android` from the same app code, with a small Java embedding like Flutter's, Vulkan with OpenGL ES as the fallback, touch, the soft keyboard and IMEs, TalkBack, predictive back, services (opening a URI, notifications), and the Android behaviors of the framework (overscroll glow and stretch, the Material text selection toolbar, the magnifier, predictive back transitions, spell check); every example but `multiwindow` and `tray` runs on a phone (§24). Everything specific to an OS or GPU sits behind two internal interfaces, so Windows, macOS and mobile can be added later without changing the framework.

---

## 1. Goals

| # | Goal |
|---|---|
| G1 | **Faithful to Flutter.** It has the same three trees (Widget → Element → RenderObject), the same layout protocol (constraints go down, sizes go up), and the same lifecycle, names, defaults and behavior. A Flutter developer can follow Flutter's documentation and translate it mechanically using the rules in §12 |
| G2 | **Required parameters are checked at compile time.** Flutter's `required` becomes a positional constructor argument (§12.1) |
| G3 | **Easy to build.** You need only Go: no C compiler, no `pkg-config`, no `-devel` packages. Cross-compiling with `GOOS`/`GOARCH` works (§16) |
| G4 | **A complete desktop toolkit** with the scope in §3 |
| G5 | **Portable by construction.** OS, windowing and GPU code is isolated behind the interfaces in §6.3 |
| G6 | **Fast.** The targets are in §15. When nothing changes, CPU use is close to zero |
| G7 | **Testable without a display.** Widget tests and golden images run without a display server or GPU (§17) |

## 2. Non-goals for 1.0

- Hot reload, or a hot restart that keeps state. Go can't do this; we offer only a fast rebuild-and-run.
- Cupertino widgets. Planned after 1.0.
- Windows, macOS, Android and iOS backends. The design supports them (§6.4), but they aren't implemented. The web comes in 1.1 (§23), Android in 1.6 (§24).
- TinyGo. Apps are built with the standard Go toolchain only, for the web too (D18).
- Dart interop, Flutter plugins, and platform channels as a message codec. Go APIs are exposed directly instead.
- Custom fragment shaders (`FragmentProgram`), 3D, video playback, WebView, SVG, printing.
- DevTools and the widget inspector UI. Debug paint flags and tree dumps are in scope (§14.3).
- Material 2 visuals. Only Material 3 is supported.
- The XEmbed system tray. Only StatusNotifierItem is supported (§8.9).
- State restoration (`RestorationMixin`, `RestorationScope`).
- Internationalization beyond the mechanism. `Localizations` works, but only English is shipped for `MaterialLocalizations` and `WidgetsLocalizations`.

## 3. Scope of 1.0 (Linux)

| Area | In scope |
|---|---|
| Windowing | Wayland (primary) and X11. Multiple windows, dialog windows, fullscreen/maximize/minimize, min/max size, window icon, app id, title bars drawn by the app when the compositor doesn't draw them |
| Input | Mouse, high-resolution scrolling, touchpad pan/zoom gestures, touchscreens, keyboard (any XKB layout, compose/dead keys), IME (Wayland text-input-v3; on X11, IBus or Fcitx5 over D-Bus) |
| Data exchange | Clipboard (text, images, files), primary selection, drag and drop with other apps and within the app |
| Rendering | Antialiased paths, clips, transforms, all of Flutter's gradients, images, opacity and layers, blur, shadows, color filters, blend modes, backdrop filters, shader masks |
| Text | System fonts and fallback, rich text, bidi, line breaking, ellipsis, decorations, inline widgets, full text editing with IME |
| Framework | foundation, scheduler, animation, physics, gestures, painting, rendering, semantics, services and widgets, covering everything listed in §11 |
| Material 3 | The full component set listed in §11.2, plus theming including `ColorScheme.fromSeed` |
| Desktop services | System tray with a menu, notifications, file open/save dialogs, opening URIs, dark/light mode and accent color from system settings |
| Accessibility | The semantics tree exposed to screen readers through AT-SPI2 (Orca) |

**Scope of 1.1** (milestones M19–M21, after 1.0):

| Area | In scope |
|---|---|
| Vulkan | A Vulkan renderer on Linux (§9.3, §9.4), used by default where Vulkan works, with GLES as the fallback (§9.6) |
| Web | `GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm` with the standard Go toolchain: a web platform backend (§23), the WebGPU renderer (§9.5), WebGL 2 through `gles` (§9.1), a 2D-canvas fallback, and `cmd/glutter-web` to build, serve and screenshot an app |
| Not on the web in 1.1 | accessibility (an ARIA tree), several views on one page, system fonts and downloaded fallback fonts, drag and drop, file dialogs, the tray |

**Scope of 1.2** (milestone M22, after 1.1; §10, §11.3): fonts loaded at run time (`LoadFontFromList`, `FontLoader`, `RunAppOpts.Fonts`), `gluttertest.Opts.Fonts`, the listed families before the system default; then font queries and the desktop's font settings, text benchmarks and an LRU glyph cache, `TreeSliver`, `ReorderableListView`.

**Scope of 1.4** (milestone M22, steps 22.9 to 22.11; §10, §11.3): tab stops in paragraph layout (`ParagraphStyleOpts.TabSize`, `TextPainterOpts.TabSize`) and a cache of shaped runs in the text engine.

**Scope of 1.5** (milestone M23, step 23.1; §8.2, §8.3, §8.7, §13): `RegularWindowController.SetHidden` and `IsHidden` on X11 and Wayland (ignored on the web), and the desktop lifecycle (`Resumed`, `Inactive`, `Hidden`) given by the windows.

## 4. Decisions log

| # | Decision | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| D1 | Flutter's API is translated with **positional required arguments plus a trailing options struct** ("Version 2") | §12. Rejected: struct literals (no compile-time required check), constructors returning `(Widget, error)`, and constructors returning error widgets |
| D2 | **No cgo.** The system's C libraries are called through `github.com/ebitengine/purego` (Apache-2.0, from the Ebiten team): `dlopen`, C calls and C→Go callbacks without a C compiler. They are libwayland-client/-egl/-cursor, libxkbcommon(-x11), libX11 (+X11-xcb, Xi, Xrandr, Xcursor), libEGL and libGLESv2 | Supersedes the earlier "cgo allowed". Same libraries as Gio uses, called without cgo, as Ebiten does. §8.1, §16. Android is the one exception (D21) |
| D3 | **Every system library is loaded at runtime** with `dlopen`, nothing is linked at build time | A missing library becomes a tagged error (`LibraryLoadFailed`, `EGLLoadFailed`) instead of a loader failure before `main`, and a missing Wayland library falls back to X11. Lookup rules are in §8.1 |
| D4 | `github.com/go-text/typesetting` is an allowed dependency (fonts, shaping, bidi, segmentation, system font discovery) | §10 |
| D5 | Scope is **Widgets + Material 3**. Cupertino comes later | §11 |
| D6 | Linux first: **Wayland primary, X11 supported**, selected at runtime | §8 |
| D7 | Renderer: **OpenGL ES 3.0 through EGL**, with GLSL ES 3.00 shaders | The same shaders run on WebGL2, ANGLE (Windows, macOS) and Android. §9 |
| D8 | Desktop extras in 1.0: system tray, notifications, file dialogs, drag and drop, multiple windows, accessibility | §8.7–8.10 |
| D9 | Errors use `github.com/rm4n0s/errors` | §14 |
| D10 | Our own engine. Gio and Ebiten aren't used | Glutter owns every layer above the system libraries |
| D11 | Go 1.27 minimum | Generic type aliases (1.24+) are used for re-exports |
| D12 | `golang.org/x/image` is an allowed dependency (v0.47.0 at the time of writing) | WebP and BMP decoding (§7) |
| D13 | Navigator ships **both** the imperative API and the Pages/Router API in 1.0 | M10 |
| D14 | D-Bus through `github.com/godbus/dbus/v5` (pure Go, BSD-2; Gio and Fyne use it too) | Calls are converted to Go types automatically, and Go objects can be served directly, which the tray, its menu and AT-SPI2 need. `internal/dbus` needs no cgo |
| D15 | Bugs panic, and **the library recovers** per element/render object/callback, reports through `ErrorReporter` and shows `ErrorWidgetBuilder`'s widget | §14.2. Rejected: returning errors from `Build`, and crashing like Gio and Ebiten |
| D16 | **Wayland must work flawlessly** (§8.11). Two more C libraries, loaded through purego and optional: **libdecor** (native title bars on GNOME) and **libfontconfig** (font matching like Flutter's Linux engine) | Without them, Glutter falls back to its own title bar and to `fontscan` |
| D17 | **Vulkan on Linux, by default where it works; GLES is the fallback** | `libvulkan.so.1` is loaded through purego like EGL (D2, D3). The renderer is chosen once per process, at the first window (§9.6). GLES stays complete and tested: it is the fallback on Linux and the WebGL 2 renderer on the web |
| D18 | **The web through the standard Go toolchain** (`GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm`): **WebGPU when the browser has it, WebGL 2 otherwise** | TinyGo is not supported. Tried on 2026-10-21 with TinyGo 0.43 and Go 1.27: it cannot compile `hash/maphash`, its `int` is 32 bits on wasm, its compiler crashed on `examples/counter`, and `internal/goid` reads `runtime.Stack`, which TinyGo does not fill. §23 |
| D19 | **One renderer for the explicit GPU APIs** (`internal/gpu/explicit`), written against a small device interface that `vulkan` and `webgpu` implement | WebGPU's default limits are the design limits (§9.3), so what the renderer does on Vulkan it can do on WebGPU. Rejected: two separate renderers (twice the code), and wgpu-native or Dawn through purego (not a system library, so every app would have to ship it) |
| D20 | **The shaders of the explicit renderer are WGSL.** `cmd/glutter-gen shaders` compiles them to SPIR-V with `naga`, and the output is checked in | Building an app still needs only Go (G3); `naga` is needed only to change a shader. WebGPU takes WGSL as it is, and naga's WGSL → SPIR-V path is the one wgpu uses for every Vulkan app. The GLES shaders stay GLSL ES 3.00 (§9.1); the parity tests keep the two sets drawing the same |
| D21 | **Android (1.6) uses cgo, in one package only**: `internal/platform/android`, in `*_android.go` files: the JNI entry points and the calls into the NDK. Everything else stays cgo-free, and `CGO_ENABLED=0 go build ./...` keeps passing for Linux and the web | Decided on 2026-10-23. An Android app is a shared library loaded by Android, which Go builds only with cgo (`-buildmode=c-shared`), and purego itself needs cgo on Android. The `gles` and `vulkan` renderers keep loading their libraries through purego. §24.2 |
| D22 | **A small Java embedding**, ported from Flutter's (`FlutterActivity`, `FlutterView`, `InputConnectionAdaptor`, `AccessibilityBridge`), talking to Go through JNI. Its `classes.dex` is generated by `glutter-gen android-dex` and checked in. No Gradle, AndroidX or Kotlin | Decided on 2026-10-23. A `NativeActivity` alone has no input connection (so no IME composition) and no accessibility provider; Java classes are the only way to have them. Like the SPIR-V of D20, the checked-in dex keeps a JDK out of building an app. §24.2 |
| D23 | **Vulkan on Android where the device's Vulkan is good, OpenGL ES as the fallback**, as Flutter's Impeller does | Decided on 2026-10-23. The explicit renderer and its WGSL shaders are shared with Linux and the web (D19). Older Android drivers are known to be poor, hence Android 10 and Vulkan 1.1 as the minimum, and Impeller's deny list. §24.4 |
| D24 | **Android 8.0 (API 26) is the minimum**; the target is API 37 | Chosen when M25 was planned (2026-10-23), not yet confirmed by the owner. It covers almost every device in use; newer APIs (insets types, back callbacks, the keyboard's animation) are used where the device has them. Raising it is the owner's decision. §24.1 |

## 5. Size estimate

These are production lines of Go, excluding tests (expect about as many test lines). They're calibrated against Flutter's framework (330k lines of Dart), Gio (40k lines of Go) and go-text (58k lines of Go).

| Layer | Lines |
|---|---|
| Platform (Wayland, X11, input, IME, clipboard, drag and drop, window decorations, purego bindings and generator) | 13–19k |
| Renderer (GLES and software) | 15–25k |
| Text layout and editing support (on go-text) | 5–8k |
| Framework core (foundation … widgets) | 80–110k |
| Material 3 | 40–60k |
| Desktop services (D-Bus, tray, notifications, portals, AT-SPI2) | 15–25k |
| **Total** | **~175–245k** |

---

## 6. Architecture

### 6.1 Layers

```
 app code (Go)
 ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
 material             Material 3 widgets, theming, ink, icons
 widgets              Widget/Element/State, every core widget, Navigator, focus, editing
 rendering semantics  RenderObject tree, layout and paint, layers, semantics tree
 painting gestures services animation physics scheduler foundation
 desktop              tray, notifications, file dialogs, open URI
 ─────────────────────────── public engine API ────────────────────────
 ui                   Canvas, Paint, Path, Picture, Scene, Paragraph, Image,
                      PlatformDispatcher, View, pointer/key data, semantics updates
 ─────────────────────────── internal ─────────────────────────────────
 internal/engine      implements the ui contracts using the three below
 internal/platform    Platform interface ── linux/wayland, linux/x11, web, android, fake
 internal/gpu         Renderer interface ── vulkan, gles, soft (Linux); webgpu, gles on WebGL 2, canvas2d (web);
                      vulkan, gles, soft into the ANativeWindow (Android)
                      explicit: the renderer that vulkan and webgpu share
 internal/text        paragraph layout on go-text
 internal/dbus        D-Bus connection and services (tray, notify, portals, AT-SPI2)
 ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
 system, all loaded with dlopen through purego (no cgo):
   libwayland-client/-egl/-cursor, libxkbcommon(-x11), libX11 (+X11-xcb, Xi, Xrandr, Xcursor),
   libEGL, libGLESv2, libvulkan, libdecor, libfontconfig;  session D-Bus through godbus (pure Go)
 browser, through syscall/js: the DOM, WebGPU, WebGL 2, the 2D canvas
 Android (1.6): the Java embedding through JNI, and libandroid, liblog through cgo (D21);
   libEGL, libGLESv3, libvulkan through purego
```

`ui` plays the role of Flutter's `dart:ui`, and `internal/engine` plays the role of Flutter's C++ engine. The framework packages talk only to `ui`.

### 6.2 Package layout

```
glutter/
  ui/                    engine API (dart:ui)
  foundation/            Key, Opt[T], ChangeNotifier, TargetPlatform, diagnostics, debug flags
  scheduler/             SchedulerBinding, Ticker, frame phases
  animation/             AnimationController, Tween, Curves, ...
  physics/               simulations
  gestures/              pointer events, hit testing, gesture arena, recognizers
  painting/              EdgeInsets, BoxDecoration, borders, TextPainter, ImageProvider, ...
  rendering/             RenderObject, RenderBox, slivers, layers, PipelineOwner
  semantics/             SemanticsNode, actions, flags
  services/              keyboard, text input, clipboard, mouse cursors, assets
  widgets/               the widgets library
  material/              Material 3 (re-exports widgets; §12.11)
  desktop/               tray, notifications, file dialogs, open URI
  gluttertest/           WidgetTester, finders, golden comparison (flutter_test)
  internal/
    engine/              wires platform + gpu + text into the ui contracts
    platform/            Platform interface, shared types
      linux/             event loop, settings, selection of Wayland or X11
        wayland/         purego; Go protocol bindings generated from protocols/*.xml
        x11/             purego
      web/               syscall/js: canvas, DOM events, the IME text field, clipboard (§23)
      android/           cgo and JNI: the view's surface, the looper, input, the input connection, insets (§24)
      fake/              scripted platform for tests
    gpu/                 Renderer interface, Scene flattening, glyph and path atlases
      explicit/          renderer for the explicit GPU APIs: Device interface, WGSL shaders, generated SPIR-V (§9.3)
      vulkan/            purego (libvulkan): explicit.Device (§9.4)
      webgpu/            syscall/js: explicit.Device (§9.5)
      gles/              purego (EGL + GLES 3.0); syscall/js (WebGL 2)
      canvas2d/          syscall/js: soft's image put into a 2D canvas, the web's last fallback (§9.6)
      nativewindow/      purego (libandroid): soft's image put into the ANativeWindow, Android's last fallback (§24.4)
      soft/              pure-Go rasterizer (goldens, headless)
    text/                paragraph layout, font collection, glyph outlines
    dbus/                pure Go (godbus): tray, notifications, portals, settings, AT-SPI2
  cmd/glutter-gen/       generators: Wayland protocols, key tables, re-exports, CopyWith, icons; and apicheck, the API conventions check
  cmd/glutter-web/       builds, serves and screenshots an app for the web (§23.1)
  cmd/glutter-android/   builds, installs, runs and screenshots an app on Android (§24.1)
  internal/androidtool/  APK packaging, the SDK and NDK finder, the Java embedding and its checked-in classes.dex (§24.2)
  internal/androidtest/  the emulator harness of the device tests (§24.13)
  examples/              counter, gallery, tray, multiwindow, editor
  third_party/flutter/   Flutter's LICENSE (we port from BSD-3 code)
  scripts/               fetch-reference.sh (pinned Flutter sources into reference/)
  reference/             git-ignored Flutter sources for porting (§20)
  docs/                  SPEC.md, IMPLEMENTATION.md, IMPLEMENTED.md
```

### 6.3 Portability contract

The two internal interfaces are the only places where an OS, windowing system or GPU API can appear. Here is a sketch of the method sets; the details are decided at milestone M1:

```go
// internal/platform
type Platform interface {
	Run(ctx context.Context, h Handler) error             // owns the UI thread's event loop
	Post(fn func())                                       // safe from any goroutine; wakes the loop
	NewWindow(cfg WindowConfig) (Window, error)
	Displays() []Display
	Clipboard() Clipboard
	Settings() Settings                                   // brightness, accent color, text scale, reduce motion, ...
	Services() Services                                   // optional capabilities; nil when absent
}

type Window interface {
	ID() ViewID
	NativeSurface() gpu.SurfaceHandle                     // opaque to everything except gpu backends
	SetTitle(string); SetSize(ui.Size); SetState(WindowState); /* ... */
	SetCursor(ui.MouseCursor)
	SetTextInput(ui.TextInputState)                        // IME: enable, cursor rectangle, surrounding text
	StartDrag(DragData) error
	Close()
}

type Handler interface { // implemented by internal/engine
	OnPointer([]ui.PointerData); OnKey(ui.KeyData); OnTextInput(ui.TextInputEvent)
	OnFrame(ViewID, time.Duration); OnMetrics(ViewID, ui.ViewMetrics); OnLifecycle(ui.AppLifecycleState)
	OnDrop(ViewID, DropEvent); OnSettings(Settings); OnContentLost(ViewID); OnCloseRequest(ViewID) bool
}

// internal/gpu
type Renderer interface {
	Attach(gpu.SurfaceHandle) (Surface, error)
	Render(s Surface, scene *ui.Scene, size ui.Size, dpr float32) error
	Snapshot(scene *ui.Scene, size ui.Size) (*image.RGBA, error) // toImage, raster cache
	Close() error
}
```

Rules:
1. Public packages never import `internal/platform/<os>`, `internal/gpu/gles` or `internal/dbus`. Only `internal/engine` chooses backends.
2. No `runtime.GOOS` and no OS build tags outside the backend packages. Framework behavior that differs by platform (scroll physics, shortcuts such as Ctrl vs Cmd, menu conventions, typography) is driven by `foundation.TargetPlatform`. That value comes from the platform, and tests can override it, as with Flutter's `debugDefaultTargetPlatformOverride`.
   Two exceptions, both files that only choose and hold no logic: `internal/engine` picks its backends in `backends_<goos>.go`, and `foundation.IsWeb` (Flutter's `kIsWeb`) is a constant set in `foundation/web_js.go` and `foundation/web_other.go`, which also give `DefaultTargetPlatform` its host value (Linux on desktop; on the web the value the browser reported through `foundation.SetBrowserTargetPlatform`, step 21.1). On Android, `foundation/host_android.go` gives `DefaultTargetPlatform` the value `TargetPlatformAndroid` (§24.12). Every package whose files all carry an OS tag keeps an untagged `doc.go`, so `./...` matches it on every OS. `GOOS=android` satisfies `linux`, so a file meant only for the Linux desktop says `linux && !android` (§24).
3. Desktop services (`Services`: tray, notifications, file chooser, open URI, accessibility bridge) are optional capabilities. A missing one returns a tagged error such as `TrayUnavailable`. It never panics.
4. Shaders are GLSL ES 3.00 in `gles` and WGSL in `explicit` (D20). A GLES call outside `internal/gpu/gles`, a Vulkan call outside `internal/gpu/vulkan` or a WebGPU call outside `internal/gpu/webgpu` is a bug.
5. Backend selection on Linux: `GLUTTER_BACKEND=wayland|x11` if set. Otherwise Wayland when `WAYLAND_DISPLAY` connects, then X11 through `DISPLAY`, then the error `NoDisplay`. The renderer is chosen as §9.6 says.
6. Nothing is linked at build time, so one binary contains both backends. A missing library only disables the backend that needs it.
7. There is no `import "C"` anywhere, except in the `*_android.go` files of `internal/platform/android` (D21). `CGO_ENABLED=0 go build ./...` must always succeed for Linux, from step 19.1 on so must `GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm go build ./...`, and from step 25.1 on `scripts/check-android.sh` (the module built and vetted for `android/arm64` and `android/amd64` with the NDK).
8. `syscall/js` is imported only in `internal/platform/web`, `internal/gpu/webgpu`, `internal/gpu/canvas2d` and the WebGL 2 files of `internal/gpu/gles`.
9. JNI, C types and NDK calls stay inside `internal/platform/android` (§24.2). The GPU backends get the Android window only as the `uintptr` of a `gpu.SurfaceHandle` of kind `SurfaceKindAndroid`, and the `ANativeWindow` calls of `internal/gpu/nativewindow` are loaded through purego from `libandroid.so`.

### 6.4 What a future port has to implement

| Target | Platform backend | GPU backend | Services |
|---|---|---|---|
| Windows | Win32 through `syscall` (no cgo): windows, IME (IMM32/TSF), OLE drag and drop, clipboard | `gles` on bundled ANGLE (`libEGL.dll`, `libGLESv2.dll`), the same approach as Flutter's Windows engine | Shell_NotifyIcon tray, toast notifications, IFileDialog, UI Automation |
| macOS | Cocoa through purego's `objc` package (no cgo) | `gles` on ANGLE/Metal; a native Metal backend later | NSStatusItem, UNUserNotificationCenter, NSOpenPanel, NSAccessibility |
| Web (done in 1.1, §23) | `internal/platform/web` through `syscall/js`: canvas element, DOM events, hidden text field for IME | `webgpu` (§9.5), else `gles` on WebGL 2 (the same API and shaders), else `canvas2d` | OpenURI and the Notifications API (§23.7); later: the ARIA tree, file dialogs |
| Android (1.6, §24) | the Java embedding and JNI through cgo (D21, D22): one of the two targets where cgo is unavoidable, since purego requires cgo on Android | `vulkan`, else `gles`, else `soft` into the `ANativeWindow` (D23) | open URI and notifications through intents; TalkBack through an `AccessibilityNodeProvider` |
| iOS | UIKit through cgo (iOS apps must link statically) | ANGLE/Metal | platform equivalents |

None of these ports needs changes above `internal/`.

**GPU libraries on Windows and macOS.** Neither OS provides EGL/GLES, so a `gles`-based port **ships ANGLE with the app**:
- **Windows**: `libEGL.dll` and `libGLESv2.dll` next to the `.exe`, loaded with `LoadLibraryEx(…, LOAD_LIBRARY_SEARCH_DEFAULT_DIRS)` (the app directory, then System32; never the current directory or `PATH`), as Gio does. `d3dcompiler_47.dll` comes with Windows 10+.
- **macOS**: the `.dylib`s in `Contents/Frameworks/`, loaded by an absolute path derived from the executable and code-signed with the app.

The alternative is native backends (Direct3D 11, Metal) behind `internal/gpu`. That's Gio's default, needs no bundled files, and costs one renderer and shader language per OS. Choose when the port starts.

---

## 7. Engine API (`ui`)

This is the Go translation of `dart:ui`. Values are immutable unless the type is a builder or recorder.

| Group | Types and functions |
|---|---|
| Geometry | `Offset`, `Size`, `Rect`, `RRect`, `RSuperellipse`, `Radius`, `Matrix4` (float32 column-major), `Rect.Intersect`/`ExpandToInclude`/`Inflate`, lerps |
| Color | `Color` (float32 ARGB in extended sRGB, as in current Flutter), `ColorSpace`, `BlendMode` (all 29 of Flutter's modes), `FilterQuality` |
| Paint | `Paint` (color, shader, blend mode, style, stroke width/cap/join/miter limit, `MaskFilter`, `ColorFilter`, `ImageFilter`, anti-alias, invert colors) |
| Shaders | `GradientLinear`, `GradientRadial` (including two-point conical), `GradientSweep`, `ImageShader`, `TileMode` |
| Filters | `ColorFilterMode`, `ColorFilterMatrix`, `ColorFilterLinearToSrgbGamma`/`SrgbToLinearGamma`, `ImageFilterBlur`, `ImageFilterMatrix`, `ImageFilterCompose`, `ImageFilterDilate`/`Erode`, `MaskFilterBlur` (`BlurStyle`) |
| Path | `Path` (MoveTo, LineTo, QuadraticBezierTo, CubicTo, ConicTo, ArcTo, ArcToPoint, relative variants, AddRect/RRect/Oval/Arc/Polygon/Path, Close, FillType, Transform, Shift, GetBounds, Contains, `PathCombine` with `PathOperation`), `PathMetrics` (length, extract path, tangent) |
| Canvas | `PictureRecorder`, `Canvas` (Save/SaveLayer/Restore/RestoreToCount, Translate/Scale/Rotate/Skew/Transform, ClipRect/RRect/RSuperellipse/Path, DrawColor/Paint/Line/Rect/RRect/DRRect/Oval/Circle/Arc/Path/Points/Vertices/Atlas/Image/ImageRect/ImageNine/Paragraph/Picture/Shadow), `Picture` (`ToImage`) |
| Compositing | `SceneBuilder` (PushOffset, PushTransform, PushClipRect/RRect/Path, PushOpacity, PushColorFilter, PushImageFilter, PushBackdropFilter, PushShaderMask, AddPicture, AddRetained, Pop), `Scene`, `EngineLayer` handles for retained layers |
| Images | `Image`, `InstantiateImageCodec(ctx, []byte)` → `Codec` (PNG, JPEG and GIF including animation through the standard library; WebP and BMP through `golang.org/x/image`), `DecodeImageFromPixels` |
| Text | `ParagraphBuilder`, `ParagraphStyle`, `TextStyle`, `StrutStyle`, `Paragraph` (Layout, Width, Height, MinIntrinsicWidth, MaxIntrinsicWidth, AlphabeticBaseline, IdeographicBaseline, LongestLine, DidExceedMaxLines, GetBoxesForRange, GetBoxesForPlaceholders, GetPositionForOffset, GetWordBoundary, GetLineBoundary, ComputeLineMetrics, GetGlyphInfoAt), `TextDirection`, `TextAlign`, `TextDecoration`, `FontWeight`, `FontStyle`, `FontFeature`, `FontVariation`, `PlaceholderAlignment`, `LoadFontFromList` |
| Platform | `PlatformDispatcher` (views, frame callbacks `OnBeginFrame`/`OnDrawFrame`, `ScheduleFrame`, pointer data packets, key data, locales, text scale factor, platform brightness, accessibility features, `OnSemanticsActionEvent`, app lifecycle, `RequestAppExit`), `FlutterView` → `View` (ID, physical size, device pixel ratio, view padding/insets, display, `Render(scene)`, `UpdateSemantics`) |
| Input data | `PointerData` (change, kind: mouse/touch/trackpad/stylus, device, position, buttons, scroll deltas, pan/zoom), `KeyData` (type, physical, logical, character, synthesized), `TextInputEvent` |
| Semantics | `SemanticsUpdateBuilder`, `SemanticsAction`, `SemanticsFlag`, `SemanticsRole` |

Numbers follow Dart's types: `Offset`, `Size`, `Rect`, `Radius`, `PointerData` and other geometry-like values use `float64` (Dart's `double`), and only `Matrix4` and `Color` use `float32`. Enums that Flutter declares start with `<Enum>Unspecified` as the zero value, followed by Flutter's values in declaration order (§12.3).

`ui` holds no global state. `RunApp` creates the `PlatformDispatcher` and hands it to the binding (§13).

---

## 8. Linux platform layer

### 8.1 System libraries

Everything is loaded with `purego.Dlopen` at startup (D2, D3). The versioned name is tried first, because the unversioned symlink exists only when the `-devel` package is installed.

| Library | Names tried | Needed for | If missing |
|---|---|---|---|
| libwayland-client | `libwayland-client.so.0` | Wayland | fall back to X11 |
| libwayland-egl | `libwayland-egl.so.1` | Wayland + EGL | fall back to X11 |
| libwayland-cursor | `libwayland-cursor.so.0` | cursors without `wp_cursor_shape_v1` | default cursor only |
| libxkbcommon | `libxkbcommon.so.0` | keymaps and compose, both backends | `LibraryLoadFailed` |
| libxkbcommon-x11 | `libxkbcommon-x11.so.0` | X11 keymaps | `LibraryLoadFailed` on X11 |
| libX11, libX11-xcb | `libX11.so.6`, `libX11-xcb.so.1` | X11 | X11 unavailable |
| libXi, libXrandr, libXcursor | `libXi.so.6`, `libXrandr.so.2`, `libXcursor.so.1` | X11 input, monitors, cursors | that X11 feature is disabled |
| libEGL | `libEGL.so.1`, `libEGL.so` | rendering | `EGLLoadFailed` |
| libGLESv2 | `libGLESv2.so.2`, `libGLESv2.so` | rendering | `EGLLoadFailed` |
| libvulkan | `libvulkan.so.1` | the Vulkan renderer (§9.4) | GLES (§9.6) |
| libdecor | `libdecor-0.so.0` (loads its own GTK or cairo plugin) | native title bars on Wayland compositors without server-side decorations (GNOME), §8.2 | Glutter draws the title bar itself |
| libfontconfig | `libfontconfig.so.1` | font matching and fallback that follow the user's fontconfig settings, as Flutter's Linux engine does, §10 | go-text's `fontscan` |

**How the libraries are found.**
- `dlopen` gets a bare name with no path, so the dynamic linker (`ld.so`) searches exactly as it does for linked libraries: `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`, then `/etc/ld.so.cache` (built by `ldconfig` from `/etc/ld.so.conf.d/`), then `/lib` and `/usr/lib`. That covers every distro's library directory (`/usr/lib64`, `/usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu`, `/usr/lib`).
- `GLUTTER_EGL_LIBRARY`, `GLUTTER_GLES_LIBRARY` and `GLUTTER_VULKAN_LIBRARY` (absolute paths) override the lookup for unusual setups, e.g. NixOS without `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`.
- On failure, the error carries the names tried and `dlerror()` as metadata.

**Rules for calling C through purego.**
1. **No C variadic functions.** purego can't call them. Use the non-variadic variant: Wayland requests go through `wl_proxy_marshal_array_flags`, not `wl_proxy_marshal_flags`. This is also why X11 IME uses D-Bus instead of XIM (§8.3).
2. **Callbacks are created once, at startup.** A process can have about 2,000 C→Go callbacks, and they are never freed. Each callback dispatches by its user-data pointer. For example, all Wayland events go through **one** dispatcher installed with `wl_proxy_add_dispatcher`. Callback arguments must fit in a pointer.
3. **C structs mirrored in Go** (`wl_interface`, `wl_message`, `wl_argument`, `XEvent` and friends, xkb and EGL types) each have a layout test. It checks `unsafe.Sizeof`/`Offsetof` against values taken from the C headers and recorded in the test. Data that C keeps (interface tables, strings) lives in C memory, allocated with libc `malloc` through purego, never in Go memory.
4. Only the UI thread calls into these libraries.

**Wayland protocol bindings.** `cmd/glutter-gen wayland` reads the protocol XML files kept in `internal/platform/linux/wayland/protocols/` (MIT licensed) and writes Go code (`*_gen.go`): typed request methods, decoded events, and the `wl_interface` tables. Neither users nor contributors need `wayland-scanner` or `wayland-protocols`.

On Linux, libEGL and libGLESv2 come from libglvnd, which dispatches to Mesa or NVIDIA using the vendor files in `/usr/share/glvnd/egl_vendor.d/`. Every Wayland desktop has them, because the compositors draw through EGL themselves. GPU-less machines get Mesa's llvmpipe (GLES 3.2 in software).


### 8.2 Wayland

| Protocol | Required? | Without it |
|---|---|---|
| `wl_compositor`, `wl_subcompositor`, `wl_shm`, `wl_seat`, `wl_output`, `xdg_wm_base` | required | error `WaylandProtocolMissing` |
| `zxdg_decoration_manager_v1` | optional | libdecor, else Glutter's own title bar (see "Window decorations" below) |
| `wp_fractional_scale_v1` + `wp_viewporter` | optional | integer `wl_surface.set_buffer_scale` (from `wl_surface.preferred_buffer_scale`, else the scale of the outputs the surface is on) |
| `wp_cursor_shape_v1` | optional | XCursor theme through wayland-cursor |
| `zwp_text_input_manager_v3` | optional | no IME; raw keys plus xkb compose only |
| `wl_data_device_manager` | optional (always present in practice) | no clipboard or drag and drop |
| `zwp_primary_selection_device_manager_v1` | optional | no primary selection |
| `zwp_pointer_gestures_v1` | optional | no touchpad pinch/rotate (`PointerPanZoom*` events) |
| `xdg_activation_v1` | optional | focus requests ignored; the token of the launcher is dropped |
| `wp_presentation` | optional | frame timing from frame callbacks only |
| `xdg_toplevel_icon_manager_v1` | optional | window icon comes only from the `.desktop` file; `SetIcon` does nothing |
| `zxdg_exporter_v2` (`xdg_foreign`) | optional | portal dialogs not parented to our window |
| `xdg_dialog_v1` | optional | modal dialogs not marked as modal for the compositor |
| `xdg_wm_base` v6+ (`suspended` state, `wm_capabilities`, `configure_bounds`) | optional (lower versions work) | no suspended state (rendering stops only when frame callbacks stop); every title-bar button is shown; the first size isn't limited to the screen |
| `wl_surface` v6 (`preferred_buffer_scale`) | optional | the scale comes from the outputs the surface is on |
| `zxdg_output_manager_v1`, `wl_output` v4 | optional | logical monitor sizes computed from mode and integer scale, and no output names other than make and model |
| `zwp_tablet_manager_v2` | optional | no stylus input (`PointerDeviceKind.stylus`) |
| `zwp_idle_inhibit_manager_v1` | optional | `desktop.KeepScreenOn` reports `IdleInhibitUnavailable` |
| `wp_content_type_v1` | optional | no content-type hint (`SetContentType` does nothing) |

**Window decorations.** Glutter picks the first of these that works, per window:
1. **Server-side**, through `zxdg_decoration_manager_v1`: KDE, wlroots compositors (Sway, Hyprland), COSMIC.
2. **libdecor**, on compositors without server-side decorations, i.e. GNOME. Its GTK plugin draws a native Adwaita title bar, matching what Flutter's GTK-based Linux engine shows. libdecor draws in its own subsurfaces around our EGL surface and reports configure, close and commit through callbacks (§8.1 rule 2).
3. **Glutter's `WindowDecoration` widget** (Material defaults, replaceable by the app). It handles title, buttons limited by `wm_capabilities`, double-click to maximize, and right-click for the window menu (`xdg_toplevel.show_window_menu`). Edge resizing goes through `xdg_toplevel.resize` and dragging through `.move`. The shadow and resize borders sit inside the surface and are excluded with `xdg_surface.set_window_geometry`.

An app that wants its own title bar (tabs in the title bar, for example) sets `WindowConfig.Decorations = DecorationsApp`. That skips steps 1 and 2, and the app places `WindowDecoration` or its own widget.

**Focus** (decided in step 27.3). The focus of a view (`Handler.OnViewFocus`, `ViewFocusEvent`, the app lifecycle) is the `activated` state of its toplevel, which the compositor sends in `xdg_toplevel.configure` (through libdecor: `LIBDECOR_WINDOW_STATE_ACTIVE`; a libdecor configure that carries no state says nothing and keeps the last one). It is reported when the configure is applied: for the first time at the window's first configure (so a window that is not active gets an `Unfocused` at once), and then whenever it changes. A hidden window (`SetHidden`) and a closed one lose it. `wl_keyboard.enter` and `leave` no longer make view focus events; they route the keys, release the held keys and tell the input method, and are reported with `platform.KeyboardFocusHandler.OnKeyboardFocus`. A seat with no keyboard has focused windows all the same.

**Hiding a window** (`platform.Window.SetHidden`, which `RegularWindowController.SetHidden` reaches, §8.7). xdg-shell has no request to hide a toplevel, so the window unmaps itself and gives up its role: it attaches a null buffer and commits, then destroys what hangs on the toplevel (the `zxdg_toplevel_decoration_v1`, the `xdg_dialog_v1` and the `zxdg_exported_v2`, whose handle names the toplevel) and the `xdg_toplevel` and `xdg_surface`, or frees the libdecor frame that made them. The compositor then forgets the window and its task-bar entry. The `wl_surface` stays, with what hangs on it: the `wl_egl_window` or the Vulkan surface, the viewport, the fractional scale, the content type and the idle inhibitor (which the compositor lifts while the surface is not shown). The windows whose parent it is lose that parent (`set_parent(null)`). To show it, the window gets a new role on the same surface, made as at creation: title, app id, size limits, parent, modal flag, the maximized and fullscreen states it had (and those asked for while it was hidden), the decoration object with the mode it had, and the icon; then the initial commit without a buffer (libdecor: a new frame and `libdecor_frame_map`). Nothing is drawn until the configure that answers it; that configure resizes the native window for the size and scale of the moment, is acknowledged, and draws at once, as the first one does (§8.11). The window then asks for the focus with `xdg_activation_v1`, and its children get it back as their parent. While it is hidden the requests that need a toplevel (title-bar actions, the window menu, `activate`, portal handles) do nothing, and the title, the size, the size limits and the icon are kept for the next toplevel.

### 8.3 X11

Xlib (through purego, as Ebiten does) with EGL (`EGL_PLATFORM_X11_KHR`). The pieces:
- **XInput2** for smooth scrolling, touch and precise pointer input
- **libxkbcommon-x11** for keymaps, so key handling is identical to Wayland
- **IME over D-Bus**: IBus (`org.freedesktop.IBus`, as Gio does on X11) or Fcitx5's D-Bus input-method frontend, whichever is running. XIM isn't used, because its API is C-variadic (§8.1 rule 1)
- **ICCCM/EWMH**: `_NET_WM_NAME`, `_NET_WM_STATE`, `_NET_WM_PING`, `_NET_WM_ICON`, `_NET_WM_WINDOW_TYPE`, `_MOTIF_WM_HINTS`, `WM_CLASS` set to the app id
- **Selections**: `CLIPBOARD` and `PRIMARY`, with `INCR` for large transfers
- **XDND v5** for drag and drop
- **Xcursor** themes for cursors: the theme and size are the settings' (§8.8), else `XCURSOR_THEME`/`XCURSOR_SIZE`, else 24, given to libXcursor (`XcursorSetTheme`, `XcursorSetDefaultSize`) with the size × the scale of the window's monitor; a cursor is loaded once per size, and set again on a change of scale or settings
- **Xrandr** for monitors (`XRRGetMonitors`) and refresh rates; changes of the screen come as `RRScreenChangeNotify`
- **`Xft.dpi`** for scaling: the scale of every monitor is `Xft.dpi` (from `RESOURCE_MANAGER` of the root window, followed live) ÷ 96, in steps of a quarter, between 1 and 4. Without it a monitor of 144 dpi or more, by its size in millimetres, gets the scale that brings it to about 96 dpi, and any other monitor 1. A window uses the scale of the monitor under its centre and keeps its size in pixels when it moves to another monitor, so its logical size changes (`Gdk/WindowScalingFactor` of XSETTINGS counts as an `Xft.dpi` of 96 times the factor when there is none)

Frames are paced by `eglSwapInterval(0)` plus a timer at the monitor's refresh rate (XRandR's `XRRConfigCurrentRate`, 60 Hz when libXrandr is missing or says nothing): the first frame is drawn when the window is mapped and after each resize, other frames come one interval after the previous one, and requests in between are merged. (`eglSwapInterval(1)` would make the swap block on a window that is hidden, the same reason as on Wayland, §8.11.)

How the X11 backend does the rest:
- The window uses the X visual of the EGL config (`gles.X11Visual`), with a colormap of its own; `eglCreatePlatformWindowSurface` gets a pointer to the window ID.
- Keyboard, pointer and touch all come from XInput2 events of the master devices, and from nothing else: key events carry the modifier and group state, which goes straight into xkbcommon, so no XKB event handling is needed. The server's auto-repeat arrives as key events flagged `XIKeyRepeat` and becomes `KeyRepeatEvent` (SPEC §8.5 repeat timing is the server's). Without libXi or XInput2 on the server the backend has no keyboard and pointer input.
- Scrolling uses the scroll valuators of the device (the change in notches, 53 logical pixels each, as a scroll signal); the legacy buttons 4 to 7 count only on devices that have no scroll valuators. There is no pan/zoom gesture on X11, so a touchpad scrolls as scroll signals and pinch does not exist.
- Focus follows `XI_FocusIn`/`XI_FocusOut`; the held keys are released when the window loses it.
- **Window state** comes from the window manager and is followed live: `PropertyNotify` of `_NET_WM_STATE` (maximized needs both `_NET_WM_STATE_MAXIMIZED_VERT` and `_HORZ`; fullscreen), `WM_STATE` (iconic, or `_NET_WM_STATE_HIDDEN`, is `WindowInfo.Minimized`) and `_NET_WM_ALLOWED_ACTIONS` (maximize, fullscreen and minimize are the `WindowCapabilities`; a window manager that lists none allows all). The focus (`XI_FocusIn`/`XI_FocusOut`) is `Activated`. A change that the app is told of arrives as `OnMetrics` with the same size and the new `WindowInfo`. The requests go the other way as `_NET_WM_STATE` messages to the root window, `WM_CHANGE_STATE` (`XIconifyWindow`) and `_NET_ACTIVE_WINDOW`.
- **Title bar drawn by the app** (`WindowConfig.Decorations = DecorationsApp`, or `GLUTTER_DECORATIONS=app`): `_MOTIF_WM_HINTS` asks the window manager for no decorations (as for a window that is not decorated or is borderless), `WindowInfo.Decoration` is `DecorationApp`, and the bar's actions go to the window manager: `_NET_WM_MOVERESIZE` with the root position, the button and the direction (move, the four edges and four corners) of the pointer press that is going on, after `XIUngrabDevice` has ended the grab of that press, and `_GTK_SHOW_WINDOW_MENU` for the window menu. Without a button that is down they do nothing.
- **Focus** (decided in step 27.3): the focus events of the window (XI2 `FocusIn` and `FocusOut`) are the view focus (`Handler.OnViewFocus`), and keys follow it. A window that is mapped and has had no focus event yet is told at the map: it has the focus when `_NET_ACTIVE_WINDOW` of the root names it and has not when it names another window or none; without the property (no EWMH window manager) the first focus event tells. A withdrawn window loses the focus.
- **Hidden windows** (`SetHidden`, §8.7): the window is withdrawn as ICCCM 4.1.4 says, with `XWithdrawWindow` (`XUnmapWindow` and the synthetic `UnmapNotify` to the root), so it leaves the screen and the task bar; the window manager removes `WM_STATE` and `_NET_WM_STATE`. A withdrawn window is not minimized, whatever a manager leaves in those properties. Its frame timer stops and a frame asked for waits for the map; the requests that would map it again (iconify, restore, activate) do nothing until it is shown. Showing it sets `_NET_WM_STATE` to the maximized and fullscreen states it had (and the modal one of a dialog), maps it with `XMapWindow`, which draws its first frame as for a new window, and asks for the focus with `_NET_ACTIVE_WINDOW`.
- **Window icon**: `_NET_WM_ICON`, each picture as its width, height and ARGB pixels (not premultiplied); an empty icon removes the property, and the icon theme name has no X11 counterpart (the window manager finds the icon by `WM_CLASS` in the app's `.desktop` file).
- **Monitors** follow `RRScreenChangeNotify` and `RRNotify` (outputs and CRTCs: `RRScreenChangeNotifyMask`, `RRCrtcChangeNotifyMask`, `RROutputChangeNotifyMask`); the handler hears of it once, when the monitors or the scale are not what they were.
- An X IO error (the server went away) makes Xlib exit the process, as in every Xlib program; there is no connection-lost error like Wayland's.

### 8.4 Event loop and threading

- `RunApp` must be called from the main goroutine. `internal/platform` calls `runtime.LockOSThread()` in an `init` function, which keeps the main goroutine on the main OS thread. That thread is the **UI thread**.
- The loop waits with `poll(2)` on:
  - the Wayland/X11 connection
  - the D-Bus connection
  - an `eventfd` (wakeups from `Post`)
  - a `timerfd` (timers and the X11 frame pacer)
- On each wakeup it does the following, in order:
  1. Dispatch display events and translate them into `ui` events.
  2. Run posted functions.
  3. On a frame callback, run the frame: `OnBeginFrame` (animations), then `OnDrawFrame` (build, layout, paint, compositing, semantics), then `Scene`, then `Renderer.Render`, then swap.
- Frames are produced only when scheduled (`ScheduleFrame`). An idle app sleeps in `poll` with no timers armed.
- Rendering stays on the UI thread for 1.0. `ui.Scene` is immutable, so a separate raster thread can be added later without API changes.

### 8.5 Input

- **Pointer**: `wl_pointer` (axis value120 high-resolution scrolling, axis source, stop events → `PointerScrollEvent` and `PointerPanZoom*` for touchpads), `wl_touch`, XI2 equivalents. Device kinds map to Flutter's `PointerDeviceKind`.
  - Wayland pointer: the events between two `wl_pointer.frame` events become one batch for `Handler.OnPointer` (without `frame`, before `wl_pointer` version 5, each event is a batch). Enter is an add, motion a hover or, with a button down, a move; the first button down is a down, the last one up an up, and the buttons in between are moves with the new button state. Leaving with buttons down releases them (synthesized) before the remove. Linux buttons map to Flutter's bits (left 1, right 2, middle 4, side/back 8, extra/forward 16).
  - Scrolling: a wheel (or tilt, or no axis source) is a scroll signal, 53 logical pixels per notch (Flutter's Linux embedder), taken from `axis_value120` (or `axis_discrete`), else from the pixel value; a continuous source scrolls by its pixels. A touchpad (`axis_source` finger) is a pan/zoom gesture of the trackpad device: it starts with the first scroll event, its pan follows the fingers (the opposite of the axis values), and `axis_stop` ends it. Pinch and swipe (`zwp_pointer_gestures_v1`) are pan/zoom gestures with the compositor's translation, scale and rotation (degrees to radians).
  - Device ids: the mouse is 0, the trackpad 1, and a touch point is 1000 plus its touch id (Flutter tells pointers apart by device). A touch point is an add and a down at touch-down, moves with the primary button, and an up and a remove at touch-up; `wl_touch.cancel` cancels and removes every point.
  - Cursors: `wp_cursor_shape_v1` when the compositor has it, otherwise the XCursor theme through `libwayland-cursor` (`XCURSOR_THEME`, `XCURSOR_SIZE`; the first image of an animated cursor), with each cursor's names tried in order (CSS names first). `MouseCursorNone` hides the cursor. The cursor is set again each time the pointer enters a window.
- **Keyboard**:
  - The evdev scancode maps to `PhysicalKeyboardKey`, and the xkb keysym to `LogicalKeyboardKey`. Both tables are generated by `cmd/glutter-gen` from Flutter's key data.
  - The logical key follows Flutter's GTK embedder. A keysym in the generated table (Return, Shift_L, F1, ...) gives that key's id. Any other keysym with a Unicode character gives the lower-case character (plane 0). A keysym with neither (a dead key) gives the keysym in the GTK plane (`0x15_0000_0000 | keysym`), as does an unknown physical key with its XKB keycode. The keys of Flutter's layout goals (letters, digits, and a few punctuation keys) take the first Latin character that any layout of the keymap has on that key, at level 0, so shortcuts work on every layout and Shift or AltGr do not change the logical key; on a keymap with no Latin character there, the mandatory goals use the US key and the others keep the layout's own.
  - The character of a key-down event comes from its keysym, not from the modifiers (Control+a is "a"), and goes through xkbcommon's compose state, so a dead key gives no text and the next key gives the composed one. Key-up events carry no character and reuse the ids of the key-down.
  - Repeat rate and delay come from the compositor (Wayland) or XKB (X11), and repeats are synthesized as `KeyRepeatEvent`. Wayland: 25 keys per second after 600 ms until `repeat_info` arrives, a rate of 0 turns repeating off, and only the key pressed last repeats.
  - Dead keys and compose use xkbcommon's compose API when no IME is active.
- `HardwareKeyboard` state (pressed keys, lock modes) is kept consistent across focus changes. Keys are synthesized up/down on focus loss/gain, as Flutter's embedders do.

### 8.6 Text input (IME)

This is Flutter's text-input model (`TextInputConnection`, `TextEditingValue`, composing range) mapped to:
- **Wayland** `zwp_text_input_v3`: enable/disable, surrounding text, cursor rectangle, content type, preedit, commit, delete surrounding text
- **X11**, and **Wayland** on a compositor without `zwp_text_input_v3`: IBus or Fcitx5 over D-Bus (§8.3)

IBus and Fcitx5 work through both.

### 8.7 Windows (multiple windows)

- Every top-level window is a `ui.View` with its own EGL surface. All windows share one EGL context, which is made current per surface.
- **Kinds**: regular, dialog (parent and modal flags), and borderless/tooltip-style regular windows. Menus and popups render inside the window's `Overlay`, as in Flutter. Native `xdg_popup` is not used in 1.0.
- **Properties**: title, size, min/max size, resizable, decorated, fullscreen, maximized, minimized, hidden, icon, app id (Wayland `app_id` / X11 `WM_CLASS`). The app id must match the app's `.desktop` file for the tray and notifications to work.
- **Widget API**: `RunApp` shows the first window. Other windows are created with Flutter's experimental windowing API (`widgets/_window.dart`, as pinned in `reference/flutter`): `RegularWindowController` with the `RegularWindow` widget, and `DialogWindowController` with `DialogWindow`, shown by a `ViewCollection` (or a `ViewAnchor`), or by a `WindowManager` and its `WindowRegistry`. A controller makes the platform window when it is created and `Destroy` closes it. `WindowScope` gives a window's widgets its controller and state. The first window has a controller too (`WindowingOwner.ImplicitWindowController`), so a dialog can name it as its parent. Flutter's `PopupWindow`, `TooltipWindow` and `SatelliteWindow` (native popups placed by `WindowPositioner`) and the windows that size themselves to their content are deferred until after 1.0. Flutter's API is still experimental, so re-check it against the pinned `reference/flutter` when it is bumped and follow it.
- **Hiding**: `RegularWindowController.SetHidden(true)` takes a window off the screen and the task bar (no surface, no input, no focus) and `SetHidden(false)` puts it back with its size, title, icon and state; `IsHidden` is what the app asked for. Only the platform window goes: the view, the widget tree and its state stay, and a frame asked for while hidden is drawn when the window is shown (presented whole). `Activate` (and `SetMinimized(false)`) shows a hidden window first. It is how an app that lives in the system tray closes to it: its `OnExitRequested` hides the window and returns `AppExitResponseCancel`. How the backends do it is in §8.2 and §8.3; the web ignores it (§23.2). Dialogs have no `SetHidden`, and the dialogs of a hidden window stay as they are. Flutter has no counterpart (§12.10).
- **Closing**: closing the last window ends `RunApp`; the first window closes like the others when the app gave it a delegate (`WindowingOwner.SetImplicitWindowDelegate`) or destroys its controller, and the app goes on while other windows exist (§12.10). A hidden window is still open: `RunApp` goes on while one exists, and closing it, `ExitApplication` and `RequestAppExit` work as for a window on the screen. An app can intercept closing with `AppLifecycleListener.OnExitRequested`, mirroring Flutter's `AppExitResponse`.

### 8.8 System settings

These come from the xdg-desktop-portal Settings interface:
- `org.freedesktop.appearance` `color-scheme` → `PlatformBrightness`
- `accent-color`
- `contrast` → high contrast
- `org.gnome.desktop.interface`: `text-scaling-factor` → text scale, `enable-animations` → `DisableAnimations`, `cursor-size`, `cursor-theme`, and (1.2) `font-name` and `monospace-font-name` (a Pango description such as `Cantarell 11` or `Source Code Pro Semi-Bold 10`) → `PlatformConfiguration.UIFontFamily`/`UIFontSize` and `MonospaceFontFamily`/`MonospaceFontSize`; both are empty/zero when the portal does not give them

Changes are followed live through the `SettingChanged` signal. If the portal is missing, the fallbacks are XSETTINGS (X11), environment variables (`XCURSOR_SIZE`, `XCURSOR_THEME`), and then defaults. The locale comes from `LC_ALL`/`LC_MESSAGES`/`LANG`.

How the backends do it: the first `Platform.Settings()` call (the engine makes it before the first frame) connects to the session bus (only when `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS` or systemd's `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/bus` is there: a connection without either would start a bus with `dbus-launch`), reads the portal and follows `SettingChanged`; every change reaches the UI thread through `Post`, sets the cursor theme and size, and arrives at the engine as `Handler.OnSettings`, which updates the `PlatformDispatcher` (`MediaQuery`, `ThemeMode.system`, `AccessibilityFeatures`). The locale is read from the environment once (`de_DE.UTF-8@euro` is `de` `DE`; the first of the three variables that is set decides, and `C` and `POSIX` name none). On X11 without the portal the settings daemon is followed instead: the owner of the selection `_XSETTINGS_S<screen>` and its property `_XSETTINGS_SETTINGS` give a dark theme (`Net/ThemeName` containing "dark" or `Gtk/ApplicationPreferDarkTheme`), `Gtk/CursorThemeName` and `Gtk/CursorThemeSize`, `Gtk/EnableAnimations`, `Xft/Antialias`, `Xft/RGBA` and `Xft/HintStyle`, and `Gdk/WindowScalingFactor` is the monitors' scale when there is no `Xft.dpi`. A change of the property, or a new daemon (a `MANAGER` message on the root window), is applied at once.

### 8.9 D-Bus services (`internal/dbus`, exposed through `desktop`)

| Service | Protocol | When unavailable |
|---|---|---|
| **System tray** | `org.kde.StatusNotifierItem` registered with `org.kde.StatusNotifierWatcher`; menu through `com.canonical.dbusmenu`; icon as ARGB32 pixmaps or a theme icon name; activate, secondary activate, scroll, tooltip, status (active/passive/needs attention) | `TrayUnavailable`. GNOME needs the AppIndicator extension; this must be documented |
| **Notifications** | `org.freedesktop.Notifications` (summary, body, icon, actions, urgency, replaces-id, timeout, `ActionInvoked`/`NotificationClosed`); inside Flatpak, `org.freedesktop.portal.Notification` | `NotificationsUnavailable` |
| **File dialogs** | `org.freedesktop.portal.FileChooser` (`OpenFile` with multiple selection, directory mode and filters; `SaveFile`; `SaveFiles`; current folder and name), parented by `xdg_foreign` handle or X11 window id | `FileChooserUnavailable`. There is no GTK fallback. Cancelling is a result, not an error |
| **Open URI** | `org.freedesktop.portal.OpenURI` | fall back to `xdg-open`, else `OpenURIUnavailable` |
| **Settings** | `org.freedesktop.portal.Settings` | §8.8 fallbacks |
| **Accessibility** | AT-SPI2 on the accessibility bus (§8.10) | logged at debug level; the app keeps working |

Calls into these services happen on a background goroutine with a `context.Context`. Results and signals are delivered to the UI thread through `Post`.

How the calls of package `desktop` work (step 15.1; the same for the file dialogs, the notifications and the tray):
- A call that waits for the desktop (`OpenURI`, `OpenFiles`, ...) takes `ctx` and a `BuildContext` and **must not be made on the UI thread**: it fails with `DesktopCallOnUIThread` there. It posts one function to the UI thread, which reads the `View` above the context and asks the platform for what a portal needs: the *parent window handle* (`PlatformDispatcher.PortalParentHandle`: `wayland:<handle>` from `zxdg_exporter_v2.export_toplevel`, exported once per window and kept until it closes, or `x11:<window id in hex>`; empty when the compositor cannot export) and an *activation token* (`ActivationToken`, Wayland's `xdg_activation_v1`) for the app that is started. Everything else happens on the calling goroutine. A context without a `WidgetsBinding` fails with `DesktopWithoutBinding`; a context that ends while the UI thread has not answered with `DesktopCallCancelled`.
- The state of the services (the connection to the session bus, made on the first call and kept) lives in the `WidgetsBinding` (`Extension`), not in a package variable.
- The file dialogs (`OpenFiles`, `SaveFile`, `SaveFiles`) call `org.freedesktop.portal.FileChooser.OpenFile`, `SaveFile` and `SaveFiles(parent_window, title, options)` the same way. The options are `accept_label`, `modal` (false for `NotModal`), `multiple`, `directory`, `filters` (`a(sa(us))`: the name, then the globs as kind 0 and the MIME types as kind 1; the first is also `current_filter`; none in directory mode), `current_folder`, `current_file` and `current_name`, and for `SaveFiles` `files` (`aay`); paths are byte strings with a closing NUL. The answer's `uris` become file system paths (percent-decoding the `file:` URIs) and the chosen filter's name is `FilesResult.Filter`. The user closing the dialog is `FilesResult.Cancelled`, not an error; a portal that cannot be reached (no session bus, no service, no such method or object, no reply) is `FileChooserUnavailable` (metadata `method`), with no fallback; an answer that says the interaction ended badly is `FileChooserFailed`, and one without a list of `file:` URIs `FileChooserReplyInvalid`. `SaveFiles` needs names that are file names (`SaveFilesWithoutNames`, `SaveFilesNameInvalid`). Closing the context closes the portal's request (`PortalRequestAborted`).
- The notifications (`ShowNotification`, `CloseNotification`) call `org.freedesktop.Notifications.Notify(app_name, replaces_id, app_icon, summary, body, actions, hints, expire_timeout)`: `app_name` is `NotificationOpts.AppName`, the app id, or "Glutter"; `actions` is the flat list of ID and label pairs; the hints are `urgency` (a byte: 0 low, 1 normal, 2 critical), `desktop-entry` (the app id, so the desktop finds the `.desktop` file and its icon) and `category`; the timeout is -1 (the server's), 0 for `Persistent`, or the milliseconds. The returned number is the `NotificationID`; `ReplacesID` replaces a notification that is shown. A server that has the `body-markup` capability (`GetCapabilities`, asked once) reads the body as markup, so `&`, `<` and `>` of a plain body are escaped unless `BodyMarkup` is set. The signals `ActionInvoked(id, action_key)` and `NotificationClosed(id, reason)` are followed from the first notification on, with a subscription that lives as long as the app; they reach `OnAction` and `OnClosed` of the notification, posted to the UI thread (a panic in the callback is `CallbackPanicked`); the callbacks of a notification are dropped when it closes. The action `"default"` is the one of a click on the notification. Inside Flatpak (`/.flatpak-info`) the calls are those of `org.freedesktop.portal.Notification`: `AddNotification(id, {title, body, icon, priority, default-action, buttons})` with a name of the app's own for each notification (kept for replacing and for `RemoveNotification`), and the signal `ActionInvoked(id, action, parameter)`; the portal has no close signal, so `OnClosed` is not called there. Without a session bus or a service, or without the portal in the sandbox, the call fails with `NotificationsUnavailable`; a summary that is empty is `NotificationSummaryEmpty`, an action without an ID or label `NotificationActionInvalid`, and a reply of `Notify` that is not a number `NotificationReplyInvalid`.
- The system tray (`SystemTray`, made with `NewSystemTray(icon, child, opts)`) is a widget that builds its child and keeps one icon in the tray while it is in the tree; its state is made when the widget first gets its dependencies and ends with the state's lifetime. A worker goroutine does the bus work: it exports `org.kde.StatusNotifierItem` on `/StatusNotifierItem` (methods `Activate`, `SecondaryActivate`, `ContextMenu`, `Scroll`; properties `Category`, `Id`, `Title`, `Status`, `WindowId`, `IconThemePath`, `Menu`, `ItemIsMenu`, `IconName`, `IconPixmap`, `OverlayIcon*`, `AttentionIcon*`, `AttentionMovieName`, `ToolTip`) and `com.canonical.dbusmenu` on `/MenuBar` (`GetLayout`, `GetGroupProperties`, `GetProperty`, `Event`, `EventGroup`, `AboutToShow`, `AboutToShowGroup`; properties `Version` 3, `TextDirection`, `Status`, `IconThemePath`; signal `LayoutUpdated`), both with `org.freedesktop.DBus.Properties` (`internal/dbus.Properties`: `PropertiesChanged` when a value changes), takes the name `org.kde.StatusNotifierItem-<pid>-<n>`, and calls `org.kde.StatusNotifierWatcher.RegisterStatusNotifierItem(name)`. It registers again when the watcher gets a new owner (`NameOwnerChanged`, the panel restarted) and takes everything off the bus when the state ends. The icon is a `ui.WindowIcon`: a theme name and/or pictures, sent as ARGB32 pixmaps in network byte order (alpha, red, green, blue, not premultiplied); `TrayIconFromRGBA(size, rgba)` scales one picture to 16, 22, 24, 32 and 48 pixels. A rebuild gives the tray a new model; what differs from the old one is told (`NewTitle`, `NewIcon`, `NewAttentionIcon`, `NewToolTip`, `NewStatus`, `LayoutUpdated` with a revision that grows when the menu's items or properties change) and nothing is sent when only the functions changed. The menu items are numbered depth first from 1 (the root is 0) on each build, and a click (`Event` with `clicked`) calls the item's function posted to the UI thread (`CallbackPanicked` for a panic); labels have `_` doubled, since the protocol takes a single one as a mnemonic mark. `ItemIsMenu` is true when there is a menu and no `OnActivate`, so that a click opens the menu. A desktop without the watcher is `TrayUnavailable` (also without a session bus); a watcher that refuses is `TrayRegisterFailed`; a name that is taken is `DBusNameTaken`; the error is reported once through the binding's error reporter and the widget is its child. An app has one tray at a time: a second widget reports `TrayDuplicate`. GNOME shows the icon only with the AppIndicator extension; the documentation of `SystemTray` says so.
- `OpenURI` calls `org.freedesktop.portal.OpenURI.OpenURI(parent_window, uri, {handle_token, activation_token})` through `dbus.PortalRequest` and waits for the `Response`: success and the user's cancel are both `nil`. When the portal cannot do it (no bus, no portal, a failed call, a response that says it ended badly) it runs `xdg-open <uri>` with `XDG_ACTIVATION_TOKEN` in its environment, and when that fails too the error is `OpenURIUnavailable` (metadata `uri`, `portalTag`, `portal`). A URI without a scheme, or with a control character, fails with `OpenURIInvalid` before anything is called.

Desktop API sketch (Version 2 style):

```go
// declarative: the tray lives in the widget tree and updates when it rebuilds
desktop.NewSystemTray(icon, child, desktop.SystemTrayOpts{
	Tooltip:    "Glutter",
	OnActivate: s.showWindow,
	Menu: []desktop.TrayMenuEntry{
		desktop.NewTrayMenuItem("Open", s.showWindow),
		desktop.NewTrayMenuSeparator(),
		desktop.NewTrayMenuItem("Quit", s.quit),
	},
})

// imperative: call from a goroutine, never block the UI thread
files, err := desktop.OpenFiles(ctx, bc, desktop.OpenFilesOpts{
	Title: "Open image", Filters: []desktop.FileFilter{{Name: "Images", Patterns: []string{"*.png", "*.jpg"}}},
})
id, err := desktop.ShowNotification(ctx, bc, "Download finished", desktop.NotificationOpts{Body: name,
	Actions: []desktop.NotificationAction{{ID: "open", Label: "Open"}}, OnAction: func(id string) { /* on the UI thread */ }})
```

### 8.10 Accessibility (AT-SPI2)

The framework's semantics tree (`ui.SemanticsUpdate`) is mirrored as AT-SPI2 objects on the accessibility bus (the address comes from `org.a11y.Bus.GetAddress`). Each object implements the matching interfaces: `Accessible`, `Component`, `Action`, `Text`, `EditableText`, `Value`, `Selection`. Roles and states are mapped from `SemanticsFlag` and `SemanticsRole`. Events are sent for focus, state changes, text changes, caret moves, child changes and visible data changes. The bridge is enabled only while a screen reader is active (`org.a11y.Status.ScreenReaderEnabled`), so the semantics tree costs nothing otherwise. This mirrors Flutter's `SemanticsBinding.semanticsEnabled`. It is tested by hand with Orca and automatically against a fake bus.

How the bridge works (`internal/dbus/atspi`, steps 16.1 and 16.2):
- The engine starts it through `platform.Services.Accessibility()` (the Linux backends share `linux.Services`). It opens the session bus only when one is there (it never starts one with `dbus-launch`), reads `org.a11y.Status.ScreenReaderEnabled` of `org.a11y.Bus` (`/org/a11y/bus`) and follows its `PropertiesChanged`. A desktop without the service leaves it off, with a debug message and no error.
- When the flag turns on, the bridge calls `org.a11y.Bus.GetAddress`, connects to the accessibility bus at that address, serves the application root (`/org/a11y/atspi/accessible/root`: `Accessible`, `Application`) and the objects of the nodes it has, calls `org.a11y.atspi.Socket.Embed((so))` on the registry (its answer is the parent of the application), and only then tells the engine (`PlatformDispatcher.SetSemanticsEnabled(true)`), so the first tree finds the bridge ready. When the flag turns off it tells the engine first, then unexports everything and closes the connection.
- The model (`Model`) mirrors one tree for each view from `ui.SemanticsUpdate` (a node that no longer is reachable from node 0 is removed; the root of a view is a `FRAME` named by the window's title, and the children of the application are the frames in the order of the view IDs). Every node is `/org/a11y/atspi/accessible/<view>_<id>` with `Accessible`, `Component` and `Action` (and `org.freedesktop.DBus.Properties`, answered from the model, so a value is never stale).
- Roles: the `SemanticsRole` when there is one (the table follows what browsers do for the ARIA role of the same name), else the flags: text field `ENTRY` (`PASSWORD_TEXT` when obscured), slider, link, header `HEADING` (with the attribute `level`), image, buttons (`CHECK_BOX`, `RADIO_BUTTON` and `TOGGLE_BUTTON` when the node is checkable, in an exclusive group, or toggled), what has text `LABEL`, the rest `PANEL`. States (`GetState` has two 32-bit words): `ENABLED` and `SENSITIVE` unless disabled, `SHOWING` and `VISIBLE` unless hidden, `FOCUSABLE` and `FOCUSED`, `CHECKABLE`, `CHECKED`, `INDETERMINATE`, `SELECTABLE`, `SELECTED`, `EXPANDABLE`, `EXPANDED`, `COLLAPSED`, `REQUIRED`, `SINGLE_LINE`, `MULTI_LINE`, `EDITABLE`, `READ_ONLY`, `MODAL`, `INVALID_ENTRY`, `HAS_TOOLTIP`, and `ACTIVE` for the frame of the window that has the focus; the full tables are in `roles.go`.
- `Action` lists the actions of the node (`click` for tap, `longpress`, `increase`, `decrease`, `dismiss`, `expand`, `collapse`, `copy`, `cut`, `paste`, the four scrolls, then the custom actions by their labels); `DoAction(i)` posts a `SemanticsActionEvent` to the UI thread (`PlatformDispatcher.DispatchSemanticsActionEvent`, custom actions with their ID as the argument). `Component` gives the extents in the pixels of the window (the transforms of the node and its ancestors; Wayland gives no position of the window on the screen, so the screen coordinates are the window's), hit testing, `GrabFocus` (the focus action) and `ScrollTo` (show on screen).
- Events (signature `siiva{sv}` on `org.a11y.atspi.Event.Object`): `StateChanged` (focused, checked, selected, expanded, enabled, ...), `ChildrenChanged` (`add` and `remove` with the index and the reference of the child), `PropertyChange` (`accessible-name`, `accessible-description`), and on `Event.Window` `Activate` and `Deactivate` with `StateChanged:active`. The cache interface (`org.a11y.atspi.Cache`) is not served: clients ask the objects.
Step 16.2 adds the rest of the interfaces and the events of the content:
- `Text` (text fields): `GetText`, `GetCharacterAtOffset`, `GetStringAtOffset` and the three `GetText*Offset` of the boundary types, the caret (`CaretOffset`, `SetCaretOffset`), the selection (`GetNSelections`, `GetSelection`, `AddSelection`, `RemoveSelection`, `SetSelection`), the attributes (none), the extents (those of the field). The offsets are characters, as AT-SPI2 counts them; the semantics tree counts the bytes of the UTF-8 text, and the bridge converts both ways (`runeOffset`, `byteOffset`). A word is the characters that are not spaces and the spaces after them (GTK's), a sentence ends at `.`, `!` or `?` with its spaces or at a new line, and a line and a paragraph end at a new line; the text is not laid out for the bridge, so a long line is one line and `GetOffsetAtPoint` has no answer. What the screen reader asks for goes to the framework as `SetSelection` (the argument is `map[string]int{"base", "extent"}`, in bytes).
- `EditableText` (the text fields that are not read-only): `SetTextContents`, `InsertText`, `DeleteText` (the new text is worked out here and sent as `SetText`), and `CopyText`, `CutText` and `PasteText` (a `SetSelection`, then the action). The framework gives a field the actions to set its text and its selection only while it has the focus, so the interface does not wait for them: the methods say false until the field has them, and a screen reader moves the focus first with `Component.GrabFocus`.
- `Value` (sliders, spin buttons and progress bars): the properties `MinimumValue`, `MaximumValue`, `MinimumIncrement`, `CurrentValue` and `Text`; the value is read from the text that the framework says ("50%" is 50 between 0 and 100; another number goes between `MinValue` and `MaxValue` of the node, or 0 and 100). Setting `CurrentValue` asks the framework to increase or decrease it (a step at a time, as the actions do) and fails when the node has no such action.
- `Selection` (a node whose children have the flag `IsSelected`): `NSelectedChildren`, `GetSelectedChild`, `IsChildSelected`, `SelectChild` and `DeselectChild` (a tap on the child), `DeselectSelectedChild`; `SelectAll` and `ClearSelection` are not supported. A node gets or loses the interface when its children's flags make it so.
- Events: `TextChanged` (`insert` or `delete` with the character offset, the length and the text; a replacement is a delete and an insert), `TextCaretMoved`, `TextSelectionChanged`, `PropertyChange:accessible-value`, `VisibleDataChanged` (when the position or the size of an updated node changes), `SelectionChanged` (on the container), with the `ChildrenChanged` and `StateChanged` of 16.1. `Properties.Get` answers `CharacterCount` and `CaretOffset` (Text), the value properties, and `NSelectedChildren`.

### 8.11 Wayland quality requirements

Wayland is the primary backend, and these behaviors are requirements, not nice-to-haves. Each one gets a `glutter_display` test or an entry in the manual checklist (§17).

**Frame pacing**
- `eglSwapInterval(0)`. Frames are paced only by `wl_surface.frame` callbacks, so `eglSwapBuffers` never blocks, even when the window is hidden or occluded.
- When the toplevel is `suspended`, or no frame callback arrives, nothing is rendered. Tickers are resumed with the next frame's timestamp, so animations jump to the right point instead of replaying.
- A window that the app hid (§8.2) renders nothing either: the frame callback in flight is forgotten (a late one is ignored), and no buffer is attached between showing it and the configure of its new role. The engine asks a hidden window for no frame; the one that was wanted is asked for when the window is shown.
- At most one frame is in flight per window. Input arriving between frames is batched into the next one.
- With Vulkan (§9.4) the same holds: the present mode never blocks (MAILBOX, else IMMEDIATE, and FIFO only when the driver has neither), and frames are still paced by `wl_surface.frame`. Presenting is the commit, so on `configure` the backend sends `ack_configure`, the renderer recreates the swapchain at the new size, and only then presents.

**Resizing and scaling**
- On `configure`: lay out and render at the new size and commit it in the same iteration, without waiting for a frame callback. Interactive resizing tracks the pointer. With EGL the buffer swap is the commit, so the backend resizes the `wl_egl_window`, sends `ack_configure`, and then renders and swaps; the first commit after the ack therefore already has the new size.
- While resizing, subsurfaces (libdecor) are in synchronized mode, so the decoration and the content never get out of step.
- Fractional scale: the buffer is `ceil(logical size × scale)`, and `wp_viewport` maps it back to the logical size. Text and shapes are rasterized at that buffer resolution, so they stay sharp at 125%/150%/175%.
- Moving a window between monitors with different scales re-renders at the new scale in the next frame, with no blurry frame in between. The window's `ViewMetrics.Display` is the output of the entered ones with the largest scale (the one that sets the window's scale); a change of it is reported at once.
- Outputs: every `wl_output` global is bound (up to v4) when it appears, including those that are plugged in later, and forgotten on `wl_registry.global_remove`; `zxdg_output_manager_v1` adds the logical position and size. `Displays()` lists them in the order announced: the bounds are in logical pixels (the logical size of `zxdg_output_v1`, else the mode divided by the integer scale, the mode's width and height swapped for a transform of 90 or 270 degrees), the pixel ratio is the integer scale or, with a logical size, mode width ÷ logical width (so a 150% output says 1.5), the refresh rate is the mode's in Hz (0 when the compositor does not say; the engine then uses 60), and the primary one is the one at the origin of the logical space, else the first. A connected, disconnected or changed output tells the handler (`OnDisplaysChanged`), and the views get the new display list and their metrics again.

**Input**
- Keyboard: the keymap is read from the compositor's fd per seat; repeat follows `wl_keyboard.repeat_info`; modifiers come from the `modifiers` event; pressed keys are released when focus leaves (§8.5).
- IME: `zwp_text_input_v3` (GNOME, KDE, wlroots). If the compositor lacks it, Glutter falls back to IBus/Fcitx5 over D-Bus, as on X11.
- Positions: the seat gives surface-local positions in logical pixels; the pointer, the touch points and the tablet tools convert them to the physical pixels that `ui.PointerData` has with the window's scale (`scale/120`, a fractional scale included), set when the pointer enters or a finger touches down and when the scale changes.
- Tablets: with `zwp_tablet_manager_v2` the tablet seat announces tools; each tool is a pointer device of its own (`input.StylusDeviceBase` + its number). A pen, brush, pencil or airbrush is `PointerDeviceKind.stylus`, an eraser `invertedStylus`, a finger `touch`, a mouse or lens `mouse`. The events of a tool between two `frame`s become one `PointerData`: `Add` when it comes near (`proximity_in`), `Hover` while it moves there, `Down`, `Move` and `Up` while it touches (the tip is the primary button; the barrel buttons `BTN_STYLUS` and `BTN_STYLUS2` are the secondary and middle buttons, also while hovering), `Remove` on `proximity_out` (a tool that leaves while touching is lifted first, and one over a window that closes is cancelled). `pressure` and `distance` are 0 to 1 (`PressureMax` and `DistanceMax` 1), `tilt` is the angle from the normal of the surface in radians (0 to pi/2) with the azimuth as `orientation`, else the rotation of the tool is the `orientation`. The cursor of a tool is the window's, set by shape with `wp_cursor_shape_v1` (without it the compositor keeps its own), and hidden for `MouseCursor.None`. The wheel, slider and pad events are not used.
- Pointer: frame-grouped events (`wl_pointer.frame`), `axis_value120` for high-resolution wheels, `axis_source` + `axis_stop` for kinetic scrolling, and touchpad pinch/rotate through `zwp_pointer_gestures_v1`.
- Cursors: `wp_cursor_shape_v1` when available (the compositor draws the shape with its own theme, size and scale). Otherwise the XCursor theme and size from the settings (the portal, §8.8), else `XCURSOR_THEME`/`XCURSOR_SIZE`, else 24, loaded through `libwayland-cursor` for each integer scale in use at the size × scale (a fractional scale uses the scale above it); the cursor surface gets that buffer scale, and the hotspot is divided by it. A change of the window's scale or of the settings sets the cursor again.

**Data exchange**
- Clipboard, primary selection and drag and drop read and write through pipes on a goroutine, never on the UI thread.
- MIME types offered: `text/plain;charset=utf-8`, `text/plain`, `UTF8_STRING`, `text/uri-list`, `image/png`, plus app-defined types.
- Drag and drop supports copy, move and ask actions and a drag icon surface.
- **Drag and drop, how it works.** The widget API is Flutter's (`Draggable`, `LongPressDraggable`, `DragTarget`, `widgets/drag_target.dart`) and works inside a window. The platform side adds two things (Glutter's own; Flutter has no drag and drop between applications):
  - *Targets.* The windows take the drags of other clients (and of the app itself): Wayland `wl_data_device` `enter`/`motion`/`leave`/`drop` with `wl_data_offer.accept` and `set_actions`, X11 XDND version 5 (`XdndAware`, `XdndEnter`, `XdndPosition`, `XdndStatus`, `XdndLeave`, `XdndDrop`, `XdndFinished`).
