The platform gives the framework a `ui.DropEvent` (phase, position, offered types and actions) and the framework answers with the action it accepts at that place; a drop carries the data, which the platform read first: the text (`text/plain;charset=utf-8`, `UTF8_STRING`, `text/plain`) and the list of URIs (`text/uri-list`), at most 5 s each. The binding hit tests the position like a `_DragAvatar` and tells the `DragTarget`s, which receive a `*ExternalDragData` (the MIME types while the drag is over them; `Text`, `URIs` and `Files()` after the drop). A `DragTarget[T]` sees the drag when a `*ExternalDragData` is a `T`, as `any` is.
  - *Sources.* A `Draggable` with `Export` starts a drag of the platform instead of one in the window: Wayland `wl_data_device.start_drag` with a `wl_data_source` and a small icon surface (the button's serial; the compositor draws the icon), X11 an owner of `XdndSelection` that follows the pointer from the XInput2 events and sends the XDND messages (no icon; the cursor shows the action). The data goes out as `text/plain` (and the other text types) and `text/uri-list`. Besides, the source offers the type `application/x-glutter-drag-<pid>-<id>`: a drag of this app that comes back to one of its windows is recognised by it, and its `DragTarget`s receive the `Draggable`'s own data (of type `T`) instead of an `ExternalDragData`. The end of the drag (`Draggable.OnDragEnd`, `OnDragCompleted`, `OnDraggableCanceled`) is what the platform reports: accepted if the target chose an action.
  - Without platform support (no `wl_data_device_manager`, a platform without drag and drop, or a pointer that is not down) an `Export` is ignored and the drag stays in the window.

**Lifecycle and correctness**
- `XDG_ACTIVATION_TOKEN` from the environment is consumed at startup, so launchers' startup feedback ends: the platform reads it and removes it from the environment (so that apps it starts do not inherit it), and uses it once, with `xdg_activation_v1.activate`, for the first window when its first configure is acknowledged. Activation requests (e.g. clicking a notification, or `activate()` on a window controller) use `xdg_activation_v1` too: `get_activation_token` with the serial of the last input event of the seat, the app id and the surface, then `activate` with the token that `done` gives. `Platform.RequestActivationToken(view, done)` (the optional interface `platform.ActivationTokenProvider`) gives a token for an app that this app starts (`XDG_ACTIVATION_TOKEN`), which the services of M15 use. The compositor decides whether the window gets the focus.
- `desktop.KeepScreenOn(bc, on)` creates (or destroys) a `zwp_idle_inhibitor_v1` for the surface of the window of `bc`; the compositor lifts it while the surface is hidden, and it goes away with the window. Without `zwp_idle_inhibit_manager_v1` it fails with `IdleInhibitUnavailable` (so does the X11 backend, for now).
- `WindowConfig.ContentType` (`ui.ContentType`: photo, video or game) is sent as `wp_content_type_v1.set_content_type` on the surface before the first commit, and `RegularWindowController.SetContentType` changes it. It is a hint; without the protocol it is ignored.
- `WindowConfig.Icon` (`ui.WindowIcon`: an icon theme name and square RGBA pictures) is set with `xdg_toplevel_icon_v1`: `set_name`, one `add_buffer` per picture (`wl_shm` ARGB8888, premultiplied; the scale of a picture is its `Scale`), then `set_icon` on the toplevel (the one libdecor made when it decorates the window), before the first commit; `SetIcon` changes it and an empty icon removes it. The pictures are kept until the next icon. Without the protocol the icon is the `.desktop` file's, and a picture that is not square or has the wrong number of bytes fails with `WindowIconInvalid`.
- A protocol error from the compositor ends `RunApp` with `WaylandProtocolError`, carrying the interface, object id and code (`wl_display_get_protocol_error`).
- The app id is set before the first commit, so the compositor matches the `.desktop` file (icon, name, tray, notifications).

**GPU drivers**
- Mesa and NVIDIA (through `libnvidia-egl-wayland`, which the NVIDIA driver installs) both work through `EGL_KHR_platform_wayland`. Explicit sync and buffer management are handled inside the EGL driver, so Glutter does nothing driver-specific.

---

## 9. Renderer

### 9.1 GLES backend (`internal/gpu/gles`)

Since 1.1 it is the fallback renderer on Linux when Vulkan does not work (§9.6), and on the web it runs on WebGL 2: the same shaders, with the GL functions bound through `syscall/js` instead of purego (step 21.4). Its technique table below is also the contract of the explicit renderer (§9.3).

- **Context**: EGL 1.4+, an OpenGL ES 3.0 context (`EGL_OPENGL_ES3_BIT`), one context shared by all windows. The libraries are opened with `purego.Dlopen`, and GL function pointers are resolved through `eglGetProcAddress` and bound with `purego.RegisterFunc`.
- **Pipeline**: the `ui.Scene` layer tree is flattened into draw lists. Commands are then batched by pipeline, texture and blend mode, and issued as instanced or batched draws.
- **Color**: premultiplied alpha, with blending in non-linear sRGB to match Flutter's output.
- **Pixel position**: a fragment shader gets the position of its pixel from a varying that the vertex shader sets to the device position of the quad (rounded to the pixel center in `devicePixel()`), never from `gl_FragCoord`. With Mesa's llvmpipe, `gl_FragCoord.y` was upside down in a draw into a layer's framebuffer made while the window's framebuffer was the parent, so every layer drawn in the window path came out wrong (cached layers empty, blurs off); `window_display_test.go` draws into the window's framebuffer for that reason.

| Primitive | Technique |
|---|---|
| Rect, RRect, RSuperellipse, oval, circle, line, DRRect | **Analytic antialiasing**: a signed distance field in the fragment shader, with no multisampling |
| BoxShadow / `drawShadow` on rects and rrects | Analytic Gaussian-blurred rrect in closed form, with no offscreen pass. That is the shape of every `BoxShadow` (`MaskFilter.blur` on a rect, rrect, oval or circle under a transform that scales and moves). `Canvas.drawShadow` and every other shape with a mask filter use the blurred layer below (see §12.10) |
| General paths | Flattened on the CPU and rasterized into a **coverage atlas** (8-bit, cached by path + transform), using the same rasterizer as the software backend; then composited on the GPU. Paths larger than an atlas tile use a tiled mask. A GPU tessellation path may be added later if profiling shows a need |
| Text | Glyph outlines from go-text as paths with their origin on a quarter of a pixel horizontally and a pixel vertically (when the transform only scales and moves), so each glyph at a size has four coverage tiles in the path coverage atlas (§12.10). Color glyphs: COLR v0 and v1, CBDT and sbix bitmaps as pictures; SVG is not drawn |
| Images | Textures with mipmaps for downscaling (`FilterQuality`). Nine-patch and atlas draws are batched |
| Gradients | Linear, radial, two-point conical and sweep in the shader; all tile modes |
| Clips | Rects through the scissor test. RRects and paths through a stencil buffer, or antialiased through coverage masks (Flutter clips are antialiased by default) |
| Layers | `SaveLayer`, opacity, color/image filters, backdrop filter and shader mask render into pooled offscreen framebuffers |
| Blur | Separable Gaussian with downsampling for large sigma |
| Blend modes | `srcOver`, `dstOver`, `plus` and `dstOut` through `glBlendFunc`; every other mode in a shader that reads a copy of the destination (`KHR_blend_equation_advanced` is not used, see §12.10) |
| Raster cache | A `RepaintBoundary` subtree that stays unchanged for 3 frames is cached as a texture, as Flutter does |
| Damage | A frame is drawn whole, and `eglSwapBuffersWithDamageKHR` (or the EXT form) tells the compositor which part changed from the last frame, when the display has the extension; otherwise it is `eglSwapBuffers`. `GLUTTER_DAMAGE=0` and `Config.DisableDamage` turn it off, `GLUTTER_RASTER_CACHE=0` and `Config.DisableRasterCache` turn the raster cache off |
| State changes | `Device` remembers the GL state it set (viewport, scissor, blend, color mask, clear color, stencil, program, texture units, framebuffer, vertex array, buffer), and each program remembers the value of its uniforms, so a repeated value is not sent to GL; `Device.Stats` counts what was skipped |

**Errors** (returned from `RunApp`): `EGLLoadFailed`, `EGLInitFailed`, `EGLNoConfig`, `GLES3Unsupported`, `ShaderCompileFailed` (an internal bug, reported with the log).

On the web the errors are `WebGL2Unavailable` (the canvas gave no context), `WebGLCanvasNotFound`, `WebGLSurfaceKindUnsupported` and `WebGLContextLost` (a read-back or a snapshot while the context is lost), besides `GLES3Unsupported` and `ShaderCompileFailed`.

**Decided in step 21.4** (WebGL 2 through `gles`):
- **Two bindings, one interface.** `glapi.go`'s `glAPI` is the interface the renderer draws through. `gl_purego.go` (Linux, the former `gl.go`) binds it over purego; `gl_js.go` (`js && wasm`) binds it over `syscall/js`. `TestBindingsHaveTheSameFunctions` reads both files and `glapi.go` as source (each carries a build tag) and fails when a binding lacks a function of the interface. The renderer, the device, the pools and the shaders (GLSL ES 3.00, `#version 300 es`, which WebGL 2 accepts as they are) are the same on both. `GL_VERSION` of WebGL 2 ("WebGL 2.0 (OpenGL ES 3.0 ...)") passes the ES 3.0 check as `isES3` accepts it.
- **Few, cheap calls into JavaScript** (§22). Every method of the context is bound once (`fn.bind(gl)`) and called with `Invoke`, which skips the lookup by name of `Call`. An upload goes through one `Uint8Array` (with a `Float32Array` on the same buffer) that is reused and only grows: one `js.CopyBytesToJS` and one call, using the WebGL 2 forms that take a source offset and a length. A uniform of one value uses the scalar function and needs no array. A read-back is one call and one `js.CopyBytesToGo`. WebGL's objects are JavaScript values, so the binding keeps each in a table (`handleTable`, plain Go and tested) and the renderer's `uint32` is the index; 0 is null, and a uniform location is the index minus one (-1 is none). Measuring and the remedy of §22 (encoding a frame's calls into one buffer that a script function runs) are step 21.7's.
- **EGL imitated, not replaced.** The renderer's context code (`context.go`) opens a display, a context and a surface through `eglAPI`. On the web `webEGL` (`context_js.go`) is that interface over a canvas, where every call that has nothing to do succeeds: the real work is in `openWeb`, which finds the canvas by `data-glutter-view`, calls `getContext("webgl2", ...)` and reports the failures with the web's tags before any EGL call is made. There is no swap (the browser presents when the task ends) and no damage (`CanSwapWithDamage` is false, so a frame is drawn whole), no BGRA and no `readPixels` format other than RGBA/`UNSIGNED_BYTE` (the renderer uses nothing else), and the canvas has a size of its own: `Context.ResizeSurface` sets it before a frame is drawn (setting it clears the canvas, and the frame covers all of it). One canvas has one context, so a second surface of the renderer (a second window) is `EGLSurfaceFailed`; the web has one window.
- **Context attributes** (`webContextAttributes`): `alpha` and `premultipliedAlpha` true (the premultiplied RGBA8 surface that EGL gives on Linux, so that blends and read-backs are the tested ones), `antialias` false, `depth` and `stencil` false (the window's framebuffer needs neither: clips are coverage masks and layers have their own framebuffers with a stencil renderbuffer), `preserveDrawingBuffer` false, `failIfMajorPerformanceCaveat` false (a software WebGL still beats `soft` in WebAssembly) and `powerPreference` default (a UI does not wake a discrete GPU).
- **Loss and restoration.** `webglcontextlost` calls `preventDefault` (without it the browser never restores) and records the loss; `Context.Lost` is that record or `gl.isContextLost()` (`loseContext()` takes effect at once and its event comes in a task of its own). While lost, `Render` draws nothing and returns no error, and a capture or a snapshot is `WebGLContextLost`. When the context is back, the next `Render` or `Snapshot` makes the device again (`Context.rebuild`: the GL binding and the device are made anew, the old ones dropped without deleting anything), drops the resource caches and the raster cache (`Renderer.recover`) and forgets each surface's last frame, so that the frame is drawn whole and every cache refills. `RendererOpts.OnContextRestored` is called from the browser's event (any goroutine) and the engine posts a frame request to the UI thread (`Config.redraw`), which draws the last scene again.
- **A new canvas for each renderer after the first.** A canvas keeps the first kind of context it was asked for, so the chooser calls `Config.renewSurface` before every try after the first, and the engine asks the window that has the handle, if it implements the new optional `platform.SurfaceRenewer`, for a new surface. The web window's `RenewSurface` makes a new canvas with the same styles, size, cursor and listeners, moves the ResizeObserver to it and puts it where the old one was (`replaceWith`). The first try uses the canvas the platform made. A renderer that fails after it made a WebGL context loses it at once (`WEBGL_lose_context`) so that the GPU memory is not held until the canvas is collected.
- **The web renderer list** is `webgl` then `soft` (`backends_js.go`; since step 21.5 it is `webgpu`, `webgl`, `soft`). The engine's `glesRenderer` is now untagged and shared: `newGLRenderer(name, gles.NewRenderer, damage, ...)` makes `gles` on Linux and `webgl` on the web (no damage on the web). `GLUTTER_RENDERER=gles` on the web is `RendererUnavailable`.
- **The browsers.** Headless Chromium has no GPU in the test environment; its WebGL 2 is SwiftShader, which it allows only with `--enable-unsafe-swiftshader` (in `internal/webtest/browser.go`, with the reason). (Since step 21.5 Chromium also gets `--enable-features=Vulkan --use-angle=vulkan`, which give it the machine's Vulkan GPU for WebGPU, §9.5; its WebGL 2 then runs on that GPU through ANGLE, and SwiftShader is the fallback of a machine without one.) Firefox on Linux has no native GL without a display (`getContext("webgl2")` answers null, `FEATURE_FAILURE_WEBGL_EXHAUSTED_DRIVERS`, even with `webgl.force-enabled`, `gfx.webrender.software` and `EGL_PLATFORM=surfaceless`), so `webtest.Launch` starts a private `Xvfb` (`-displayfd`, under `timeout -k`, in its own process group, stopped by `Process.Stop`) for Firefox and sets `DISPLAY` to it; its headless mode then draws WebGL with Mesa's software rasterizer. The user's display is still never passed. Without Xvfb installed (`sudo dnf install xorg-x11-server-Xvfb`) Firefox runs with no display and the WebGL browser tests fail, saying so.
- **Browser tests** are `internal/gpu/gles/glwebtest` (a program whose query parameter `mode` is `parity`, `unavailable`, `loss`, `choose` or `restore`) and `browser_test.go`: every proof scene against `soft` within `gputest.ProofTolerances` with nothing skipped, the snapshot equal to the frame and a repeated frame equal to the first; the tags of the failures; the loss with `WEBGL_lose_context` and the pixel-for-pixel equal frames after the restoration; the choice through the engine (`auto`, `webgl` and `soft` by name, `auto` with `getContext("webgl2")` made to answer null falls back to `soft` on a new canvas with one canvas left, `webgl` by name then is `WebGL2Unavailable`); and an idle app that asks for a frame only after the restoration.

### 9.2 Software backend (`internal/gpu/soft`)

A pure-Go scanline rasterizer computing exact area coverage. It renders the same `ui.Scene` into `*image.RGBA` and implements every feature in §9.1. It serves as:
1. the renderer for golden tests (deterministic, no GPU)
2. the backend for `Picture.ToImage` in headless tests
3. the shared path and glyph rasterizer used by `gles` for its atlases

Tests tagged `glutter_display` compare `gles` against `soft` within a per-pixel tolerance.

### 9.3 Explicit-API renderer (`internal/gpu/explicit`)

One renderer for Vulkan and WebGPU (D19). It draws a `ui.Scene` from the draw lists of `gpu.Flatten`, like `gles`, on top of `explicit.Device`, and implements the engine's renderer and `ui.Rasterizer`. Every technique in the §9.1 table holds for it too (analytic antialiasing, coverage atlas, clip masks, layers, blend modes, blur, raster cache, damage); the differences are listed here. The code that does not depend on GL (instance encoding, paint parameters, uniform layouts, batching keys, the atlas, the raster-cache rule, damage tracking) moves from `gles` into `internal/gpu`, so both renderers share it.

The device interface, a sketch; the details are decided in step 19.3:

```go
// internal/gpu/explicit
type Device interface {
	Limits() Limits
	NewBuffer(size int, usage BufferUsage) (Buffer, error)
	NewTexture(desc TextureDesc) (Texture, error)     // 2D: rgba8unorm, bgra8unorm or r8unorm, with mip levels
	NewSampler(desc SamplerDesc) (Sampler, error)
	NewPipeline(desc PipelineDesc) (Pipeline, error)  // a generated shader, vertex layout, blend state, target format
	NewBindGroup(p Pipeline, group int, entries []Binding) (BindGroup, error)
	WriteBuffer(b Buffer, offset int, data []byte)
	WriteTexture(t Texture, level int, r image.Rectangle, data []byte, stride int)
	NewSwapchain(h gpu.SurfaceHandle) (Swapchain, error)
	Begin() (Encoder, error)                          // one per frame
	Close() error
}

type Encoder interface {
	BeginPass(target Texture, load LoadOp, clear ui.Color) Pass
	CopyTexture(src Texture, r image.Rectangle, dst Texture, at image.Point)
	ReadTexture(src Texture, r image.Rectangle, done func(*image.RGBA, error)) // done runs once the GPU finished
	Submit() error
}

type Pass interface {
	SetPipeline(Pipeline)
	SetBindGroup(group int, g BindGroup, dynamicOffsets ...uint32)
	SetVertexBuffer(slot int, b Buffer, offset int)
	SetIndexBuffer(b Buffer, offset int)
	SetScissor(r image.Rectangle)
	Draw(vertices, instances, firstVertex, firstInstance int)
	DrawIndexed(indices, instances, firstIndex, baseVertex, firstInstance int)
	End()
}

type Swapchain interface {
	Configure(width, height int) error     // the size in device pixels
	Acquire() (Texture, error)             // out of date: Configure again and retry once
	Present(damage []image.Rectangle) error
	Destroy()
}
```

**Decided in step 19.3** (`internal/gpu/explicit/device.go`, `limits.go`, `shader.go`): the sketch above holds, with these details.
- Every uniform buffer binding takes a dynamic offset (the ring buffer of a frame), one offset per uniform binding of the group in binding order. `BufferUsage`, `TextureUsage` (`TextureSampled`, `TextureRenderTarget`, `TextureCopySource`), `TextureFormat`, `BlendState` (source and destination factors, off by default) and `VertexBufferLayout` describe what the backends need; every enum's zero value is `Unspecified` or the documented default.
- A broken limit is checked by `Limits.CheckTexture`, `CheckPipeline`, `CheckBindGroup`, `CheckSetBindGroup` and `CheckSetVertexBuffer`, which each backend and `fakedevice` call: in debug builds they panic with `GPULimitExceeded` (metadata `limit`, `value`, and `max` or `alignment`). `Limits.AlignedRowPitch` rounds a row up to `CopyRowPitchAlignment`.
- `glutter-gen shaders` writes, for each `shaders/<name>.wgsl`, `shaders/<name>.spv` (one module with every entry point) and `shaders_gen.go`: `ShaderBlit`, `ShaderSolid`, ... of type `Shader` (name, WGSL, SPIR-V through `embed`, and the bindings of each entry point). An entry point reads a binding when its name is in its body or in a function it calls. Only `var<uniform>`, `texture_2d<f32>` and `sampler` bindings are accepted. The header of `shaders_gen.go` records naga's version, and a test compares a fresh generation with the checked-in files when that version is installed.

**Decided in step 20.3** (`internal/gpu/explicit`, `internal/gpu`):
- What `gles` and the explicit renderer share lives in `internal/gpu`: `Affine2D`, the instance layout and `ShapeEncoder` (`shapes.go`: every analytic shape becomes `gpu.ShapeInstance`s, the same twelve vec4 attributes for both shaders; it returns the name of a feature when a shape has no analytic form and the coverage atlas has to draw it), `FixedBlendOf` (the four modes the blend unit makes exactly) and `PaintOf`. `gles` keeps unexported aliases (`affine2D`, `rrectParts`, `kind*`) and wraps the encoder. The paint uniform layouts and the batching of draw calls stay in `gles` until the steps that need them (20.4 and 20.5): the explicit renderer has its own, smaller `callKey` (fixed blend and scissor).
- `shaders/shape.wgsl` is `shape.{vert,frag}.glsl` with `sdf.glsl`: 6 vertices per instance, flat varyings, the position of the pixel from a varying and not from the built-in. WGSL wants derivatives in uniform control flow, so the fragment shader finds every distance first and takes `dpdx` and `dpdy` of it after the branches, before the `discard`; the clip masks and the paint's shader, filter and read-the-destination blend are left out until steps 20.4 and 20.5.
- A frame is flattened and built into instances and draw calls before the device is touched (`frame.go`), so a feature the renderer cannot draw fails with `GPUNotImplemented` (metadata `feature`) before a frame begins, or with `RendererOpts.SkipUnsupported` is skipped and listed in `LastFrame`. Consecutive instances with the same fixed blend and scissor are one draw; each draw sets its scissor, its vertex buffer offset and draws 6 vertices times the instances.
- `WriteBuffer` copies at once (Vulkan's buffers are mapped), so the renderer keeps one set of dynamic buffers for each frame in flight (`explicit.FramesInFlight`, 2): the instances, and the uniform ring (the frame's size and origin at dynamic offset 0, 256-byte aligned), grown when a frame needs more and never shrunk. The frame with number n uses set n % 2; the device's slots alternate with every `Submit`, so the device has finished the set when `Begin` returns.
- Pipelines are made when first needed: one per fixed blend, for the frame texture's format (rgba8unorm).

**Decided in step 20.4** (paths, clip masks, images, text):
- `Texture.Level(i)` is added to the device interface: a texture over one mip level (the size of that level, one level) that a pass can draw into and a bind group can sample. The downsampling pass that makes mipmaps (SPEC §9.3 "Frames") draws each level from the one above with a linear sampler, through `blit.wgsl`, which averages four texels; the levels of an image are made in the frame that first draws it, before the scene's pass. Vulkan makes one view for each level (owned by the parent texture).
- The pure parts of `gles`' paths, clip masks, images and vertices moved into `internal/gpu` and are shared: `MaskCache` (the coverage atlas and the tiling of shapes into it; it is given the rasterizer, so `gpu` does not import `soft`, the way to make a page and the way to upload a tile), `ShapeOf`, `StyleKey`, `MaskMargin`, `ClipKey`, `ClipCoverage`, `ImageEncoder` (rects, nine-patches and sprites), `NinePieces`, `MipLevels`, `VertexTriangles`, `BlendIndex` and the cache budgets. `gles` and the explicit renderer keep only their own textures and draw calls.
- Bind groups of the primitive pipelines: group 0 is the frame uniform and the draw uniform, both with dynamic offsets (one 256-aligned ring entry for each draw call: the clip mask's rectangle, and for vertices the paint, the clip and the blend index too); group 1 is the clip mask texture (a 1x1 white texture when the clip has no shapes); group 2 is the pipeline's texture: an atlas page (`mask.wgsl`), or an image with its sampler (`image.wgsl`). A call's bind groups are made when the call needs them and kept by the device until the frame is done.
- Text needs nothing of its own: `gpu.Flatten` expands a paragraph into the paths of its glyphs, which are drawn from the atlas as in `gles`. The color glyphs painted with gradients wait for step 20.5.
- Still unsupported when step 20.4 was done, named by `GPUNotImplemented`: shaders (gradients), color filters, blend modes that read the destination, sprites with colors, vertices blended by a mode other than src and dst and layers (all drawn since step 20.5); mask filters and image filters (step 20.6). An image larger than the device's textures fails with `GPUImageTooLarge` (skipped with `SkipUnsupported`), and a disposed image with `GPUImageDisposed`.

**Decided in step 20.5** (gradients, blend modes, layers):
- The paint of a draw (shader, color filter, inverted colors, blend) is a `gpu.PaintSpec`, shared with `gles`: `SetGradient`, `SetImageShader` and `SetColorFilter` fill it, and the renderers send it to their shaders. `explicit/shaders/paint.wgsl.inc` holds the one `Paint` uniform (55 vec4, 880 bytes: shader kind, the inverse transform, the gradient geometry, the image info, the constant color, flags, blend index, the color filter and the ramp of up to 32 stops) and every function that reads it, and `blend.wgsl.inc` the blend functions of `blend.glsl`. `glutter-gen shaders` expands `//#include "file.wgsl.inc"` lines before it runs naga, and `Shader.WGSL` holds the expanded text.
- Bind groups of the primitive pipelines are now: group 0 frame, draw and paint uniforms (dynamic offsets), group 1 the clip mask, group 2 the pipeline's texture, group 3 the image-shader texture (binding 0, read with `textureLoad`) and the copy of the destination (binding 1); a 1x1 stand-in fills what a pipeline does not read, and `declares()` keeps only the bindings the shader uses.
- Porter-Duff modes the blend state can express (SPEC §9.1) are fixed-function. Every other mode reads a copy of the frame texture: the region of the draw call's scissor is copied to a `dst` texture between two passes (`CopyTexture`, then a pass that loads the target), and the shader blends the source with it. The copy happens once for each call of a reading blend, so such calls are not batched together.
- `SaveLayer`, opacity, color-filter and clip layers draw into pooled RGBA8 textures whose sizes are rounded up to 64-pixel granules (`pool.go`) and are composited with the `layer` pipeline (`layer.wgsl`) when they end. Inside a layer the frame uniform's surface is the texture's size and its origin is the layer rect's top left, so the shaders need no other coordinate system; scissors are in surface coordinates and the recorder subtracts the layer's origin. Layers outside the surface are culled, and nesting uses one pooled texture for each level.
- Still unsupported when step 20.5 was done, named by `GPUNotImplemented`: image filters, backdrop filters, shader masks and mask filters (drawn since step 20.6).

**Decided in step 20.6** (effects, raster cache, snapshots, damage):
- What `gles` and the explicit renderer plan the same way moved into `internal/gpu`: the filter passes (`FilterPass`, `BlurPasses`, `FilterPasses`: the blur, dilation, erosion, matrix and color passes, the downsampling of a large blur and its kernel), the shadow of `Canvas.drawShadow` (`PlanShadow`, the Skia constants), `PaintAccess`, `WhiteFill`, `StyleCode`, and the raster cache policy (`LayerCache[T]`, generic over the renderer's texture; the rule of step 17.2). The renderers run the plans with their own shaders and textures.
- A paint with a mask filter is drawn as in `gles`: a filled rectangle, rounded rectangle, oval or circle under a transform that only scales and moves is one instance of the shape pipeline's `KIND_SHADOW`, the closed form of the blur (`shadow.wgsl.inc`, ported from `shadow.glsl`; `shape.wgsl` computes its coverage without an early return, so the derivatives stay in uniform control flow); any other shape is drawn white into a layer that is blurred, whose alpha then fills the paint through the `layer` pipeline (`layerOwn` is the layer before the blur, for the solid, outer and inner styles). Image filters, backdrop filters, shader masks and `drawShadow` are layers whose `effect` the recorder runs.
- The filter passes run with `filter.wgsl`: one full-screen triangle for each pass, over `layerTexture`s of the pass's size rounded to the granule, with a scissor of the exact size. The pass's parameters (`Pass`, 6 float4s) and its paint (only a color-filter pass has one; the others share an empty paint) are entries of the uniform ring, planned with the frame's, and its source is group 1. A blur that needs it first averages the picture down by a power of two (`PassDown`) and scales it up again afterwards (`PassUp`). A backdrop filter copies the region under the layer (`CopyTexture`) into a pooled texture, runs its passes from it into the layer's texture, and the layer's pass keeps the result (`LoadLoad`).
- The raster cache is `RendererOpts.RasterCacheAfter` (the engine sets 3 unless `GLUTTER_RASTER_CACHE=0`). A layer the flattener folds into a group (`FlattenOpts.Boundary`) that has been in the last three scenes is drawn into a pooled texture that the cache keeps (`opComposite` with `keep`), and the frames after draw that texture with one `opDrawCached` instance of the layer pipeline; the group is composited under the clip it is in, so the clip's edge applies to it as a whole. The cache is not used by snapshots, and its textures return to the pool when evicted (120 idle frames, or 64 MiB).
- `Renderer.Snapshot` and `RasterizeScene` (`ui.Rasterizer`) draw a scene without a window into a pooled texture the size of the image, over transparent pixels, and read it back premultiplied, top row first. They need the device, which the first frame of a window opens (`RendererNotAttached` before), and do not change `LastFrame`.
- Damage (`gpu.DamageTracker`) was wired in step 20.2 (`Swapchain.Present(damage)` into `VK_KHR_incremental_present`); `FrameStats` now says what a frame reported (`DamagedPixels`, `DamageRects`), as `gles` does.
- Everything `gles` draws is drawn: `GPUNotImplemented` remains only for a perspective transform, a gradient with more than 32 stops, an unknown blend mode, an unknown image filter or layer type and an unknown operation.

**Decided in step 20.7** (Vulkan by default, device loss):
- The candidates of `auto` on Linux are `vulkan`, then `gles`. A Vulkan whose only device is a CPU gives `VulkanNoDevice` unless Vulkan was asked for by name (`Config.rendererNamed`), and the chooser then uses GLES; every failure before the first presented frame logs `renderer not usable, trying the next` once, with `renderer` and the error's `tag`. After the first frame the choice is for the whole process.
- Device loss is handled by `explicit.Renderer.Draw`, so Vulkan and WebGPU share it: an error tagged `VulkanDeviceLost` or `GPUDeviceLost` from a frame destroys everything that belongs to the device (windows' swapchains and frame textures, pipelines, samplers, pools, the raster cache, the image and coverage caches, the device) and draws the frame again, which opens a new device for the same windows. A second loss less than 10 seconds after the first returns the error unchanged, which ends `RunApp`. `RendererOpts.Now` is the clock the rule reads, so tests move it, and `RendererOpts.Log` receives the Info line that says that the device was lost.
- The comparison of the two renderers on a GPU (the performance table of `docs/PERFORMANCE.md`) needs a window that a GPU can present to, which the headless compositor does not give: it is measured on the user's desktop (step 20.7's manual checks).

**Design limits.** These are WebGPU's default limits, so neither backend requests more:
- at most 4 bind groups; at most 16 sampled textures, 16 samplers and 12 uniform buffers per shader stage; dynamic uniform offsets aligned to 256 bytes; a uniform binding of at most 64 KiB
- textures of at most 8192 pixels per side; at most 8 vertex buffers and 16 vertex attributes
- copies between buffers and textures use a row pitch that is a multiple of 256 bytes
- no push constants, no geometry or tessellation shaders, no compute (in 1.1), no multisampling, no stencil (clips use coverage masks, §9.1), no sRGB formats (blending in non-linear sRGB, as §9.1)
- a texture read back is delivered later (`ReadTexture`'s `done`), never inside the frame that asked for it

**Frames.**
- Each frame draws into an offscreen *frame texture* (rgba8unorm, the window's size) and ends with one blit into the swapchain image, whatever its format. The frame texture is what advanced blend modes and backdrop filters copy from, and what screenshots read, so neither depends on the swapchain's format or usage flags.
- Uniforms go into a ring buffer per frame and are addressed with dynamic offsets; instance data goes into vertex buffers (instanced quads, as in `gles`).
- At most 2 frames are in flight on the GPU. The resources a frame uses are kept until the GPU finished it (a fence on Vulkan, `onSubmittedWorkDone` on WebGPU).
- Mipmaps are made by a downsampling render pass, because neither API has `glGenerateMipmap`.
- `Snapshot` (`Picture.ToImage`) draws offscreen and reads back. Vulkan waits for the fence. WebGPU cannot wait (§23.3), so the `webgpu` renderer's `Snapshot` draws with `soft` (§12.10).

**Errors**: `ShaderModuleInvalid` (an internal bug, with the message of naga or of the driver), `GPUOutOfMemory`, `GPUDeviceLost`. A broken design limit is a programmer error: it panics with `GPULimitExceeded` in debug builds (§14.2).

**Tests**: `explicit/fakedevice` records every call and checks the design limits, for unit tests without a GPU. Each backend runs the parity test over every golden scene against `soft`, with the tolerance `gles` uses.

### 9.4 Vulkan backend (`internal/gpu/vulkan`)

- **Loading**: `purego.Dlopen("libvulkan.so.1")` (`GLUTTER_VULKAN_LIBRARY` overrides it), then `vkGetInstanceProcAddr`, and the device's functions through `vkGetDeviceProcAddr`. The functions are listed in one table, as in `gl.go`; a missing one is `SymbolMissing`.
- **Version**: Vulkan 1.3, or 1.1 or later with `VK_KHR_dynamic_rendering` and `VK_KHR_synchronization2`. There are no render pass objects. Without these, `VulkanUnsupported`.
- **Instance extensions**: `VK_KHR_surface` and `VK_KHR_wayland_surface` (the `wl_display` and `wl_surface`) or `VK_KHR_xlib_surface` (the Xlib `Display*` and the window). `VK_EXT_debug_utils` only with validation.
- **Device**: the first physical device in the loader's order that has a queue family with graphics and present support for the window's surface, and the version or extensions above. Mesa's device-select layer puts the GPU of the display first and honors `DRI_PRIME`. `GLUTTER_VULKAN_DEVICE=<index>` or `=<part of the device name>` picks another. A device of type CPU (lavapipe) is used only when Vulkan is asked for (`GLUTTER_RENDERER=vulkan`); `auto` uses GLES instead (§9.6).
- One `VkInstance`, one `VkDevice` and one queue serve every window; each window has a swapchain.
- **Swapchain**: `B8G8R8A8_UNORM` or `R8G8B8A8_UNORM`, never an SRGB format; composite alpha opaque; present mode MAILBOX, else IMMEDIATE, else FIFO (§8.11); usage color attachment and transfer destination; the driver's minimum image count plus one. `VK_ERROR_OUT_OF_DATE_KHR` and `VK_SUBOPTIMAL_KHR` recreate it, at the size of the next frame.
- **Damage**: the damage rectangles (§9.1 "Damage") go to `VK_KHR_incremental_present` when the device has it; otherwise frames are presented whole. `GLUTTER_DAMAGE=0` and `Config.DisableDamage` work as with GLES.
- **Memory**: blocks of 64 MiB per memory type, sub-allocated with a free list. Uniform, instance and upload buffers are host-visible, coherent and mapped for good; textures are device-local and filled through a staging ring.
- **purego rules** (§8.1): structs that point to other data (create infos, `pNext` chains and the arrays they point to) are built in a C-memory arena (libc `malloc`) that is reset after each frame, never in Go memory. Every Go mirror of a Vulkan struct has a layout test against `/usr/include/vulkan/vulkan_core.h`. Non-dispatchable handles are `uint64`, so only 64-bit targets are supported. A test checks that no function in the table takes a struct by value. The only callback is the debug-utils messenger, created once and only with validation.
- **Validation**: `GLUTTER_VULKAN_VALIDATION=1` turns on `VK_LAYER_KHRONOS_validation` and logs its messages. The display tests turn it on and fail on any validation error.
- **Device lost**: every resource is recreated once and the frame is drawn again. A second loss within 10 seconds ends `RunApp` with `VulkanDeviceLost`.
- `GLUTTER_SCREENSHOT` and `Snapshot` read the frame texture back.

**Decided in step 20.1** (`internal/gpu/vulkan`): offscreen only for now; the window part (surfaces, swapchains) is step 20.2.
- `vulkan.Open(Options)` loads the library, makes the instance, chooses the physical device (`choose.go`, a pure function over `physicalInfo`: the version or extensions, the `dynamicRendering` and `synchronization2` features, textures of 8192 pixels, a queue family with graphics and, later, present support; a CPU device only with `Options.AllowCPU`; `GLUTTER_VULKAN_DEVICE` as an index or part of a name) and makes the device with one queue. It returns an `explicit.Device`.
- Every texture lives in the `GENERAL` layout (drawn into, sampled, copied from and to), so no pass or bind group has to track layouts; after each pass, copy and upload one global memory barrier makes the writes visible. The backend uses synchronization 2 for barriers and `vkQueueSubmit` for submits.
- Two frame slots, each with a command pool and buffer, a fence and descriptor pools. `Begin` waits for the slot's fence; bind groups are descriptor sets of the pool of the frame being recorded (or of the next frame, before `Begin`), valid until the slot is reused; a destroyed resource is released when the fence of that frame is done. `ReadTexture` copies into a host buffer, and its `done` runs at the end of `Submit` after the fence (so a read waits for the GPU).
- Staging: one 16 MiB host buffer, half for each slot, reset when the slot is reused; a larger upload gets a buffer of its own that the frame releases. Memory blocks are separate for buffers and for images of a memory type, so the buffer-image granularity never matters.
- `GPUOutOfMemory`, `VulkanDeviceLost` and `VulkanCallFailed` (any other `VkResult`) come from one function, `vkError`. The programmer errors are `VulkanFrameOpen`, `VulkanFrameNotOpen`, `VulkanPassNotEnded`, `VulkanForeignResource`, `VulkanWriteOutOfRange` and the invalid-description tags `VulkanTextureInvalid`, `VulkanPipelineInvalid` and `VulkanBindGroupInvalid`.

**Decided in step 20.2** (windows):
- A `VkSurfaceKHR` is made from the `SurfaceHandle` (`VK_KHR_wayland_surface` or `VK_KHR_xlib_surface`, with `VK_KHR_surface`); the instance enables the extensions of the surface kind that `Options.Surface` names, and the device choice then requires `VK_KHR_swapchain` and a queue family that can present to that surface. `VulkanSurfaceFailed` covers a missing extension, a lost surface and a window that is in use; `VulkanSwapchainFailed` covers the swapchain.
- The swapchain format is `BGRA8` or `RGBA8` unorm in the sRGB-nonlinear color space (never an `_SRGB` format: colors are blended as the other renderers do). Present mode: `MAILBOX`, then `IMMEDIATE`, then `FIFO` (always there). Image count is `minImageCount + 1`, capped by the maximum.
- `Swapchain.Acquire` needs the frame's encoder to be open; the swapchain image is blitted from the frame texture, moved to `PRESENT_SRC`, and presented after the frame's submit. `VK_ERROR_OUT_OF_DATE_KHR` at acquire returns `explicit.SwapchainOutOfDate`, and `explicit.Renderer` configures the swapchain again at the current size and retries once; `VK_SUBOPTIMAL_KHR` (at acquire or present) and an out-of-date present only mark the swapchain, which the next frame makes again.
- Recreating or destroying a swapchain waits for the device to be idle (`vkDeviceWaitIdle`) first, which keeps the semaphores of the old images safe to destroy; a resize is not a hot path.
- Present damage (`VK_KHR_incremental_present`) is given from the top left of the frame, as the platform wants it; a device without the extension presents the whole image.
- Until step 20.7 (fallback and defaults) `vulkan` comes after `gles` in the engine's candidate list, so it is used only when `GLUTTER_RENDERER=vulkan` names it; a named renderer may use a CPU device (`Options.AllowCPU`).

**Errors**: `VulkanLoadFailed`, `VulkanUnsupported`, `VulkanNoDevice`, `VulkanSurfaceFailed`, `VulkanSwapchainFailed`, `VulkanDeviceLost`, `GPUOutOfMemory`. With `auto`, the ones that happen before the first frame is presented make the engine use GLES (§9.6).

### 9.5 WebGPU backend (`internal/gpu/webgpu`)

- `navigator.gpu` through `syscall/js`. `requestAdapter()` and `requestDevice()` with the default limits (they are the design limits of §9.3, so no limit or feature is requested). Both are awaited at startup, before the platform installs its event listeners (§23.3).
- The swapchain is the canvas's `webgpu` context: `configure({device, format: navigator.gpu.getPreferredCanvasFormat(), alphaMode: "opaque"})`. `Acquire` is `getCurrentTexture()`; presenting happens when the browser task ends; `Configure` sets the canvas's width and height.
- Shaders are the WGSL sources as they are (`createShaderModule`). Errors in `getCompilationInfo()` are `ShaderModuleInvalid`.
- Data reaches the GPU in few, large calls: a frame's instance and uniform data are written with one `queue.writeBuffer` each, through one reused `Uint8Array` (`js.CopyBytesToJS`).
- `ReadTexture` is `copyTextureToBuffer` and `mapAsync`; its `done` runs in a later task. It serves screenshots only; `Snapshot` uses `soft` (§9.3).
- **Device lost** (`device.lost`): a new device is requested and every resource recreated. If that fails, or the device is lost a second time within ten seconds, the page switches to WebGL 2 (§9.6; "Decided in step 21.6" below).

**Errors**: `WebGPUUnavailable` (no `navigator.gpu`, or no adapter), `WebGPUDeviceFailed`, `GPUDeviceLost`, `ShaderModuleInvalid`.

**Decided in step 21.5** (`internal/gpu/webgpu`):
- **Plain Go and thin `syscall/js`.** `mapping.go` (the names the API uses for usages, formats, filters, address modes, vertex formats and blend factors), `layout.go` (`planLayouts`: the bind group layouts of a pipeline from the bindings of its two entry points), `supply.go` (the devices that the browser gives, and what is asked for after a loss), `loss.go` (`lossState`), `readback.go` (`unpackRows`), `errors.go` and `renderer.go` are plain Go, tested in the normal run and under Node. `provider_js.go`, `device_js.go`, `frames_js.go`, `swapchain_js.go` and `script_js.go` carry `js && wasm`; elsewhere `unavailable.go` makes `Acquire` fail with `WebGPUUnavailable`, so `go build ./...` and `go vet ./...` work on Linux.
- **Startup is awaited, once.** `webgpu.Acquire(ctx, Options)` runs a small script (`script_js.go`) that awaits `requestAdapter()`, `requestDevice()` with the default limits, and a `createShaderModule` and `getCompilationInfo()` for every shader (`explicit.Shaders()`); it fails with `WebGPUUnavailable` (no `navigator.gpu`, or the adapter is null), `WebGPUDeviceFailed` (the device is refused, or `Options.Timeout`, 20 s by default, or the context ends) or `ShaderModuleInvalid` (every error message of the browser's compiler, with the module name and line). It is the one place where the UI goroutine waits for promises: the web's `connectDisplay` calls it, before the platform exists and so before the listeners, when the renderer is `auto` or `webgpu` (not for `webgl` or `soft`). Why there is no device is kept and returned from the first frame of the `webgpu` renderer, so `auto` logs it and goes on to WebGL 2, and `webgpu` asked for by name ends `RunApp` with it. A pipeline's own errors (a layout that does not fit its shader) are validation errors of the browser, which no call can wait for: they come to the page's `uncapturederror` event, which `Provider.ValidationErrors` collects and the log shows (the browser tests fail on any).
- **The device.** `Provider.Open` is the open function of the explicit renderer; the `Device` it makes holds the `GPUDevice`, its queue and the compiled modules. Pipelines have explicit bind group layouts (a group with no binding gets an empty one so that the numbers are the shader's), because an `"auto"` layout cannot have dynamic offsets, which every uniform binding uses (§9.3); a binding is visible to the stages whose entry point reads it. Buffers and textures always have `COPY_DST` (the queue writes them); a render target also has `COPY_SRC`. A buffer's size is rounded up to four bytes (`writeBuffer`'s rule). `WriteBuffer` and `WriteTexture` copy into one reused `Uint8Array` with `js.CopyBytesToJS` and make one queue call. A destroyed buffer or texture is destroyed when the frame being recorded is submitted (WebGPU keeps what submitted work uses), and the frames-in-flight bookkeeping of Vulkan has no counterpart: the queue orders `writeBuffer` with the submits. A call that throws becomes `WebGPUCallFailed`; a texture, pipeline or bind group that cannot be described is `WebGPUTextureInvalid`, `WebGPUPipelineInvalid` or `WebGPUBindGroupInvalid`; the programmer errors have the tags of the Vulkan backend with the prefix `WebGPU` (`WebGPUFrameOpen`, `WebGPUFrameNotOpen`, `WebGPUPassNotEnded`, `WebGPUForeignResource`, `WebGPUWriteOutOfRange`, `WebGPULevelInvalid`).
- **The swapchain** is the canvas of the view (`data-glutter-view`, as `canvas2d` and `gles` find it): `getContext("webgpu")`, `configure({device, format: getPreferredCanvasFormat(), alphaMode: "opaque"})` once (the format must be `rgba8unorm` or `bgra8unorm`), `Configure` sets the canvas's size, `Acquire` is `getCurrentTexture()` (it never says out of date), `Present` does nothing (the browser presents when the task ends, and has no damage; the engine passes `Damage: false`). Failures: `WebGPUSurfaceKindUnsupported` (a handle that is not a canvas's), `WebGPUCanvasNotFound`, `WebGPUContextUnavailable` (the canvas has a context of another kind already) and `WebGPUConfigureFailed`.
- **A read-back that finishes later.** `ReadTexture` records `copyTextureToBuffer` into a `MAP_READ` buffer (rows padded to 256 bytes); `Submit` queues the frame and then starts `mapAsync` without waiting; the script's `then` calls a callback, made once at startup, that copies the pixels out, releases the buffer, and runs `done` on the UI goroutine through `Options.Post` (the platform's `Post`). `explicit.Renderer.Draw` of a capture on such a device returns no image and no error, and hands the image to `RendererOpts.OnCapture` when it arrives; a device that finishes reads inside `Submit` (Vulkan, the fake device) still returns it from `Draw`, and without `OnCapture` a late capture is `RendererCaptureFailed`. The engine's `Config.captured` is that callback: `GLUTTER_SCREENSHOT` on the web ends `Run` when the PNG, taken from the frame texture after the first idle frame, has been posted.
- **`Snapshot` is `soft`'s.** `webgpu.Renderer` embeds the explicit renderer and overrides `Snapshot` and `RasterizeScene` (`ui.Rasterizer`) with `soft.Render` over transparent pixels: the same pixels, so the browser tests compare it with `soft` exactly.
- **Device loss.** The script calls back on `device.lost` and on `uncapturederror`. A loss that is not the owner's own `destroy()` (a closed `Device` reports the reason `destroyed` too) marks the `Device`, which then fails every call that can fail with `GPUDeviceLost` (metadata `reason` and `message`; the writes do nothing), and the provider asks the browser for a new device at once, without waiting. The explicit renderer's loss path (§9.3) then runs as it does for Vulkan: destroy everything, open again. `open` finds the device either ready or still coming; for the second case the new error tag `GPUDevicePending` (`explicit.IsDevicePending`) means "no device yet, and the backend will tell": `Draw` draws nothing and returns no error for that frame, and when the device has arrived `Options.OnDeviceReady` (the engine's `redraw`, posted to the UI goroutine) asks for a frame, which opens it and draws everything again. A second loss within 10 seconds ends `RunApp` with `GPUDeviceLost`, as on Vulkan. A new device that the browser refuses is `WebGPUDeviceFailed` from the next frame; the last resort of the page switching to WebGL 2 is step 21.6 (below).
- **Added to the explicit package** (all additive): `Shaders()`, `GPUDevicePending` and `IsDevicePending`, `RendererOpts.OnCapture`.
- **Browser flags** (`internal/webtest/browser.go`, with the reason for each). Headless Chromium on Linux gets WebGPU from `--enable-unsafe-webgpu --enable-features=Vulkan --use-angle=vulkan`; with the first flag alone the adapter is SwiftShader's and the canvas's swap chain cannot be created in headless mode (`Could not find SharedImageBackingFactory`, then the device is destroyed at `getCurrentTexture`). With the three, the adapter is the machine's Vulkan GPU (the AMD RDNA 3 with RADV where step 21.5 was done, so these tests ran on a real GPU, not on a software adapter); WebGL 2 then also runs on that GPU through ANGLE, and `--enable-unsafe-swiftshader` stays for machines that have no Vulkan GPU. Firefox has WebGPU behind `dom.webgpu.enabled`, which the profile's `user.js` sets; Firefox 158 on Linux answered with an adapter and passed the same tests, so nothing is skipped for it. The tests still skip Firefox, with the reason from the program's probe, when its build has no adapter, and fail for Chromium.

**Decided in step 21.6** (the last resort of WebGPU, `internal/engine`):
- **What triggers it.** The explicit renderer's rule is unchanged: the first loss is recovered on a new device (asked for without waiting), a second loss within ten seconds returns `GPUDeviceLost` from `Draw`, and a new device that the browser refuses is `WebGPUDeviceFailed` from the next `Draw`. The engine's chooser (§9.6) treats those two tags as the last resort of the `webgpu` candidate (`rendererCandidate.SwitchOn`, set by `webgpuLastResort`; no other candidate has one, so Vulkan's second loss still ends `RunApp`, and WebGL 2's lost context is restored by the `gles` renderer itself, step 21.4).
- **What the switch does.** The chooser closes the WebGPU renderer, has the platform put a new `<canvas>` in place (`platform.SurfaceRenewer`, because the old one has a `webgpu` context for ever), and draws the same frame, with the same capture request, with the candidates that follow, by the rules of the first choice: `webgl`, and `soft` if WebGL 2 fails too. Each switch is logged once at Info level (`renderer given up, switching to the next`, with the tag), then the usual `renderer chosen` line names the new renderer. The app is not told. The switch is one way: the page does not go back to WebGPU, and a later loss of the WebGL 2 context is the `gles` renderer's own recovery (step 21.4).
- **By name.** `GLUTTER_RENDERER=webgpu` never falls back (§9.6): the second loss ends `RunApp` with `GPUDeviceLost`, as `GLUTTER_RENDERER=vulkan` does, and the loader shows the error in the page (§23.1). `auto` is the only choice that switches.
- **Not done.** A WebGPU device that arrives after the switch (the request that was still on its way) is destroyed when the provider is closed at the end of the program, not at once.

### 9.6 Choosing the renderer

- `RunAppOpts.Renderer` (empty means `"auto"`) overrides the environment variable `GLUTTER_RENDERER`. On Linux the values are `auto`, `vulkan` and `gles`; on the web `auto`, `webgpu`, `webgl` and `soft`; on Android `auto`, `vulkan`, `gles` and `soft`. Another value is `RendererUnknown`; a known renderer that this build or OS does not have is `RendererUnavailable`.
- **`auto` on Linux**: Vulkan, when the library loads, the instance has the surface extension of the backend, a device that is not a CPU presents to the first window, and the first frame is presented without an error. Otherwise GLES.
- **`auto` on the web**: WebGPU, when `navigator.gpu` gives an adapter and a device and the canvas configures. Otherwise WebGL 2 (`gles`) when `getContext("webgl2")` works. Otherwise `soft` drawing into a 2D canvas (`canvas2d`, with `putImageData`), so the app still shows. A canvas keeps the first kind of context it was asked for, so the platform puts a new canvas element in place before each try.
- **`auto` on Android** (1.6): Vulkan on Android 10 or later with Vulkan 1.1 and a driver that is not on Impeller's deny list, when the first frame is presented. Otherwise GLES. Otherwise `soft` into the `ANativeWindow` (§24.4).
- Each failed try is logged once at Info level with its tagged error, and the app is not told. A renderer that was asked for by name never falls back: its error is what `RunApp` returns.
- `RendererUnknown` and `RendererUnavailable` are returned before the display is touched: the names are checked against the list of the OS when the engine starts. The renderer itself is chosen at the first frame (`internal/engine/renderers.go`): each candidate is made and draws the first frame of the first window; the one that presents it is kept, and `gpu.SurfaceHandle` carries what each candidate needs (`Display`, `Window` for EGL, `Surface` for Vulkan).
- The choice is made once, at the first window, for the whole process, and every window uses that renderer. A GPU lost later is handled inside the renderer (§9.4, §9.5), except WebGPU's last resort, which switches the page to the next renderer of the list, WebGL 2 (`auto` only; a renderer asked for by name never switches, §9.5 "Decided in step 21.6").
- The startup log and `GLUTTER_FRAME_STATS` name the renderer and the device (e.g. `vulkan: AMD Radeon 8060S Graphics (RADV STRIX_HALO)`).

Flutter: no counterpart. Flutter's Linux embedder draws with GL only, and Flutter web chooses between CanvasKit and Skwasm.

---

## 10. Text

- **go-text/typesetting** is used for:
  - font parsing (TrueType, OpenType, CFF, variable fonts, bitmap fonts)
  - shaping (its HarfBuzz port)
  - bidi
  - UAX #14 line breaking and UAX #29 graphemes and words
  - system font discovery and fallback (`fontscan`, cached in `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/glutter/fonts`) when fontconfig is unavailable
- **fontconfig** (`libfontconfig.so.1`, §8.1), when present, decides family matching and fallback order, as in Flutter's Linux engine. That means `sans-serif`, the user's UI font, emoji and CJK preferences, and per-language rules. go-text then parses and shapes the files fontconfig picks. Antialiasing and hinting follow GNOME's `font-antialiasing` and `font-hinting` settings from the settings portal.
- **Bundled fonts** (in `material/fonts`, through `embed`):
  - Roboto. It's the first family in Flutter's Linux typography; the system fallbacks Ubuntu, Cantarell, DejaVu Sans and Liberation Sans follow it, as in Flutter
  - Material Icons: the font of the Flutter SDK (`MaterialIcons-Regular.otf`, whose code points are those of Flutter's `Icons`), with the license text that ships with it (CC BY 4.0)
- **Loaded fonts** (1.2, M22). An app adds fonts to the text engine while it runs: `PlatformDispatcher.LoadFontFromList(list, family)` (Flutter's `ui.loadFontFromList`) takes the bytes of a TrueType or OpenType file and a family name; `services.FontLoader` (Flutter's `FontLoader`) loads several fonts under one family from bytes, functions or the `AssetBundle`. Both run on the UI thread. A family can have several fonts (regular, bold, italic...): the weight and slant of each come from the font itself (its OS/2 table; a variable font with a `wght` axis counts as every weight in the axis), and a request gets the closest one first, then the others of the family in order of distance (they are the fallback inside the family). A loaded font of a family wins over a bundled font of the same name, and both win over the system fonts. Loading the same bytes again under the same family does nothing. After a load the framework lays the text out again (`PaintingBinding.SystemFonts` notifies, as Flutter's `fontsChange` message does). `RunAppOpts.Fonts` loads fonts before the first frame, from bytes or from `RunAppOpts.Assets`; `gluttertest.Opts.Fonts` gives a test engine fonts (§17).
- **Font fallback order** (1.2, M22). The fonts of a style are tried in this order, and the first face that has the glyph draws it:
  1. `TextStyle.FontFamily`, then each entry of `TextStyle.FontFamilyFallback`, in order. An entry gives only the faces that belong to it: the fonts the app loaded or Glutter bundles under that name, and the system fonts of that family (a system font belongs to the family it is named for, or to a family that the user's fontconfig, or fontscan, says the name stands for, such as Arial for Liberation Sans). A name with no such font gives nothing; the system's default font does not take its place. A **generic** family (`sans-serif`, `serif`, `monospace`, `cursive`, `fantasy`, `emoji`, `math`) gives the system's whole fallback list for it, best match first, as before: that is how `monospace` reaches the user's monospace font.
  2. `sans-serif`, the system's default.
  3. A font that has the character, from the matcher (fontscan's script-based fallback).
  4. The first font that loads, which draws the missing-glyph box.
- **Font queries** (1.2, M22). `PlatformDispatcher.ResolveFont(family, weight, style)` says which face a family resolves to: a `ui.ResolvedFont` with the family name of the face (which can differ from the name asked for: a generic name such as `monospace` gives the system's monospace font, an alias such as `Arial` the font it stands for), the file and the index in it (empty for a loaded or bundled font), where the font comes from (`FontOriginLoaded`, `FontOriginBundled`, `FontOriginSystem`) and its weight and slant as the font says. It is the first font of the family that loads, in the order of §10's fallback, and false for a family that has no font (it does not resolve to the default font). `PlatformDispatcher.InstalledFontFamilies()` lists the families of the system (fontconfig's `FcFontList`; without fontconfig, the families of the font scan, named as their fonts name them), the bundled and the loaded fonts, sorted without regard to case. `PlatformConfiguration` carries the desktop's font settings (`UIFontFamily`, `UIFontSize`, `MonospaceFontFamily`, `MonospaceFontSize`, in points, from GNOME's `font-name` and `monospace-font-name`, §8.8), read with `PlatformDispatcher.UIFont()` and `MonospaceFont()`. A text engine that cannot answer (`ui.FontQueryEngine`) gives false and nil.
- **Glyph outline cache** (1.2, M22). A paragraph's glyphs are drawn from cached outlines (`internal/text/layout/paint.go`); the cache keeps the most recently used 16,384 and drops the oldest when it is full, instead of clearing everything.
- **Tab stops** (1.4, M22; a Glutter addition: Flutter has none, §12.10). `ui.ParagraphStyleOpts.TabSize` is the distance between two tab stops, as a count of the advance of U+0020 SPACE in the paragraph's own font (the `FontFamily`, `FontSize`, `FontWeight` and `FontStyle` of the paragraph style, as the number of `tab-size` is in CSS); it is a `float64` and 0, the default, keeps Flutter's behavior (a tab is white space with no stop). A negative, `NaN` or infinite value panics with the tag `TabSizeInvalid`. `painting.TextPainterOpts.TabSize` (and `TextPainter.TabSize`/`SetTabSize`, `TextPainterComputeWidthOpts.TabSize`, `GetParagraphStyleOpts.TabSize`) passes it on; `Text`, `RichText`, `RenderParagraph` and `EditableText` do not have it yet. With a tab size, a tab advances to the next multiple of the tab width measured from the start of its line, as CSS's `tab-size` does; a tab that begins on a stop advances a whole tab width, never 0. The letter spacing and word spacing of the tab's style are added after the tab, as CSS does, so the next stop is measured from where the text goes on. The tab draws nothing.
  - *Start of the line.* Positions are measured in logical order from the start edge of the line: the left edge of a left-to-right paragraph, the right edge of a right-to-left one. A tab has the level of the paragraph (rule L1 of UAX #9: a segment separator takes the base level), so it is never inside a right-to-left run of a left-to-right paragraph and the text before it stays on its start side: the distance from the start edge to the tab is the sum of the advances before it, in either direction.
  - *Breaking.* A tab is a break opportunity (UAX #14 class BA, as before). A tab at the end of a line hangs, like other white space: it does not count for the width the line needs, so a line is never wrapped because of its last tab, and `LongestLine` does not include it. A wrapped line starts its tab stops again from its own start. The maximum intrinsic width follows the stops of the unwrapped lines; the minimum intrinsic width is the widest unbreakable word, as before.
  - *Queries.* The tab's advance is the advance of its glyph, so `GetBoxesForRange`, `GetPositionForOffset`, `GetGlyphInfoAt`, the caret metrics, the line metrics and the word and line boundaries all see the whole width; a caret after a tab is at the tab stop, and a justified line widens a tab like a space.
- **Shaped run cache** (1.4, M22). Shaping is the main cost of building a paragraph, and an editor builds again lines whose spans did not change, so the engine keeps the shaped glyphs of each run (a stretch of one style, one face, one direction and one script, §10's paragraph layout steps 1 and 2) between builds. The key is the text of the run, the font face, the font features, the direction, the script and the language, and, because HarfBuzz reads the text around a run, the pre-context (whether there is any, and for the scripts that join letters, the five runes before) and the post-context (for those scripts, the five runes after): two runs with the same key shape to the same glyphs. The size is not in the key, because the engine shapes at one size and scales the result; the font variations are part of the face. A hit costs a copy of the glyphs (letter and word spacing are applied after the cache). The cache belongs to the engine and, like its other caches, is used on the UI thread only; it holds at most 4,096 runs and 8 MiB of keys and glyphs, and drops the least recently used entry when either bound is passed. It is emptied when the fonts change (`LoadFontFromList`, which is also what the `SystemFontsDidChange` path follows): the engine compares the generation of its font collection. Its results equal fresh shaping (a fuzz test compares every run of random paragraphs with the pipeline as it was before the cache).
  - *Around the cache.* What the engine finds out about a text before shaping (the bidi levels, the pieces of one level and one script, the line break opportunities, the grapheme clusters and, when a query asks, the words) does not depend on the fonts or the styles, so it is kept by text, direction and language in a second least-recently-used cache (2,048 texts, 4 MiB), which fonts do not empty. Text that is all ASCII in a left-to-right paragraph, and text below U+0300 for the clusters, take shortcuts that give what go-text's segmenter gives (a test compares them on random text). The faces of the ASCII characters of a style are kept in an array, and the last eight styles that were looked up are found by their values. `ui.ParagraphBuilder` keeps one `ResolvedTextStyle` for each pushed style, and `ui.TextRun.Style` points to it.
  - *Layout on demand.* `BuildParagraph` no longer lays the paragraph out: the intrinsic widths are known at once, and anything else that asks (`Layout` for a width, or a query, or painting) finds it laid out, with an infinite width when nothing laid it out. Flutter's `ParagraphBuilder.build` does not lay out either; a caller that calls `Layout` (as `TextPainter` does) no longer pays for a layout it throws away.
- **Paragraph layout** (`internal/text`), in this order:
  1. Spans become runs, segmented by script, direction and font, with fallback.
  2. Runs are shaped.
  3. Lines are broken, honoring `softWrap`, `maxLines` and ellipsis (and, with `TabSize`, the tab stops of each line).
  4. Each line is reordered for bidi.
  5. Text is aligned and justified, with strut, height, leading distribution, letter and word spacing.
  6. Placeholders for `WidgetSpan` are laid out.
  7. Decorations are added: underline, overline and line-through in solid/double/dotted/dashed/wavy styles, with thickness.
  8. Metrics are computed: baselines, line metrics and intrinsics.
- **Queries for editing**: boxes for a range, position for an offset, word and line boundaries, caret metrics, glyph info.
- **Text scaling** follows Flutter's `TextScaler`, including clamping.
- **Font features and variations** are passed through to shaping.

### 11.3 Additions in 1.2 (M22)

- **`TreeSliver`** (`widgets`, Flutter's `widgets/sliver_tree.dart`): a sliver that shows a tree of nodes as an indented list that expands and collapses (`TreeSliverNode`, `TreeSliverController`, `TreeSliverStateMixin`'s toggling, `TreeSliverIndentationType`, `TreeSliverNodeCallback`, `TreeSliverNodeBuilder`, `TreeSliverRowBuilder` and the `RenderTreeSliver` that lays the rows out). It is a code editor's file tree. Ported from `reference/flutter/packages/flutter/lib/src/widgets/sliver_tree.dart` and `rendering/sliver_tree.dart` with their tests.
- **`ReorderableListView`** (`material`, Flutter's `material/reorderable_list.dart`): the Material list whose items the user drags to reorder (`NewReorderableListView`, `NewReorderableListViewBuilder`, `buildDefaultDragHandles`, the drag handle on desktop, the proxy decorator, `header`, `footer`). It is built on `widgets.SliverReorderableList`, which exists since step 9.3; it is done in step 22.8.
- **Tab stops and the shaped run cache** (1.4, §10): `ParagraphStyleOpts.TabSize`, passed through `TextPainterOpts.TabSize`, and a cache of shaped runs in the text engine that makes a line whose spans did not change cheap to build again (`docs/PERFORMANCE.md`).
