| `kElevationToShadow` and `kMaterialEdges` are `const` maps | `ElevationToShadow` and `MaterialEdges` are package variables that hold maps (the only exported variables that are not struct values, the rest of the table values like `Colors` being structs); they are read-only by contract | Go has no constant maps; `apicheck.allow` lists them |
| Enums whose zero value is not `Unspecified` (`ScriptCategory`, `DatePickerMode`, `StretchMode`, `Decorations`, `ScrollPositionAlignmentPolicy`, `DiagonalDragBehavior`, `SemanticsRole` and the other `ui` semantics enums, `WindowKind`, `ColorSpace`, `Assertiveness`, and the enums that are results, events or states such as `StateLifecycle`, `RoutePopDisposition`, `RefreshIndicatorStatus`, `PathVerb`, `DropPhase`, `DayPeriod`) | the zero value is Flutter's default for the enum, so `Unspecified` would mean the same thing, or no options struct takes the enum (it is a result, an event or a state) | §12.3 wants `Unspecified` so that an options struct can tell "not given" from the first value; neither case arises. `apicheck.allow` lists them, with the reason |
| `EditableText.debugDeterministicCursor` is a static variable | `widgets.DebugOptions.DeterministicCursor`, read through the binding in debug builds | no global mutable state (§12.9) |
| `kIsWeb` | `foundation.IsWeb`, a constant set by build tags | named like `DebugMode`; the framework reads it where Flutter reads `kIsWeb` |
| Flutter web draws with CanvasKit or Skwasm (Skia on WebGL 2) | WebGPU through `explicit`, else WebGL 2 through `gles`, else `soft` into a 2D canvas (§9.6) | Glutter's renderers are its own; there is no Skia |
| Flutter web downloads Noto fallback fonts from Google's font CDN | the web has the bundled fonts and the fonts the app loads; a character that none of them has draws as a missing-glyph box (§23.6) | no network access the app did not ask for; a page cannot read the system's fonts |
| Flutter web can render several views into host elements (multi-view) | one view per page, filling the page or one host element (§23.2); a second `NewWindow` fails with `WindowUnsupported` | the 1.1 scope |
| `Picture.toImage` is drawn by the GPU on every renderer | on WebGPU, `Snapshot` draws with `soft` (§9.3) | a WebGPU readback finishes in a later browser task, and `ToImage` returns at once |
| Flutter web has no renderer that gives up for another one at run time (CanvasKit or Skwasm is chosen at start-up) | a WebGPU whose device is lost a second time within ten seconds, or cannot be had again, switches the page to WebGL 2 on a new canvas (`auto` only; a renderer asked for by name ends `RunApp`) (§9.5) | Glutter's renderers are its own, and a page with a lost GPU should keep drawing |
| `url_launcher` opens `javascript:` URIs | `desktop.OpenURI` refuses `javascript:` and `vbscript:` with `OpenURISchemeBlocked` (§23.7) | they run script in the page that opens them; Flutter apps decide in their own code |
| a notification of the Linux desktop has buttons, a body in markup, a category, a time-out and an icon from the theme | on the web only a click (the action `"default"`), a plain-text body, an icon that is a URL and the flags `requireInteraction` and `silent` exist; the time-out is a timer of the page (§23.7) | the Notifications API without a service worker has no more |
| Flutter web finds the logical key of a key that is a character with the `web_locale_keymap` package, and makes up the ids of the keys it has no id for with Dart's `String.hashCode` | the lower case of the character, or the key of the layout goal where a mandatory goal's key is not a Latin letter or digit (as the Linux table does); made-up ids use a stable FNV-1a hash of the name (§23.4) | the package is not part of the sources the port reads, and `hashCode` is not portable |
| `PointerDataConverter` keeps the state of the pointers in a static, `BrowserContextMenu` is static and keeps a hidden singleton | the converter is a field of the platform's pointer input; `WidgetsBinding.BrowserContextMenu()` returns the controller, whose calls are done when they return (§23.4) | no global mutable state (CLAUDE.md), no futures (§12.6) |
| Flutter web sets the CSS cursor of the page's body and puts the key listeners on `window` (capture) | the cursor is set on the view's canvas and the key listeners are on the host element (§23.4) | a host element in a bigger page must not change the cursor of, or take the keys of, the rest of the page |
| the key records of the web keyboard converter survive a loss of the focus | the page's `blur` gives the framework a synthesized up for every key that is down (§23.4) | the keyups go to another window, and the framework would see a key down for ever |
| Flutter web's `ClickDebouncer` and the touch handling of the semantics placeholder | not ported (no semantics tree in 1.1, §23.7) | |
| `WidgetStateMouseCursor.adaptiveClickable` is the arrow on desktop and the pointing hand on the web | the same: `foundation.IsWeb` decides; step 21.2 added the web branch the port had left out | the web is a target since 1.1 |
| the web text field sends the framework its whole editing state (or deltas), and the framework takes the platform's word for the selection; the framework's text shortcuts are off on the web (`kIsWeb`) and the browser edits | the hidden field sends `ui.TextInputEvent` (the text change and the composing text) and the framework keeps the caret and the selection and its shortcuts (§23.5); only the paste shortcut is left to the browser | one contract for every platform's input method; the Linux bindings are the only ones ported |
| the engine's `handleChange` waits for the next `input` or `selectionchange` after a composition ends | `compositionend` reads the element and commits at once | no event is waiting to commit the last composed text |
| a character key that nothing handles is typed by the browser into the hidden field | the framework types it, and `TextInput.HandleUnhandledKey` reports it, so the key's default is prevented | the same typing code as on Linux; no double typing |
| `ButtonStyleButton.build` makes a new `ThemeData` with `copyWith(iconTheme:)` in every build, and the `AnimatedTheme` around the ink compares it field by field with the last one | `ThemeData.copyWithIconTheme` gives the same copy again for an equal icon theme (the last sixteen are kept in the theme they were made from, as `ThemeOf` keeps its localized copies), so the comparison is one comparison of pointers | on the web a full rebuild of the gallery page spent 11% of its time making and comparing these copies (step 21.7) |
| `_RenderCustomClip`, `RenderCustomPaint`, `RenderFlow` and the custom layout boxes compare `runtimeType`s | `foundation.SameRuntimeType`: the same type once pointers are looked through, which is what `RuntimeTypeName(a) == RuntimeTypeName(b)` answered, without building two names with regular expressions | the names cost 10% of a full rebuild on the web |
| `DriverInfoVK::IsKnownBadDriver` reads the PowerVR series only when the vendor is `kPowerVR` | the PowerVR rules apply to the vendor ID `0x1010` (`kImgTec`) | Impeller's `IdentifyVendor` never returns `kPowerVR`, so its PowerVR rules (the Pixel 10 driver, series below CXT) never apply; here they do, as they say (`vulkan.DriverInfo.IsKnownBadDriver`, step 25.5) |
| `InputConnectionAdaptor` and `ListenableEditingState` send the framework the whole editing state or the deltas, and the framework takes the input method's word for the selection; `ImeSyncDeferringInsetsCallback` defers the keyboard's insets until the animation ends | `GlutterInputConnection` sends the whole `Editable` with a number for each edit, `textInput.edit` finds the change against the framework's text (the web's `TextEditor`) and sends a `ui.TextInputEvent`; a selection that only the input method moved is not sent; the framework's state carries the number of the last edit it followed and is ignored by the Java side when a later edit is on its way; the Enter key of the keyboard first commits the composing text; the keyboard's insets follow each step of the animation (`WindowInsetsAnimation.Callback`), holding back the end state until the animation ends | the contract of Linux and the web (§12.10, §23.5); two threads need the number; no AndroidX |
| `SystemChrome`, `HapticFeedback` and `SystemSound` are classes of static methods that send `flutter/platform` channel calls and return `Future<void>` | `services.SystemChrome`, `services.HapticFeedback` and `services.SystemSound` are instances that the binding makes (`WidgetsBinding.SystemChrome()` and so on) over a backend, which `*ui.PlatformDispatcher` is, and reach the engine through `ui.SystemChromeHost`; the methods return nothing and `SystemUiChangeCallback` is `func(bool)`; the desktop and the web hosts do nothing, as in Flutter | no singletons (§7) and no platform channels (SPEC §24.7) |
| `SystemChrome.setSystemUIOverlayStyle` debounces with `scheduleMicrotask` | the dispatcher's `Post` | no microtasks in Go |
| `SystemChrome.setEnabledSystemUIMode(manual)` asserts that the overlays list is not null | no list means no bars shown | Go cannot tell a nil list from an empty one without a new error tag |
| `SystemChrome.restoreSystemUIOverlays` asks the plugin to apply what it remembers | `SystemChrome` remembers the last mode and overlays and sends them again; nothing if no mode was set | `ui.SystemChromeHost` has no restore call |
| `HapticFeedback.successNotification`, `warningNotification` and `errorNotification` (iOS only) | not ported; `ui.HapticFeedbackKind` has `Vibrate`, `LightImpact`, `MediumImpact`, `HeavyImpact` and `SelectionClick`; the pickers of `material` do not give haptic feedback yet | no iOS; the pickers are a follow-up |
| `RenderView._updateSystemChrome` calls the static `SystemChrome.setSystemUIOverlayStyle` | it calls the field `RenderView.OnSystemUIOverlayStyle`, which the view widget sets to the binding's `SystemChrome` | `rendering` does not know the binding |
| `PlatformConfiguration.configurationId` makes every update of the configuration a change, and `scaleFontSize` asks the platform synchronously | `PlatformConfiguration.ScaleFontSize` (a function) does; a configuration with one counts as a change of the text scale (functions cannot be compared); a negative result falls back to the linear scale; the asserts on a negative or non-finite size are not ported; a change of `AlwaysUse24HourFormat` alone calls only `OnPlatformConfigurationChanged`, so `MediaQuery` does not rebuild for it, as in Flutter | no platform channel; the result of the Android call is remembered until the settings change |
| Skia parses `fonts.xml` with libexpat, with the "lmp" parser (version 21 and later) or the "jb" parser (older) | Go's `encoding/xml`; every `familyset` and `fonts-modification` file uses the lmp handlers, and the pre-Android 5 format (`nameset`/`fileset`, `fallback_fonts-XX.xml`, `system_fonts.xml`) is not read; an entity declaration or malformed XML fails the file with `AndroidFontsXMLInvalid` | no cgo and the standard library only; Glutter supports Android 8 (API 26) and later |
| Skia builds `SkTypeface`s by opening each font file and takes the weight and slant from the font when `fonts.xml` gives none | no typefaces: the matcher reads only `fonts.xml` and returns `text.FontFile`s; a `<font>` with no `weight` is 400 and style `auto` is upright; a font whose file does not exist is dropped, and a family left with no font with its aliases | reading every font at the start is slow, and `text.Collection` loads faces itself |
| Skia checks glyph coverage with `unicharToGlyph` on the typeface | `Fallback` asks an injected `Options.HasGlyph`; the default reads the cmap with go-text and caches it per file and index | the same check without Skia objects; tests need no real fonts |
| Skia's `matchStyleCSS3` (weights, slants and widths) | reimplemented from CSS Fonts 3 (`src/core/SkFontMgr.cpp` is not in the sparse `reference/skia`); widths are ignored (Android's are all normal), and there is no oblique | not in the reference checkout; the same intent |
| Skia's `SkLanguage` matching is `startsWith`, tries the tag and its parents, and splits `lang` on whitespace | matching ends at a hyphen (`ja` does not match `jam`) and is case-insensitive; a comma also separates languages (Android 8 to 10 write `zh-Hant,zh-Bopo`); the script is inferred when the tag has none (`zh-TW` becomes `zh-Hant-TW`, `ja` becomes `ja-Jpan`) | without it `zh-TW` and `zh-CN` would never match `fonts.xml`'s `zh-Hant` and `zh-Hans`; this is what Minikin does |
| `onMatchFamilyStyleCharacter` takes `bcp47[]` and iterates from its last element | one tag, or a list in priority order, from `lang` (the first is the highest), and `Options.Locale` when `lang` is empty | `text.Matcher` passes one BCP 47 string |
| Skia answers one character with one typeface per request | `Match` returns the whole ordered chain (the named family first, then Skia's step order) and `Fallback` is the per-character form | the layout checks the candidates in order and asks `Fallback` for the rest |
| Skia's `fallbackFamilies` hash map has no defined order | the families made by `fallbackFor` keep file order | deterministic |
| Skia resolves the font files of a customization file against `$ANDROID_ROOT/fonts/` | against `/<partition>/fonts` for `/<partition>/etc/fonts_customization.xml`; like Skia, `customizationType="append-to-existing"` and `supportedAxes` are ignored | that is where the partition's fonts are |
| Skia's duplicate-axis check compares against all but the last axis | against all the previous axes | Skia's loop bound looks like a bug; the axes are not used for matching |
| Flutter on Android leaves the family of the default text style to Skia's font manager and `SkFontMgr` `SkTypeface`s | the engine's `text.Collection` gets `androidfonts.Matcher` as its `Matcher`, so go-text's `fontscan` (and its cache directory) is not used on Android | the matcher knows the system's fonts from `fonts.xml`; scanning `/system/fonts` would lose the aliases and the fallback order |
| `AccessibilityBridge.updateSemantics` takes a `ByteBuffer`, a `String[]` and a `ByteBuffer[]` from the engine | one versioned, bounded, little-endian byte array (command 16) with the strings inline and UTF-16 offsets (`accessibility_wire.go`, mirrored in `GlutterAccessibility.java`, with golden bytes in both); the Java side drops a bad payload and logs it | there is no engine-side encoder, and the main thread must not crash on bad input |
| The bridge works out the `AccessibilityNodeInfo` fields on the main thread from the semantics node | Go works out the class name, the properties, the texts as styled pieces, the actions, the range and the collection shape (`newAndroidNode`); Java keeps the tree and does the geometry, the hit test, the focus-dependent parts, the predicted state, the events and `performAction` | most of the logic is plain Go and table-tested on Linux, and the main thread never waits for the UI thread |
| Text offsets and `StringAttribute` ranges are UTF-16 in the bridge | the framework's UTF-8 byte offsets are converted at the edge: in Go for the node data, in Java for the arguments of actions | the rule for text offsets |
| Platform views are mirrored through `AccessibilityViewEmbedder` | not supported | Android has no platform views in 1.6 |
| `SemanticsService` announce and tooltip events, `setLocale` and the `didGainFocus` message | not sent: `semantics.SemanticsOwnerOpts.OnSendEvent` is not wired to the platform, so no `ANNOUNCEMENT` event is made (a live region's change is a `WINDOW_CONTENT_CHANGED` event, as in Flutter) | the framework does not send them to the platform yet |
| `ACTION_FOCUS` is not handled | handled when the node has the focus action (not advertised) | the dev event `a11y:focus` and the services that use it need it |
| Custom actions are added twice to the node's list, and the tap and long press overrides are never cleared | each is added once and the overrides are rebuilt on each update | bugs of Flutter's code |
| `AccessibilityFeatures` come from the bridge's flag bits | `platform.Settings.AccessibleNavigation`, set from the touch exploration state | the platform interface carries the other features already |
| A notification's icon is a theme name or a file (Linux), a URL (web) | ignored on Android: the notification wears the app's icon (the system's fallback icon when the app has none) | the notification manager cannot load a path or a URL, and Android notifications always show the app's icon |
| `BodyMarkup`, Category and AppName of a notification | the tags are stripped and the entities read, as on the web (`web.StripMarkup`); Category and AppName are ignored | notification text is plain, the system names the app, and channels take the place of categories |
| Urgency is a hint (Linux) or `silent` and `requireInteraction` (web) | one channel per urgency (`glutter_low` silent, `glutter_normal`, `glutter_critical` heads-up), created when first used; the user can change a channel in Settings | the importance of a notification belongs to its channel |
| A Persistent or Critical notification stays until the user acts | it has no timeout, and it is not ongoing, so the user can still swipe it away | an ongoing notification could not be dismissed |
| `Timeout` of a notification | a Go timer closes it as `Expired`, and `setTimeoutAfter(timeout + 2 s)` is a backstop for a frozen process | the system gives no callback when a notification times out |
| Notification actions are buttons, the click on the body is `"default"` | the same: `"default"` is the body's click (only when the app listed it), the other actions are buttons (Android shows at most three), and a click or a button cancels the notification and reports `Dismissed` | the system drops buttons beyond three |
| The close reasons of the Desktop Notifications specification | `Dismissed` (swiped, cleared, or clicked), `Expired` (the timer) and `ByApp` (`CloseNotification`); `Unknown` is not sent | Android reports only a swipe, through the delete intent |
| The permission is asked once per decision (web) | a refusal is remembered for the run and not asked again, an undecided prompt (interrupted, no activity) is asked again, and the system's own status is read first, so a grant made later in Settings works | after two refusals Android stops showing the prompt by itself |
| The tray and the file dialogs use D-Bus (Linux) | `TrayUnavailable` and `FileChooserUnavailable` whose cause is `DBusUnavailableOnAndroid`; D-Bus is never tried | the Storage Access Framework gives `content:` URIs, not paths, and Android has no session bus |
| `StretchEffect` and `_StretchOverscrollEffect` use `ui.ImageFilter.isShaderFilterSupported` and `ImageFilter.shader` | always the matrix transform of Flutter's fallback (`Transform` with `diagonal3Values`, `FilterQuality.medium` while the strength is not 0); there is no fragment-shader path | custom fragment shaders are a non-goal (§2); the stretch on screen is therefore the small scale of the fallback |
| `OverscrollIndicatorNotification.leading`, `paintOffset` and `accepted` (`@protected`, `@visibleForTesting`) | `Leading` and `PaintOffset` are exported fields; `accepted` is `Accepted()` and `DisallowIndicator()` sets it | Go has no protected members |
| `_StretchController.animate(...).whenComplete` runs in a microtask | the `TickerFuture` completes inside the controller's tick, so the callback is posted with `WidgetsBinding.Post` and checks that its controller is still the current one | no microtasks in Go (the `Future` rows) |
| the `assert(notification.dragDetails == null)` of the glow and the stretch indicators and `StretchEffect`'s `assert(stretchStrength >= -1 && <= 1)` | the tags `GlowingOverscrollVelocityWithDrag`, `StretchingOverscrollVelocityWithDrag` and `StretchEffectStrengthInvalid` | §12.9 |
| the Dart tests of the overscroll indicators use the `paints` matcher and Ahem text | the `paints` matcher is emulated by recording the foreground painter and matching the calls in order; `translate(y: 0)` is not recorded by `ui.Canvas`; the text is Roboto 14 with a 16 px line, so 'Stretch limit' scales 52.63178407049861 by 54/51 and compares with a 1e-4 tolerance (the `Matrix4` is float32); the goldens are made by Glutter's soft renderer; `TestScrollBehavior1` and `TestScrollBehavior2` are two Go types, because `ScrollConfiguration` notifies its dependents only when the type of the behavior changes | test-only differences |
| `EditableText.magnifierConfiguration` and `TextField`'s, `SelectableRegion`'s and `SelectionArea`'s | `MagnifierConfiguration` is a `*TextMagnifierConfiguration` (nil is `TextMagnifierConfigurationDisabled` for `EditableText` and `SelectableRegion`, and `TextMagnifierAdaptiveMagnifierConfiguration()` for `TextField` and `SelectionArea`; compared by identity, as Dart's has no `==`; `TextFormField` takes it through `TextFieldOpts`); `EditableTextState` has `ShowMagnifier(position)` and `HideMagnifier()` | no value equality for configurations in Dart |
| `MagnifierController`, `MagnifierInfo`, `TextMagnifierConfiguration`, `MagnifierDecoration`, `RawMagnifier`, `TextMagnifier`, `Magnifier` | `NewMagnifierController(opts)`; `Show(bc, builder, opts)` and `Hide(opts)` return a `*scheduler.TickerFuture` where Dart returns a `Future<void>` (`Hide` never completes if the reverse animation is cancelled, as in Dart); `removeFromOverlay: false` is `KeepInOverlay`; `animationController` has `AnimationController()` and `SetAnimationController()`; `MagnifierController.shiftWithinBounds` is `MagnifierControllerShiftWithinBounds`; `MagnifierInfo` is a struct with exported fields (`NewMagnifierInfo(position, caret, field, line)`) and `MagnifierInfo.empty` is `MagnifierInfoEmpty`; `shouldDisplayHandlesInMagnifier: false` is `HideHandlesInMagnifier: true` and `TextMagnifierConfiguration.disabled` is the read-only `TextMagnifierConfigurationDisabled`; `RawMagnifier`'s `magnificationScale` and `MagnifierDecoration`'s `opacity` are `Opt[float64]` and an unset `clipBehavior` is `ClipNone`; `TextMagnifier.adaptiveMagnifierConfiguration` is the function `TextMagnifierAdaptiveMagnifierConfiguration()` (read on each call, Dart reads the platform once); `Magnifier.kDefaultMagnifierSize` and `kStandardVerticalFocalPointShift` are `MagnifierDefaultMagnifierSize` and `MagnifierStandardVerticalFocalPointShift`; `Magnifier`'s `shadows` is nil for the default and an empty slice for none; the tags are `MagnifierRectLargerThanBounds`, `RawMagnifierScaleZero` and `TextMagnifierPositionNotSet`. **Deviation:** there is no `CupertinoTextMagnifier`, so the adaptive configuration builds nothing on iOS (it keeps Flutter's flag that shows the handles on iOS) | no Cupertino library; no global mutable state |
| a `BackdropFilter` whose filter is an `ImageFilter.matrix` (the magnifier's) reads the pixels it moves from, which can lie outside the layer: Skia reads a backdrop through the filter's reverse bounds | `gpu.BackdropMatrix` gives the source rectangle (the layer's rectangle moved back through the filter, plus one pixel for the bilinear taps, within the parent layer); `gles` and `explicit` copy that rectangle and the matrix pass reads from the corner of the copy; `soft` filters the whole surface under the layer; any other backdrop filter still reads the layer's rectangle; a backdrop filter inside an opacity layer reads only that layer, in all three renderers | the magnifier looks at pixels below the lens |
| the Dart toolbar tests use Ahem text and the default platform Android | the tests run on Android and load Go Mono as the default font (`sans-serif`); the adaptive overflow case uses a 500 wide view, the 55-character handle tests a 300 wide field, and the bottom inset is 500/3 (the pixel ratio is 1) | the test engine has no system fonts |
| `PredictiveBackEvent.fromMap(Map)`, `SwipeEdge` as left and right at 0 and 1, asserts and a `RangeError` for a bad event | `services.NewPredictiveBackEventFromUI(ui.BackGestureEvent)`, `SwipeEdgeUnspecified` as the zero value, an immutable struct with accessors and `Equal`; the asserts panic with the tags `PredictiveBackProgressOutOfRange` and `PredictiveBackSwipeEdgeInvalid`; the binding reports a bad event through the error reporter and does not take the gesture (Flutter throws into the channel handler) | no channel: the engine passes a struct; the enum's zero value is `Unspecified` (§12) |
| The `flutter/backgesture`, `flutter/navigation` and `flutter/platform` channels carry `startBackGesture`, `updateBackGestureProgress`, `commitBackGesture`, `cancelBackGesture`, `popRoute`, `memoryPressure` and `setFrameworkHandlesBack`; `handlePopRoute` and `_handleCommitBackGesture` return a `Future` | `ui.PlatformDispatcher` callbacks (`OnPopRoute`, `OnStartBackGesture`, `OnUpdateBackGestureProgress`, `OnCommitBackGesture`, `OnCancelBackGesture`, `OnMemoryPressure`) and the host interface `ui.BackHost` (`SetFrameworkHandlesBack`); `HandlePopRoute` and `HandleCommitBackGesture` run at once | no channels, no `Future` (§12) |
| `BackEvent.EDGE_NONE` (a back from a button or a key) reaches the framework as a swipe edge that is neither left nor right | `internal/platform/android` drops the start and the progress of such a back; the commit that follows is a plain pop (`HandleCommitBackGesture` with no observer that took the start) | Android starts a key back as a gesture with nothing to animate; the framework's `SwipeEdge` has two values |
| `SystemTextScaler.==` compares the factors | the same, through the optional method `EqualTextScaler(TextScaler) bool` that `painting.TextScalerEqual` asks first; the asymmetry of Flutter is kept (`linear(1)` is not equal to a system scaler when it is on the left) | `==` cannot be expressed from `widgets` otherwise |
| `AccessibilityBridge` turns animations off when `TRANSITION_ANIMATION_SCALE` is 0, and `PlatformPlugin` and `FlutterView` use AndroidX | animations are also off when `ANIMATOR_DURATION_SCALE` is 0 (SPEC §24.7); the platform's own `WindowInsetsController` is used, and below API 30 the old flags; `ZeroSides` of `FlutterView.calculateShouldZeroSides` is not ported (below API 30 the side padding of the navigation bar stays in landscape) | the animator scale is what the developer option and "remove animations" set; no AndroidX in the embedding |
| `AndroidTouchProcessor.java` ignores the historical samples of an `ACTION_MOVE` | each pointer of each historical sample is a move of its own, with the sample's time and position, before the current sample's data | a fast swipe that Android batches into one event keeps its path, and the velocity of a fling is measured on all of it |
| `PointerDataPacketConverter` asks a delegate whether a view exists | `viewExists`, a function that is nil (every view exists) while the platform has only the implicit view; the `FML_DCHECK`s are not logged and a change in a state they forbid is handled as the release build handles it | no delegate type, and a debug assert is not a failure in a Go program |
| `KeyEmbedderResponder` and `KeyboardManager` throw `AssertionError` for a key action other than down or up, for a pressed key pressed again, and for a released key released again | an unknown action is ignored, and the other two just set or remove the record | the invariants hold by construction; an app never crashes for a key event |
| `KeyboardManager.CharacterCombiner` works on Java `char`s and truncates a code point above U+FFFF; dead keys are composed with `java.text.Normalizer` | the whole code point is kept; `deadchar.go` has the compositions of the Latin, Greek and Cyrillic letters and the additional Latin letters of Unicode 16 | Go has no `Normalizer` in the standard library and the module does not depend on `golang.org/x/text` |

### 12.11 One import for apps

Flutter's `material.dart` re-exports `widgets.dart`, which exports all of `animation.dart` and `painting.dart` and the names that its libraries re-export from `foundation`, `rendering`, `gestures`, `services`, `physics`, `scheduler` and `dart:ui`. Glutter gets the same effect with `cmd/glutter-gen reexport` (run it in the root of the module; `go generate ./material` does), which type-checks those packages and writes `material/widgets_reexport_gen.go`:
- type aliases (generic aliases included)
- thin wrapper functions (generic ones too), which the compiler inlines
- the constants and the variables, copied (a read-only value such as `Curves` or `Colors` is a copy; a variable of a basic type is not copied, as a copy would not follow the writes to the original, and the file says which ones)

The packages are re-exported whole (`widgets`, `animation`, `painting`) or by the names that Flutter exports (`DefaultSpecs` in `cmd/glutter-gen/internal/reexport`): a Go identifier belongs to a name when it is that name, or starts with it (optionally after `New`) and goes on with a capital letter or a digit, so that the constructors, the options and the functions of a class come with it. `foundation.Opt`, `Some` and `None` are re-exported too, as the options structs need them. When two packages have an identifier of the same name the first one wins (`widgets`, `animation`, `painting`, then the others), and an identifier that package `material` declares itself is not re-exported (the file lists them). A function whose signature has a type that another package cannot name (an unexported type, or a type of an `internal` package) is left out.

Apps import only `m "github.com/rm4n0s/glutter/material"`. The widgets layer itself never imports `material`.

Apps import only `m "github.com/rm4n0s/glutter/material"`. The widgets layer itself never imports `material`.

### 12.12 Reference example

This is `examples/counter/main.go` without its header comment and without the code of `GLUTTER_DEV_TAPS` (`InitState` and `devTaps`, which start the counter at that number in debug builds, for the proof screenshot). It differs from the first draft of this section in the names that Glutter's API actually has: `m.Colors.Blue.Primary`, `m.Icons.Add()` (§12.10) and the `TextTheme.HeadlineMedium` field. It also sets the window and turns the debug banner off, so the screenshot shows the page only.

```go
package main

import (
	"log"
	"strconv"

	m "github.com/rm4n0s/glutter/material"
)

func main() {
	err := m.RunApp(NewMyApp(), m.RunAppOpts{
		AppID:  "dev.glutter.counter",
		Window: m.WindowConfig{Title: "Counter", Size: m.Size{Width: 480, Height: 640}},
	})
	if err != nil {
		log.Fatal(err)
	}
}

type MyApp struct{ m.StatelessWidget }

func NewMyApp() *MyApp { return &MyApp{} }

func (a *MyApp) Build(bc m.BuildContext) m.Widget {
	return m.NewMaterialApp(m.MaterialAppOpts{
		Title:                      "Counter",
		Theme:                      m.NewThemeData(m.ThemeDataOpts{ColorSchemeSeed: m.Some(m.Colors.Blue.Primary)}),
		DebugShowCheckedModeBanner: m.Some(false),
		Home:                       NewCounterPage("Counter"),
	})
}

type CounterPage struct {
	m.StatefulWidget
	title string
}

func NewCounterPage(title string, opts ...m.WidgetOpts) *CounterPage {
	return &CounterPage{StatefulWidget: m.NewStatefulWidgetBase(opts...), title: title}
}

func (p *CounterPage) Title() string        { return p.title }
func (p *CounterPage) CreateState() m.State { return &counterPageState{} }

type counterPageState struct {
	m.StateOf[*CounterPage]
	count int
}

func (s *counterPageState) increment() { s.SetState(func() { s.count++ }) }

func (s *counterPageState) Build(bc m.BuildContext) m.Widget {
	return m.NewScaffold(m.ScaffoldOpts{
		AppBar: m.NewAppBar(m.AppBarOpts{Title: m.NewText(s.Widget().Title())}),
		Body: m.NewCenter(
			m.NewColumn([]m.Widget{
				m.NewText("You have pushed the button this many times:"),
				m.NewText(strconv.Itoa(s.count), m.TextOpts{Style: m.ThemeOf(bc).TextTheme.HeadlineMedium}),
			}, m.ColumnOpts{MainAxisAlignment: m.MainAxisAlignmentCenter}),
		),
		FloatingActionButton: m.NewFloatingActionButton(s.increment, m.NewIcon(m.Icons.Add()),
			m.FloatingActionButtonOpts{Tooltip: "Increment"}),
	})
}
```

---

### 12.13 The API check

`go run ./cmd/glutter-gen apicheck -allow cmd/glutter-gen/apicheck.allow` reads the public packages (`foundation` to `material`, `ui`, `desktop` and `gluttertest`) and reports what breaks §12:

| Rule | What it checks |
|---|---|
| `ctor-shape` | an exported `NewX` takes no `XOpts` value except as its trailing variadic parameter (§12.1) |
| `ctor-order` | `child`, `children`, `sliver` and `slivers` are the last positional parameters (§12.2) |
| `ctor-size` | a constructor has at most 6 required parameters (§12.1) |
| `doc-ref` | the doc comment of an exported type or function ends with a `Flutter:` (`Flutter: no counterpart` included) or `Glutter-specific:` paragraph |
| `enum-zero` | an integer type declared as `T = iota, ...` starts with `<T>Unspecified` (§12.3); `1 << iota` flag types are not enums in this sense |
| `mutable-var` | an exported package variable is not a map, slice, function, channel or basic value, and no statement of its package assigns to it (§ "No global mutable state" of CLAUDE.md) |

`cmd/glutter-gen/apicheck.allow` lists the violations that §12.10 accepts, one `rule pkg.Name` per line with the reason as a comment; a line that no longer matches fails the check. `TestTheModuleKeepsTheConventions` in `cmd/glutter-gen/internal/apicheck` runs it on the module, so `go test ./...` keeps the API in line.

## 13. Threading and lifecycle

- `m.RunApp(app Widget, opts ...RunAppOpts) error` must run on the main goroutine (§8.4); `RunWidget` is the same without the default `View`. It returns when the last window closes or after `SystemNavigatorPop`. Environment failures come back as tagged errors (§14.1).
- `RunAppOpts` include:
  - `Assets fs.FS` for the `AssetBundle`
  - `AppID`, `Window` (the first window's config)
  - `Backend` (overrides `GLUTTER_BACKEND`) and `Renderer` (overrides `GLUTTER_RENDERER`, §9.6)
  - `ErrorReporter` and `ErrorWidgetBuilder` (§14.2)
  - `Logger *slog.Logger`
  - `Debug *DebugOptions` (§14.3)
  - `Fonts []FontSource` (1.2): fonts loaded before the first frame, each from `Data` bytes or from an `Asset` key of `Assets`, under `Family` (§10)
- The UI thread owns the element tree, render tree, `ChangeNotifier`s, controllers and GL. From other goroutines, only these are safe:
  - `State.SetState`
  - `WidgetsBindingOf(bc).Post(fn)`
  - the `desktop` service calls (they take a `context.Context` and deliver results with `Post`)
- `debug` builds detect calls made from the wrong goroutine on the main framework entry points and report them with tag `WrongThread`.
- App lifecycle maps to `AppLifecycleState`:
  - `Resumed`: focused
  - `Inactive`: visible but unfocused
  - `Hidden`: minimized or all windows hidden
  - `Detached`: shutting down
  - `Paused` is not used on desktop, as in Flutter's desktop embedders.
- On the desktop the backends do not report a lifecycle; the engine gives it from the windows, once a window has had the focus or was hidden: `Hidden` when every window is hidden (`SetHidden`, §8.7), `Resumed` when one of them has the focus (it is the active window, §12.10), `Inactive` otherwise. A change of focus changes it after the framework got the view focus events of that change (in the next frame, with the other input), so the focus moving from one window of the app to another is not an `Inactive` in between, and the focus manager does not give back the focus it suspended in the old window. Closing a window counts too: the hidden windows that are left make the app `Hidden`. Hidden stops the frames and the tickers (`SchedulerBinding` disables frames), and showing a window starts them again. A minimized window still counts as visible: Wayland does not say that a window is minimized, and X11's `WindowInfo.Minimized` is not used for the lifecycle yet. A platform that reports a lifecycle itself (the web) is followed instead.
- On the web the UI thread is the main goroutine too; §23.3 says how the browser's callbacks reach it, and the one rule that keeps them from deadlocking.
- On Android (1.6) the UI thread is the OS thread that runs `main`, and Android's main thread is a second thread that only the embedding uses; §24.3 says how they talk and the rule that keeps them from deadlocking. Android reports the lifecycle itself, with `Paused` (§24.8).

## 14. Errors and diagnostics

### 14.1 Environment errors

These are returned from `RunApp`, image/font loading and `desktop` calls, always with tags from `rm4n0s/errors`. The initial tags are:
- `LibraryLoadFailed`, `NoDisplay`, `BackendUnknown`, `BackendUnavailable`, `WaylandConnectFailed`, `WaylandProtocolMissing`, `WaylandProtocolError`, `X11OpenDisplayFailed`, `IdleInhibitUnavailable`
- `EGLLoadFailed`, `EGLInitFailed`, `EGLNoConfig`, `GLES3Unsupported`, `ShaderCompileFailed`
- `AssetNotFound`, `ImageDecodeFailed`, `FontLoadFailed`
- `DBusConnectFailed`, `TrayUnavailable`, `NotificationsUnavailable`, `FileChooserUnavailable`, `OpenURIUnavailable`; on the web also `DBusUnavailableOnWeb`, `OpenURISchemeBlocked`, `NotificationPermissionAborted`, `WebNotificationFailed` (§23.7)
- `RendererUnknown`, `RendererUnavailable`, `VulkanLoadFailed`, `VulkanUnsupported`, `VulkanNoDevice`, `VulkanSurfaceFailed`, `VulkanSwapchainFailed`, `VulkanDeviceLost`, `GPUOutOfMemory`, `GPUDeviceLost`, `GPUDevicePending`, `ShaderModuleInvalid` (1.1, §9.3–9.6)
- `WebGPUUnavailable`, `WebGPUDeviceFailed`, `WebGPUCanvasNotFound`, `WebGPUSurfaceKindUnsupported`, `WebGPUContextUnavailable`, `WebGPUConfigureFailed`, `WebGPUCallFailed`, `WebGPUReadFailed`, `WebGL2Unavailable`, `HostElementNotFound`, `WindowUnsupported`, `WebConfigInvalid`, `WebClipboardUnavailable`, `DragUnsupported`, `CanvasNotFound`, `Canvas2DUnavailable`, `WebDevEventsInvalid`, `SyntheticCanvasNotFound`, `WebClipboardReadFailed`, `WebClipboardWriteFailed`, `WebClipboardFormatUnsupported`, `SyntheticTextFieldNotFound` (1.1, §23); the web clipboard also uses `ClipboardPrimaryUnavailable` and `ClipboardReadTimeout`
- `MultipleOptions`, `WrongThread`
- `FontDataInvalid`, `FontFamilyMissing`, `FontLoaderReused`, `FontLoadCanceled`, `FontLoadUnsupported`, `FontSourceInvalid` (1.2, §10: loading fonts at run time)

Every tag has a route test.

### 14.2 Programmer errors and panics in app code (D15)

Glutter follows Flutter. Flutter reports failed asserts and exceptions through `FlutterError.onError`, replaces the failing subtree with `ErrorWidget`, and keeps the app running.

- **Signaling**: `Build`, layout, paint and callbacks never return errors. Bugs panic: Go runtime panics, and failed debug checks, which panic with a tagged `*errors.Error` (e.g. `SetStateAfterDispose`).
- **Recovery is done by the library.** App code never writes `recover()`. The framework recovers at the same points where Flutter catches exceptions:

  | Recovery point | Flutter equivalent |
  |---|---|
  | `Build` of every element | `ComponentElement.performRebuild` |
  | layout and paint of every render object | `RenderObject._reportException` |
  | gesture callbacks | `GestureRecognizer.invokeCallback` |
  | `Listenable` and animation listeners | `ChangeNotifier.notifyListeners` |
  | scheduler frame/post-frame callbacks and timers | `SchedulerBinding` |
  | image and font stream listeners | `ImageStreamCompleter` |

- **Reporting**: each recovered panic becomes a tagged error (`BuildPanicked`, `LayoutPanicked`, `PaintPanicked`, `CallbackPanicked`) carrying the panic value, the stack and the element path. A panic that is already a tagged `*errors.Error` (a failed debug check) is reported with its own tag. It goes to `RunAppOpts.ErrorReporter func(error)`, which the binding passes to every part of the framework (§12.10). A part that has no reporter does not recover. The default reporter logs the error through the app's `slog.Logger` as the attribute `"err"` (§14.4), so that the entry has the tag, route, metadata and stack of the error. An app can forward errors to its own service, or call `os.Exit` to crash instead.
- **Display**: the failing subtree is replaced by `RunAppOpts.ErrorWidgetBuilder func(error) Widget`. The default is Flutter's: a red box with the message in debug builds, a plain gray box in release builds.
- **Goroutines**: Go can't catch a panic in a goroutine started with a bare `go` statement, so it crashes the program. Use `State.Go(func(ctx context.Context))`, which runs the function with the state's `Lifetime()` context under the same recovery and reporting.
- **Tests**: `gluttertest` fails a test on any reported error unless the test takes it with `tester.TakeError()`, Flutter's `takeException()`.
- **Constructors** never return errors or error widgets (D1).

### 14.3 Debugging aids

- Flags in `rendering`/`widgets`: `DebugPaintSizeEnabled`, `DebugPaintBaselinesEnabled`, `DebugPaintLayerBordersEnabled`, `DebugRepaintRainbowEnabled`, `DebugPrintRebuildDirtyWidgets`, `DebugPrintLayouts`, `DebugProfileBuildsEnabled`. These are fields of a `DebugOptions` value passed through `RunAppOpts`, not global variables.
- `DebugDumpApp`, `DebugDumpRenderTree`, `DebugDumpLayerTree`, `DebugDumpSemanticsTree`, `DebugDumpFocusTree`.
- A performance overlay (frame build and raster time graphs): `MaterialApp.ShowPerformanceOverlay` or the `PerformanceOverlay` widget, drawn from the frame timings that the engine reports.
- `runtime/trace` regions around the frame phases.

### 14.4 How errors are made (`rm4n0s/errors` v0.2.0)

Every error of the module is made, returned, logged and tested the way `AI_GUIDE.md` of `github.com/rm4n0s/errors` says (D9). The project owner requires it. The guide is in the module cache (`$(go env GOMODCACHE)/github.com/rm4n0s/errors@v0.2.0/AI_GUIDE.md`) and is read in full before error code is written (IMPLEMENTATION.md, "Errors"). The rules, with the guide's numbers:

| Rule | What the code does | Why |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `errors.New(tag, message, metadata...)` always has a tag and a message | the tag names the one thing a function cannot do; tests and callers match on it |
| R2 | the metadata is one list of `"key", value` pairs with string keys: given to `errors.New`, or in exactly one `SetMetadata` after `NewErr` and `NewErrf`; a later `SetMetadata` only adds data that becomes known in another function | a key that is not a string is stored as `arg<index>` and a missing value as `nil`; both are bugs |
| R3 | an error is tagged once, where it first appears. An error of this module goes up as it is (`return err`). An error from outside it (standard library, purego, godbus, go-text, `x/image`) is tagged where it first appears with `NewErr`. A failure that the code finds itself is `errors.New` | a second tag or a `fmt.Errorf` hides the first tag and cuts the route; `errors.FromError` is a plain type assertion |
| R4 | the constructor is called in the function that fails, not in a helper that builds the error | the route is the call stack at the constructor: a helper adds a frame that is not the failing function |
| R5 | a tag is a constant next to the code that produces it; code compares `Tag` with that constant or uses `errors.Is` with a sentinel (`&errors.Error{Tag: TagX}`), never a string literal or the message; no package-level `errors.New` | messages are for people and change; a package-level `New` has the route of `init` |
| R6 | only `github.com/rm4n0s/errors` is imported (it re-exports `Is`, `As` and `Unwrap`); the standard library's package, when `Join` is needed, is imported as `stdErrors` | one import, one behavior |
| Logging | an error is logged with `slog` as `"err", err`; `*errors.Error` is a `slog.LogValuer` (keys `Tag`, `Message`, `Route`, `OriginalErr`, `Metadata`, `StackFrames`), and the entry unmarshals into `errors.ErrorJson` and replays with `errors.FromJson`. `ToJson` and `json.Marshal` are not used to log | the route in the entry is cut at the function that logs |
| Tests | each tag has a test with `HasRoute("Outer->Inner.Tag")` (from the entry function that the test calls, with the tag), and the metadata where it matters | the test shows which path made the error |

A tag that is built at run time from a prefix (a widget's name in a debug check, `prefix + "Negative"`) stays an expression; `errcheck.allow` lists those places. A table that turns a C error code into a tag with a switch (`vkError`) is a function that returns an error and is called from many places; it is the one accepted exception to R4 and is listed in `errcheck.allow` with that reason. An exported function or method is never a helper in the sense of R4 (it is API, or it implements an interface, and its whole job can be to fail, such as an abstract method that panics). Package `desktop` gives the public tag of a service (`NotificationsUnavailable`, `TrayUnavailable`, `FileChooserUnavailable`, `TrayRegisterFailed`, §14.1) to the error of a bus that nobody serves, keeping the bus's own error as the cause; `errcheck.allow` lists those places as the one accepted exception to R3, because an app matches on the public tags.

`go run ./cmd/glutter-gen errcheck -allow cmd/glutter-gen/errcheck.allow` reads every Go file of the module (the Linux build, the `js/wasm` build and the files with the `glutter_display` and `glutter_browser` tags, tests included) with `go/types` and reports:

| Rule name | What it checks |
|---|---|
| `std-errors` | the standard library's `errors` is imported without an alias (R6) |
| `fmt-errorf` | `fmt.Errorf` is called (R3) |
| `metadata-chain` | `SetMetadata` follows `errors.New`, or another `SetMetadata` (R2) |
| `metadata-pairs` | the metadata has an odd number of values, or a key that is a non-string literal (R2) |
| `sentinel-ctor` | a package-level variable is made by `errors.New`, `NewErr` or `NewErrf` (R5) |
| `error-helper` | a function only builds an error and returns it, or panics with it (R4) |
| `error-rewrap` | `NewErr` or `NewErrf` wraps an error that came from a function of the module (R3) |
| `error-untagged` | an error that came from outside the module is returned as it is (R3) |
| `tag-branch` | in code that is not a test, `Tag`, `Message` or `Error()` is compared with a string literal, searched with `strings`, or switched on with string literal cases (R5). Tests are not checked: a test pins the string that is the contract (a public tag, or the text of a message that a ported Flutter test asserts) |
| `tag-literal` | the tag given to `New`, `NewErr` or `NewErrf` is a string literal, not a constant (R5). The constants are unexported (`tagX`) and declared at the end of the file that makes the error; a constant that another package matches on is exported (`TagX`, with a doc comment) from the package that makes it, and the others use it |
| `error-tojson` | `ToJson` is called outside a test (logging) |

`cmd/glutter-gen/errcheck.allow` lists the accepted violations as `rule file:Func` with the reason as a comment; a line that no longer matches fails the check. `TestTheModuleKeepsTheErrorRules` in `cmd/glutter-gen/internal/errcheck` runs it on the module, so `go test ./...` keeps the rules.

---

## 15. Performance targets

These are measured on a 2020 laptop with an Intel Iris Xe or similar GPU and Mesa, at 1920×1080 and a device pixel ratio of 1.25.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Idle CPU (no animation, no input) | ~0%: no frames scheduled, no timers armed |
| Frame rate (gallery screen, ~500 visible widgets, one animation) | the display's refresh rate with no dropped frames |
| UI thread CPU per frame, animation-only frame (no rebuild) | < 2 ms |
| UI thread CPU per frame, full rebuild of the gallery screen | < 8 ms |
| Steady-state allocations, animation-only frame | ≤ 1 KB per frame |
| Cold start to first frame | < 150 ms (fonts cached) |
| Resident memory, counter example | < 60 MB |

Benchmarks (`go test -bench`) in `rendering`, `widgets`, `examples/gallery` and `internal/gpu/soft`, and `scripts/perf.sh`, track these numbers; the targets are for release builds (`-tags glutter_release`). `docs/PERFORMANCE.md` has the machine, the numbers and the exceptions. Regressions above 10% need a justification.

**Vulkan** (1.1) meets the same targets on the same machine, and no benchmark is more than 10% slower with Vulkan than with GLES.

**Web** (1.1), measured in Chromium (WebGPU) and Firefox (WebGL 2) on the same machine, release builds made by `glutter-web build -release`:

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Idle CPU | ~0%: no `requestAnimationFrame` asked for while nothing is scheduled |
| Frame rate (gallery screen, one animation) | the display's refresh rate with no dropped frames |
| UI thread CPU per frame, animation-only frame | < 4 ms |
| UI thread CPU per frame, full rebuild of the gallery screen | < 16 ms |
| Size of `app.wasm.gz`, counter / gallery | < 4 MB / < 5 MB (on 2026-10-21, without the web platform and renderers: 2.3 MB / 2.4 MB) |
| Start to first frame, `app.wasm` in the browser's cache | < 1 s |

**Android** (1.6), release builds made by `glutter-android build -release` (a debug build is far slower: its frames are 5 to 10 times longer), measured with `scripts/perf-android.sh` (`glutter-android perf`, §24.13). Proposed in step 26.5 from the emulator (an API 37 x86_64 image: the host's CPU, and a GPU that is SwiftShader, a software Vulkan, so a frame's GPU time is not a phone's) and confirmed or changed on the owner's phone (`docs/MANUAL-CHECKS.md`, 26.5):

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Idle CPU (counter, no input) | ~0%: no frames scheduled, no timers armed (0 CPU ticks in 10 s) |
| Frame rate (gallery data page, one animation) | the display's refresh rate with no dropped frames (the phone only: the emulator's GPU takes about 1.5 s for a frame of the gallery) |
| UI thread build time per frame, animation-only frame | < 4 ms |
| UI thread build time per frame, full rebuild of the gallery page | < 16 ms |
| Cold start to first frame (`first frame presented since_run`, software renderer) | < 1 s |
| APK size per ABI, counter / gallery | < 30 MiB / < 42 MiB |
| Memory (`dumpsys meminfo` TOTAL PSS), counter | < 100 MB |

`docs/PERFORMANCE.md` has the numbers, what is not the same as on the desktop (the APK holds the program uncompressed, aligned to 16 KB, §24.1) and the exceptions.

## 16. Build and runtime requirements

**Building an app**: Go 1.27+ and nothing else. `CGO_ENABLED=0 go build` produces a binary that loads the system libraries at runtime. It's dynamically linked against glibc's loader, which is how purego reaches `dlopen`. Cross-compiling for linux/amd64 and linux/arm64 from any OS works.

**Building for the web** (1.1): `GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm go build`, or `go run ./cmd/glutter-web build` for a directory a static web server can serve (§23.1). Only the standard Go toolchain is supported (D18).

**Building for Android** (1.6): Go 1.27+, the Android NDK, the SDK's build-tools and one platform, and a Java runtime for `apksigner`; `go run ./cmd/glutter-android build` makes the APK (§24.1). No JDK is needed unless the Java embedding is changed (D22).

**Contributing**: Go 1.27+ plus a C compiler, **only** because `go test -race` requires cgo. When cgo is enabled, purego uses it transparently, so the same code is tested both ways (§17). No `-devel` packages, `wayland-scanner` or `wayland-protocols` are needed.

**Runtime**:
- **Required**:
  - a Wayland compositor with `xdg_wm_base`, or an X server
  - an EGL driver with OpenGL ES 3.0 (Mesa ≥ 20, NVIDIA through glvnd, or llvmpipe)
  - the runtime libraries in §8.1. They're installed on every Linux desktop (Wayland compositors and Xwayland depend on them), and a missing one is reported as a tagged error.
- **Optional**:
  - a D-Bus session bus, which every desktop session has; all the services below use it
  - xdg-desktop-portal plus a backend, for file dialogs, settings and open URI
  - a StatusNotifierItem host, for the tray (KDE, XFCE, Cinnamon, and GNOME with the AppIndicator extension)
  - a notification daemon
  - at-spi2-core, for accessibility
  - a Vulkan driver (§9.4); without one, GLES is used (§9.6)

**Runtime on Android** (1.6): Android 8.0 (API 26) or later, on arm64 or x86_64, with OpenGL ES 3.0; Vulkan 1.1 is used where it is good (§24.4).

**Runtime on the web** (1.1): a browser with WebAssembly and WebGL 2, which every current browser has. WebGPU is used where the browser has it (Chromium-based browsers, Safari, and Firefox where it ships WebGPU).

**Contributing to 1.1** also needs: Node.js (the wasm unit tests, §17), Chromium and Firefox (the browser tests), Mesa's lavapipe and `vulkan-validation-layers` (the Vulkan display tests), and `naga` (`cargo install naga-cli --locked`), only to change a WGSL shader (D20).

**Contributing to 1.6** also needs: the Android SDK command-line tools with `platform-tools`, a platform, `build-tools`, the NDK, the emulator and the two system images of §24.13; a JDK 17 or later, only to change the Java embedding; and, for the Vulkan device tests, the Khronos validation layer for Android. `docs/IMPLEMENTATION.md` ("Tools for M25–M26") has the versions and the commands.

## 17. Testing strategy

- **Unit tests** are table-driven and sit next to the code (CLAUDE.md).
- **Widget tests** (`gluttertest`):
  - `t := gluttertest.New(tb)`, then `PumpWidget`, `Pump(d)`, `PumpAndSettle`, `Tap`, `LongPress`, `Drag`, `Fling`, `Hover`, `Scroll`, `EnterText`, `SendKeyEvent`, `SendKeyDownEvent`/`UpEvent`, `PumpFrames`
  - finders: `ByText`, `ByKey`, `ByType[T]`, `ByIcon`, `ByTooltip`, `BySemanticsLabel`, `Descendant`, `Ancestor`
  - assertions are plain functions returning errors for `t.Error` (`FindsOneWidget`, `FindsNothing`, ...); `MatchesGolden(name)` compares the view with a golden
  - a fake clock drives tickers and timers
  - the `fake` platform scripts windows, input and settings
  - at the end of a test the tree is unmounted (1.7, M27): `New` registers a cleanup that pumps an empty widget, so every `State.Dispose` runs and every `State.Lifetime` is cancelled, as `flutter_test` does after the body of a test. It does nothing when the test failed already (`tb.Failed()`, when `tb` has it), when the framework reported an error that the test did not take, or when no widget was pumped. An error that the unmounting reports fails the test like any other. Cleanups run last in, first out: a cleanup that the test registers after `New` runs before the unmounting and still sees the tree, so an object that the tree uses and that the test disposes (a delegate, a controller) is registered before `New`, to be disposed after the unmounting
  - `gluttertest.Opts.Fonts` (1.2) gives the test engine fonts besides the bundled Roboto and Material Icons: `[]gluttertest.Font{{Family, Data}}`, so that a golden test can show monospace text, or any text, the same on every machine. The test engine has no system fonts
- **Golden tests** compare against PNGs in `testdata/goldens/`, rendered by `internal/gpu/soft`. `-update-goldens` rewrites them.
- **Ported Flutter tests**: for each widget and render object, the relevant cases from Flutter's `packages/flutter/test` are ported as table tests. This is the main way to check fidelity.
- **Platform tests** (`-tags glutter_display`) open real Wayland/X11 windows and GL contexts (they can run under a headless compositor such as `weston --backend=headless` or `Xvfb`), and compare `gles` against `soft`.
- **Wayland compatibility matrix.**
  - Every milestone that touches the platform is checked against:
    - compositors: GNOME/Mutter, KDE/KWin, Sway, Hyprland, Weston and COSMIC
    - drivers: Mesa (Intel, AMD), NVIDIA proprietary, and llvmpipe
    - scales: 100%, 125%, 150% and 200%
    - a two-monitor setup with mixed scales
  - The automated part runs under headless compositors (`weston --backend=headless`, `sway` with `WLR_BACKENDS=headless`, `mutter --headless`, `kwin_wayland --virtual`).
  - The rest is a manual checklist in `docs/IMPLEMENTATION.md` covering every item in §8.11.
- **D-Bus services** are tested against an in-process fake bus that records method calls and emits signals. Real-desktop checks are listed per milestone in `docs/IMPLEMENTATION.md`.
  - The tests of an app that uses package `desktop` call `tester.FakeDesktop()` (`gluttertest`): a notification service, a StatusNotifierWatcher and a file chooser portal on the fake bus. The test reads `Notifications()`, `TrayMenu()` and `TrayProperty`, and acts like the user with `ClickTrayMenuItem`, `ActivateTray`, `InvokeNotificationAction`, `CloseNotification`, `AnswerFileChooser` and `CancelFileChooser`; `DisableFileChooser()` (1.7) takes the portal away, as on a desktop that has none, so that `OpenFiles`, `SaveFile` and `SaveFiles` fail with `FileChooserUnavailable` (a dialog that is open stays open); `WaitFor` runs the UI thread's posted functions while the app's goroutines work.
  - What the fake bus cannot show is tested against a real `dbus-daemon` that a test starts (it is skipped when the daemon is not installed): the types that godbus gives and takes (the layout `(ia{sv}av)`, the pixmaps, the errors of a missing service, which are values and not pointers) and the life of the connection.
- **Both build modes**:
  - `go test -race ./...` runs with cgo (purego uses the real cgo runtime)
  - `CGO_ENABLED=0 go test ./...` runs purego's cgo-free path, which users get
  - both must pass
- **C struct layout tests** for every Go mirror of a C struct (§8.1 rule 3).
- **Fuzz tests** cover the X11/Wayland event decoders, the XKB compose handling, the paragraph layout, and the image and font loaders.
- **Vulkan** display tests (`glutter_display`) run under headless sway, and under Xvfb for X11, with Mesa's lavapipe (`VK_DRIVER_FILES` pointing at `lvp_icd.*.json`) and with validation on (§9.4); a validation error fails the test. Every golden scene is compared against `soft`, as for `gles`.
- **Web unit tests**: `scripts/test-wasm.sh` runs the tests of every package that has no OS tag with `GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm go test` under Node (Go's `go_js_wasm_exec`). It runs them with a minimal environment, because Go's wasm loader limits the size of the environment.
- **Browser tests** (`-tags glutter_browser`): `internal/webtest` builds a wasm test program, serves it with `net/http/httptest`, and starts a headless browser with a temporary profile: Chromium for WebGPU and WebGL 2, Firefox for WebGL 2 and, where its build has it, WebGPU (§9.5). The page posts its results and its read-back PNGs to the server, and the test compares them with the `soft` goldens. No WebDriver or DevTools library is used. Every browser process is killed when its test ends.
- **Android** (1.6): `scripts/check-android.sh` (a standard check: build and vet for `android/arm64` and `android/amd64`), and **device tests** (`-tags glutter_android`) on the emulator through `internal/androidtest`, with Vulkan validation on; manual checks on a real phone where the emulator cannot tell (§24.13).

## 18. Milestones

Each milestone is broken into steps in `docs/IMPLEMENTATION.md` (written after this spec is approved) and follows the workflow in CLAUDE.md.

| # | Milestone | Done when |
|---|---|---|
| M0 | Skeleton: `go.mod`, package layout, `cmd/glutter-gen` stub, `reference/` fetch script, `third_party/flutter/LICENSE` | `go build ./...` and all four checks pass |
| M1 | Platform core: purego library loaders and C struct mirrors, Wayland binding generator, Wayland and X11 window, event loop, EGL, GLES clear, frame callbacks, pointer and keyboard (xkb), fake platform | a window opens and closes and logs input on both backends |
| M2 | `ui` engine + software renderer: geometry, Path, Paint, Canvas, Picture, SceneBuilder, analytic shapes, shadows, gradients, clips, layers, golden harness | goldens for every `Canvas` operation |
| M3 | GLES renderer to parity with `soft`, plus atlases and batching | `gles` matches the `soft` goldens within tolerance |
| M4 | Text: font collection, fontscan, Roboto embedded, Paragraph with every query, glyph atlas | text goldens, including bidi, ellipsis and decorations |
| M5 | foundation, scheduler, physics, gestures (hit testing, arena, every recognizer) | ported Flutter gesture tests pass |
| M6 | rendering core: RenderObject, RenderBox, PipelineOwner, layers, compositing, every box render object | ported Flutter rendering tests pass |
| M7 | widgets core: Element tree, State, keys, GlobalKey, InheritedWidget family, basic widgets, MediaQuery, `RunApp`, `gluttertest` | counter example runs on Linux |
| M8 | animation library, implicit and explicit animations, transitions, Hero | ported animation tests pass |
| M9 | scrolling: Scrollable, physics, ListView/GridView/CustomScrollView, slivers, PageView, NestedScrollView, scrollbars, reorderable lists | ported scrolling tests pass |
| M10 | navigation and focus: Navigator (both APIs), Router, Overlay, OverlayPortal, focus system, Shortcuts/Actions | ported tests pass |
| M11 | text editing: EditableText, selection, Linux shortcuts, undo, IME (text-input-v3 on Wayland; IBus/Fcitx5 over D-Bus on X11), clipboard, SelectableRegion | editing works with IBus and Fcitx5 on both backends |
| M12 | Material foundation: ThemeData, ColorScheme (+ HCT port), Typography, Material, Ink, icons, localizations | theme goldens |
| M13 | Material components, in batches: app structure → actions → inputs → dialogs and pickers → display | gallery example covers §11.2 |
| M14 | desktop windowing: multiple windows, dialog windows, decorations (server-side, libdecor, app-drawn), cursor shapes, fractional scale, settings, drag and drop, every item in §8.11 | multiwindow example; drag and drop with Files/Dolphin; the Wayland matrix (§17) passes |
| M15 | D-Bus services: tray, notifications, file dialogs, open URI | tray example on KDE and GNOME+AppIndicator |
| M16 | accessibility: semantics → AT-SPI2 | gallery usable with Orca |
| M17 | effects and performance: backdrop filter, advanced blend modes, raster cache, performance overlay, benchmarks meeting §15 | targets met |
| M18 | 1.0: documentation, examples, API review against §12 | release |
| M19 | Portability groundwork (1.1): per-OS backend files, the js/wasm build, renderer selection, WGSL toolchain, the explicit device interface | `GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm go build ./...` passes; the WGSL shaders compile to SPIR-V |
| M20 | Vulkan renderer, at parity with `soft`, default on Linux with GLES as the fallback | `vulkan` matches the `soft` goldens; `auto` uses Vulkan on Mesa and NVIDIA and GLES without Vulkan |
| M21 | Web: platform, input, text input, WebGL 2, WebGPU, fallback, `glutter-web`, examples | the examples run in Chromium (WebGPU) and Firefox (WebGL 2) and match the goldens; release 1.1 |
| M22 | Fonts and trees for code editors (1.2): fonts loaded at run time, fonts in `gluttertest`, the family fallback order, then font queries and the desktop's font settings, text benchmarks and an LRU glyph cache, `TreeSliver`, `ReorderableListView`, tab stops in paragraph layout and a cache of shaped runs (1.4) | a monospace font loaded by the app is used in `TextStyle`, in fallback and in goldens; release 1.2 after steps 22.1–22.3; a tab in a code line lands on its stop in every query; a line of code built again is at least 5 times cheaper; release 1.4 after step 22.10 |
| M23 | Windows for tray apps: hiding a window and showing it again (`RegularWindowController.SetHidden`, §8.2, §8.3, §8.7), and the desktop lifecycle given by the windows (§13) | ✕ in the tray example takes the window off the screen and the task bar on X11 and Wayland while the app and its tray icon keep running, a click on the icon brings it back as it was, and `RunApp` ends only when the window closes or the app exits; release 1.5 |
| M24 | Errors to the fullest: `rm4n0s/errors` v0.2.0 used the way its `AI_GUIDE.md` says (§14.4): tags are constants, an error is tagged once where it first appears, constructors are called in the function that fails, errors are logged with `slog` as `"err"`; `glutter-gen errcheck` keeps it | `go run ./cmd/glutter-gen errcheck -allow cmd/glutter-gen/errcheck.allow` reports nothing, and the default error reporter's log entry replays through `errors.FromJson` |
| M25 | Android platform (1.6): the build tags and checks, `glutter-android` and the Java embedding, the platform (looper, surface, frames, lifecycle), `soft`, GLES and Vulkan on Android, input, insets and system UI, text input and the IME, back and lifecycle, fonts, TalkBack, services (§24) | `examples/counter` and `examples/gallery` run on the emulator with Vulkan, GLES and `soft` and match the goldens; typing with the soft keyboard, predictive back and TalkBack work on the owner's phone |
| M26 | Android behaviors of the framework, and 1.6: overscroll indicators, the Material text selection toolbar and magnifier, predictive back transitions, spell check, the examples on Android with their performance (§24.12) | the ported Flutter tests pass; every example but `multiwindow` and `tray` runs on Android; release 1.6 |
| M27 | Fixes for Emetos (1.7): scroll positions after their `Scrollable` is gone, `gluttertest` disposing the tree and a fake desktop without a file chooser, the focus of a window from its activation, what a window controller can do (title, activation, full screen, initial state) and the first window in tests, closing and destroying the first window, `RawTooltip` (delay on enter, callbacks, controller, key), the cursor of a pressed pointer, `Draggable` and `DragTarget` (slop, Escape, moves while resting), `TreeSliver` (changing children, lazy nodes, keys), the cold start (a profile of the first draw, a shader cache) | none of the Emetos workarounds is needed: the program no longer panics when a `Scrollable` leaves the tree, a test's states are disposed, a headless window gets focus events, the first window closes like the others; a second start compiles no shader; release 1.7 |

## 19. Examples (`examples/`)

`counter`, `layout` (Row/Column/Stack/Wrap), `animations`, `scrolling` (lists, grids, slivers), `navigation`, `editor` (text editing, clipboard, IME), `gallery` (all Material components, light/dark), `multiwindow`, `tray` (tray + notifications + file dialog), `custompaint`, `todo` (a DataTable of tasks with Edit and Delete buttons in each row).

On the web (1.1) every example except `multiwindow` and `tray` builds and runs (§23), and on Android (1.6) too (§24). `examples/examples_test.go` builds each of them for `android/arm64` (skipped without an NDK and under `-short`), and a device test (`TestEveryExampleDrawsItsFirstFrame`) starts each on the two emulators and waits for its first frame. In a window narrower than 600 logical pixels (a phone) the gallery keeps its list of pages in a drawer that the app bar's button opens, its sections shrink to the width of the screen and wrap, and the data table scrolls sideways.

**Reference sources.** `reference/flutter` holds a sparse checkout of `packages/flutter/lib` and `packages/flutter/test`, pinned to the latest stable Flutter tag when M0 starts (the tag is recorded in `reference/VERSION`). It is git-ignored and fetched by `scripts/fetch-reference.sh`.
For 1.6 `reference/skia` holds a sparse checkout (`src/ports`, `include/ports`) of Skia at the revision that Flutter's `DEPS` pins (`skia_revision`) at the pinned Flutter commit, recorded in `reference/VERSION`; the Android font manager is ported from it (§24.9) and Skia's BSD-3-Clause license is `third_party/skia/LICENSE`.
For 1.1 the checkout also holds Flutter's web engine, `engine/src/flutter/lib/web_ui`, at the same commit: its input, keyboard and text-editing code is ported for §23 (Flutter's BSD-3-Clause license covers it).

## 21. Open decisions

New ones are added here with a recommendation and moved to §4 once decided.

| Decision | Recommendation |
|---|---|
| File dialogs on Android: the Storage Access Framework gives `content:` URIs, which the path-based `desktop.OpenFiles` and `SaveFile` cannot carry (§24.11) | An API that reads and writes through the URI (`desktop.OpenDocuments` returning readers, `SaveDocument` taking a writer), usable on Linux through the portal too. Decide before a step adds it; 1.6 returns `FileChooserUnavailable` |

## 22. Risks

| Risk | Mitigation |
|---|---|
| Without cgo, purego imitates the cgo runtime (`internal/fakecgo`), which depends on Go runtime internals and can break with a new Go release | Pin the purego version. Run both test modes (§17) on every new Go release before raising the minimum Go version. Ebiten depends on the same mechanism, so breakage is found and fixed upstream quickly |
| A wrong Go mirror of a C struct corrupts memory silently | layout tests (§8.1 rule 3); fuzz tests on event decoding |
| The C→Go callback limit (~2,000 per process) | callbacks are created once at startup and dispatch by user data (§8.1 rule 2); a test counts them |
| C variadic APIs can't be called (XIM, some Xlib calls) | non-variadic variants; IME over D-Bus on X11 |
| purego callbacks can't take structs by value | none of the libraries used need that (checked per library in M1) |
| libdecor's GTK plugin loads GTK 3 into the process (more memory, slower first window) | load it only on compositors without server-side decorations; `DecorationsApp` skips it; measure against §15 in M14 |
| Vulkan drivers differ more than GLES drivers (window-system integration, present modes, synchronization) | validation on in the display tests; `auto` falls back to GLES on any failure before the first frame (§9.6); `GLUTTER_RENDERER=gles` is the user's way out |
| Each call from Go into JavaScript costs about a microsecond | a frame's data goes to the GPU in few large writes (§9.5); `gles` already skips repeated GL state. Measured in step 21.7; if it is still too slow, a frame's GL calls are encoded into one buffer that a small JavaScript function runs |
| Some browsers have no WebGPU (Firefox on Linux, older Safari) | the WebGL 2 fallback, tested in Firefox |
| A browser callback that waits for the UI goroutine deadlocks if the UI goroutine waits for a promise | the rule in §23.3; a browser test sends keys while a clipboard read is pending |
| cgo on Android brings C into the module (D21) | cgo only in the `*_android.go` files of one package, a standard check that builds and vets it, and the Linux and web checks still run with `CGO_ENABLED=0` |
| The emulator translates the GPU and runs x86_64 code, so it hides the bugs of phone drivers (Adreno, Mali) and of arm64 | manual checks on a real phone in every step that renders or takes input; Impeller's driver deny list; `auto` falls back to GLES on any Vulkan failure before the first frame; `GLUTTER_RENDERER=gles` as an intent extra |
| A deadlock between Android's main thread and the UI thread | the rule of §24.3: the UI thread never waits for the main thread, and the main thread waits only in `surfaceDestroyed`, with a time limit |
| Older Android versions have a CBDT emoji font, which Glutter did not draw | drawn from step 25.10 on (§24.9) |
| Go's wasm binaries are large | `-ldflags=-s -w`, gzip made by `glutter-web`, and the size targets in §15 |

## 23. Web (`GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm`, 1.1)

### 23.1 Build and packaging

- Apps build with the standard Go toolchain: `GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm go build -o app.wasm`. TinyGo is not supported (D18).
- `go run ./cmd/glutter-web build [-o dir] [-release] <package>` makes a directory that any static web server can serve:
  - `index.html`: the loader, and `globalThis.glutterConfig` (§23.2)
  - `wasm_exec.js`, copied from `$(go env GOROOT)/lib/wasm` (it must match the Go that built the app)
  - `app.wasm` and `app.wasm.gz`

  `-release` builds with `-tags glutter_release -ldflags=-s -w`.
- `glutter-web serve [-addr :8080] <package>` builds and serves the app, with the type `application/wasm`, and sends the gzip file to a browser that accepts it.
- `glutter-web shot -browser chromium|firefox [-renderer r] -o file.png <package>` saves a screenshot of the app (proof method D, §23.8).
- `glutter-web perf -browser chromium|firefox [-renderer r] [-release] [-query k=v] [-clicks x,y;x,y] <package>` measures the app in a headless browser and prints `key value` lines (§23.8, "Decided in step 21.7"); `scripts/perf-web.sh` runs it for the web table of §15.
- The loader fetches `app.wasm` with `WebAssembly.instantiateStreaming`. It copies the query parameters of the page's URL whose names start with `GLUTTER_` into the Go program's environment, so `?GLUTTER_RENDERER=webgl` works as the environment variable does on Linux. Without WebAssembly it shows a message instead. When the Go program ends with a non-zero exit code (`RunApp` returned an error, such as `WebGPUUnavailable` for `?GLUTTER_RENDERER=webgpu` in a browser without WebGPU, or the program panicked), the loader puts a message into the page, in an element `#glutter-message` fixed over the page: the exit code and the last lines of what the program wrote to standard error that are not Info, Debug or Warn log lines (each cut at 300 characters), so that the user does not have to open the console. A program that ends with code 0 shows nothing.

### 23.2 Page and view

Port from `lib/web_ui/lib/src/engine/view_embedder/` (dimensions, full-page and custom-element embedding).

- One view per page. `globalThis.glutterConfig.hostElement` (a CSS selector, set in `index.html`) names the element the view fills; without it the view fills the page. A selector that matches nothing is `HostElementNotFound`.
- The platform puts a `<canvas>` and the hidden text field (§23.5) into the host. For the full page, the body has no margin and no scroll bars, and the canvas fills the viewport. The canvas has `touch-action: none`.
- The physical size comes from a `ResizeObserver` with `devicePixelContentBoxSize` (exact device pixels), else from `round(CSS size × devicePixelRatio)`. A change of `devicePixelRatio` (zoom, another monitor) is seen through `matchMedia("(resolution: <n>dppx)")`. The canvas's `width` and `height` are set to the physical size before a frame of that size is drawn.
- `WindowConfig.Title` sets `document.title`. Size, size constraints and window states are ignored, except fullscreen (`requestFullscreen` on the host, which the browser allows only during a user action). Hiding the window (`SetHidden`) is ignored too: the canvas is the page's only view, a page cannot take itself off the screen, and the engine keeps drawing it (`platform.Window.SetHidden` returns false). A second `NewWindow` is `WindowUnsupported`.
- `Displays()` is one display from `screen`, with a refresh rate of 60.
- `TargetPlatform` is the browser's OS, found as Flutter web does (port `lib/web_ui/lib/src/engine/browser_detection.dart` and `packages/flutter/lib/src/foundation/_platform_web.dart`). `foundation.IsWeb` is true.

### 23.3 Event loop and threading

- The UI thread is the main goroutine. `RunApp` calls `Platform.Run`, which waits on the platform's queue; `Post` adds to the queue and wakes it. `RunApp` does not return on the web, unless starting fails; `SystemNavigatorPop` and `RequestAppExit` do nothing, because a page cannot close itself.
- The browser's callbacks (`js.FuncOf`) are made once, at startup, and kept for the life of the app, as §8.1 rule 2 does for C. A callback copies what it needs out of the event into Go values (a JavaScript event is only valid during its callback) and queues it. Go's wasm runtime runs every goroutine that is ready before a callback returns to the browser, so the UI goroutine handles the event in the same browser task.
- Frames: `RequestFrame` asks for one `requestAnimationFrame`, and its timestamp is the frame's time. Nothing is asked for while no frame is scheduled. A hidden page gets no animation frames; the app is then `Hidden`.
- The callbacks that need an answer wait for the UI goroutine. `keydown` and `keyup` call `preventDefault` when the framework handled the key, as Flutter web does; `wheel` calls it when a scrollable took the scroll. That is safe only because of this rule: **once `Run` has installed the event listeners, the UI goroutine never waits for a promise.** A promise (a clipboard read, `mapAsync`, a new WebGPU device after a loss) continues in a `then` callback that posts to the UI goroutine. What startup awaits (`requestAdapter`, `requestDevice`) it awaits before the listeners are installed.
- Wasm runs one goroutine at a time. `SetState` and `Post` from other goroutines work as on Linux, and Go's timers become `setTimeout` calls.

### 23.4 Input

Port from `lib/web_ui/lib/src/engine/pointer_binding.dart` (and `pointer_converter.dart`), `keyboard_binding.dart` and `mouse/cursor.dart`, or the files that hold that code at the pinned commit.

- Pointer events (`pointerdown`, `pointermove`, `pointerup`, `pointercancel`, with `setPointerCapture` on down) become `PointerData` of kind mouse, touch or stylus, with buttons, pressure and tilt. Several touches are told apart by `pointerId`.
- `wheel` becomes scroll deltas, converted from lines and pages to pixels as Flutter web converts them. The listener is not passive, so `preventDefault` can keep the page from scrolling (§23.3).
- `keydown` and `keyup` on the focused element become `KeyData`: the physical key from `event.code`, the logical key from `event.key`, through tables that `glutter-gen keys` generates from Flutter's key data (the web names). Modifier and lock keys get the synthesized events Flutter web sends.
- `MouseCursor` becomes CSS `cursor` names, mapped as Flutter web maps them.
- Focus: the host is focusable (`tabindex`). `focus`/`blur` make the app `Resumed`/`Inactive`, and `visibilitychange` makes it `Hidden`.
- The browser's context menu: port `services.BrowserContextMenu` (enabled by default, as in Flutter; an app can disable it).
- Drag and drop is not in 1.1: files dropped on the page are ignored.

**Decided in step 21.2** (web input):
- **Plain Go conversions.** `internal/platform/web` has the conversions as plain Go over plain values, tested in the normal run and under Node: `PointerInput` (`pointer.go`: the button sanitizer, `PointerConverter`, the wheel conversion with the trackpad detection and the line and page deltas), `KeyConverter` (`keyboard.go`, with the generated `keymap_gen.go` that `glutter-gen keys -web-out` writes from Flutter's data: `names.chromium` and `otherWebCodes` of the physical keys, `names.web` of the logical keys, `web_logical_location_mapping.json`, and the layout goals by `code`) and `CursorCSS` (`cursor.go`). `input_js.go` only copies the fields of each event into those values inside the callback (one `js.Func` per kind of event, made in `New`) and queues the conversion.
- **Pointer capture.** `pointerdown` calls `setPointerCapture` on the canvas, prevents the default action and focuses the host (`tabindex` is set to 0 unless the host has one); the moves, the up and the cancel are listened to on the canvas, which the capture makes receive them outside it too. `pointerleave` and `touchend`/`touchcancel` (iOS only, `CancelAbandonedTouches`) as in Flutter.
- **Answers.** The browser's `keydown`, `keyup` and `wheel` callbacks wait for the UI goroutine (`callSync`). The engine's handler implements the optional `platform.SyncInputHandler`: `OnKeySync` and `OnPointerSync` first handle what waits for its frame, then handle the event at once and return what the framework made of it (`DispatchKeyData`'s result, and `PointerData.Respond` called before `OnPointerSync` returns). The other pointer events are batched to the frame as on Linux.
