A callback that finds the queue's current function running was called from inside it (a function that dispatched an event: the browser calls the listener on the goroutine that dispatched it) and does its work at once instead of waiting for itself; a program that dispatches synthetic events from another goroutine, as the tests do, takes the waiting path, as the user's events do.
- **Wheel.** `preventDefault` unless a scrollable at its boundary allowed the platform default; a full-page app in an iframe lets the page scroll only when it was allowed and no widget took the scroll (Flutter's rule). Lines are `fontSize/4` (found with a probe element, 16 when unknown), pages are the size of the view in physical pixels, pixels are multiplied by the device pixel ratio on macOS.
- **Keys.** The converter is Flutter's, including the synthesized ups of modifiers that the event says are up, the synthesized modifier events that pointer events cause (`SynthesizeModifiersIfNeeded`), CapsLock and the 2-second key guards on macOS and iOS. The page's `blur` also releases every key that is down (a deviation, §12.10).
- **Focus.** `focus` and `blur` of the window drive the lifecycle (as in 21.1) and now also `Handler.OnViewFocus`.
- **Context menu.** `WidgetsBinding.BrowserContextMenu()` (`services.BrowserContextMenu`); the engine's `ui.ContextMenuHost` reaches the windows that implement `platform.ContextMenuController`, which the web's window does by adding or removing a `contextmenu` listener that prevents the default. Elsewhere it does nothing, and `Enabled()` stays true.
- **Debug-only scripted events.** In debug builds (`foundation.DebugMode`) `GLUTTER_DEV_EVENTS` (a query parameter of the page) is a script such as `wait:500ms;move:240,400;wheel:240,400,0,300;tap:x,y;key:KeyK,k`, replayed from its own goroutine after the first frame by synthetic DOM events (`internal/platform/web/synthetic`, also used by the browser tests). `glutter-web shot` has `-events` and `-after` for it; a bad script is `WebDevEventsInvalid`.
- **Browser tests** are `internal/platform/web/inputtest` (a program that dispatches its own events and posts what it saw, run by `webtest.Await`), `TestBrowserInput` and, in `examples/counter`, three taps on the floating action button by the scripted events.

### 23.5 Text input, IME and clipboard

Port from `lib/web_ui/lib/src/engine/text_editing/` (`text_editing.dart`, `composition_aware_mixin.dart`, `input_type.dart`, `input_action.dart`) and `clipboard.dart`.

- A transparent text field (`<textarea>` for multiline fields, `<input>` for single-line ones) is placed at the caret rectangle that `SetTextInput` gives, with the field's font size, so the IME's candidate window opens at the caret. It holds the editing state (text, selection, composing range), and its `beforeinput`, `input`, `compositionstart`, `compositionupdate`, `compositionend` and `selectionchange` events become `TextInputEvent`s. It has the focus while a text field is focused and gives it back to the host on blur.
- Being a real text field, it also brings up the virtual keyboard on phones and tablets, set up through `inputmode` and `enterkeyhint` from `TextInputType` and `TextInputAction`.
- Clipboard: copy and cut through `navigator.clipboard.writeText` (asynchronous; the result is posted to the UI goroutine). Paste through the `paste` event of the text field when the user presses the paste shortcut (no permission needed), and `navigator.clipboard.readText` when the app pastes by itself (the browser may ask the user). Only text is supported on the web; other kinds fail as the platform's `Clipboard` documents. There is no primary selection.

**Decided in step 21.3** (web text input, IME and clipboard):
- **The contract is the Linux one.** The hidden field speaks `ui.TextInputState` (to the field) and `ui.TextInputEvent` (from it), as the Linux input methods do, so the framework's `TextInput` service is unchanged. Flutter's engine sends whole editing states or deltas and trusts the platform for the selection; here the framework keeps the caret and the selection (it handles the arrow keys, the mouse and Backspace itself, which keeps working in a field that the browser does not edit), and only text and composing text come back from the element. `internal/platform/web.TextEditor` is the plain-Go model: `SetState` says what to write into the element (only when it differs from the state without the composing text: writing would end an IME composition), `Handle` takes the element's text, selection and the composing range from the composition events (`Composition`, ported from `CompositionAwareMixin`) and finds the change against what the framework holds (`diffEdit`: the end of the change is anchored at the caret), as `DeleteBefore`, `DeleteAfter`, `Commit` and `Preedit`. A selection that only the browser moved is not sent. UTF-16 offsets of the DOM and the UTF-8 offsets of the framework are converted at the edge.
- **Additions to the contract** (all additive): `ui.TextInputEvent.DeleteSelection` (a Backspace of a soft keyboard over a selection commits nothing but must remove it; the Linux input methods never set it), `ui.TextInputState.EnterKey` (from the `TextInputAction`, for `enterkeyhint`), and the optional `platform.SyncInputHandler.OnTextInputSync`, which the engine implements as it does the key: the event is handled at once, after the input that waits for its frame, so that the framework's echo is in the element before the next event is read. `font-size` of the field is the height of the caret rectangle; the state carries no font.
- **Who types a character.** A key that no handler took is typed by the framework from its character (`TextInput.HandleUnhandledKey`, as on Linux) and `OnKeyData` now answers true for it, so the web calls `preventDefault` and the browser does not type it a second time. A key the framework cannot use (`Process` of an IME, a dead key, a key with Control or Alt) is not prevented: the browser, the IME or the virtual keyboard type into the field, and `beforeinput`, `input`, `compositionstart`, `compositionupdate`, `compositionend` and `selectionchange` bring the text. `compositionend` also reads the element, so that the composed text is committed at once (Flutter waits for the next `input` or `selectionchange`). `beforeinput` records an `insertCompositionText` that no `compositionupdate` announced.
- **Paste.** The paste shortcut of the browser (Control+V, Control+Shift+V, Shift+Insert; Command+V on macOS needs nothing) is not given to the framework while the field has the focus (`IsNativePasteKey`), so that nothing handles it and the browser pastes into the hidden field and fires `paste`; the field takes the text of the event (`preventDefault`, the framework gets `Commit`), which needs no permission. Flutter turns the framework's text shortcuts off on the web and lets the browser edit; Glutter ports only the Linux bindings for every platform (§12.10), so this one shortcut is routed to the browser instead. Copy, cut and the toolbar's paste are the framework's: `Clipboard.setData` and `getData` through `navigator.clipboard`, and Control+C in a key event writes in the user's action as the browsers ask.
- **Clipboard.** `clipboard` (`clipboard.go`) is plain Go over a `clipboardAPI` (`ReadText`, `WriteText`); `clipboard_js.go` is `navigator.clipboard`, whose promises continue in two callbacks made at startup that find the call by a number (a small script function attaches them). The answer is posted to the UI goroutine; a read that the context ends is `ClipboardReadTimeout`, once. No `navigator.clipboard` (an insecure context) is `WebClipboardUnavailable`; a refused promise `WebClipboardReadFailed` or `WebClipboardWriteFailed`; the primary selection `ClipboardPrimaryUnavailable`; writing a content without text `WebClipboardFormatUnsupported`; reading another format gives an empty content.
- **The element.** `<input>` or `<textarea>` (the multiline hint), `type=password` for hidden text, with Flutter's static style (transparent, no border, `pointer-events: none`, `position: fixed` at the caret rectangle plus the canvas's position in the page); `inputmode` and `enterkeyhint` only on iOS and Android, as Flutter does; `autocapitalize` and `autocorrect` from the hints. It is made when a text field gets the focus and removed when none has it (the focus goes back to the host); a blur to another element of the host takes the focus back, the page's `focus` event brings it back after the window lost it. A pointer-down focuses the field instead of the host while it exists.
- **Not done:** autofill and `AutofillGroup`, `TextInputType.none` with a physical keyboard (the framework turns the input method off for it, as on Linux), the selection of the element moved by the browser alone (mobile cursor gestures on the space bar), Safari's order of events (`compositionend` before the last `input`).
- **Debug-only scripted events** also type: `input:text`, `compose:a,b,c` and `paste:text` act on the hidden field (`synthetic.TypeText`, `Compose`, `Paste`), which is how the proof types into `examples/editor`; a step with no text field is logged. The editor example still reads and writes its notes in the data directory, which a page does not have: on the web its status bar says so and the saved copy stays "Loading..." (step 21.7 makes the examples fit the web).
- **Browser tests**: `internal/platform/web/texttest` (a Material app with a text field, a password field and a multiline field that dispatches its own events, replaces `navigator.clipboard` by a stub whose promises it settles, and posts what it saw) and `TestBrowserTextInput`.

### 23.6 Fonts, assets, images and settings

- There are no system fonts. The text engine gets the bundled fonts (`material/fonts`) and the fonts the app loads (`ui.LoadFontFromList`, `FontLoader`). `fontscan` and fontconfig are not used. A character with no font draws as a missing-glyph box (§12.10).
- Assets come from `RunAppOpts.Assets` (usually an `embed.FS`), as on Linux. Network images go through `net/http`, which uses `fetch` on js/wasm, so the browser's CORS rules apply.
- Settings: `prefers-color-scheme` gives the brightness, `prefers-reduced-motion` turns animations off, `prefers-contrast: more` gives high contrast, `navigator.languages` gives the locales, and the text scale is 1. Changes arrive through `matchMedia` listeners and `languagechange`.

### 23.7 Services

- `desktop.OpenURI` opens the URI with `window.open(uri, "_blank", "noopener")`, after the same validation as on Linux.
- `desktop.ShowNotification` uses the Notifications API. It asks for permission once; if it is refused, `NotificationsUnavailable`.
- The tray and the file dialogs return `TrayUnavailable` and `FileChooserUnavailable`. D-Bus is never tried on the web.

**Decided in step 21.6** (web services):
- **Where it lives.** `internal/platform/web.Desktop` (`desktop.go`) is plain Go over two small interfaces, `uriOpener` (`window.open`) and `notificationAPI` (the Notifications API), tested in the normal run and under Node with fakes; `desktop_js.go` is the thin `syscall/js` layer (the API objects are looked up at each call, so a page without `Notification`, or a test page that replaces it, is served), and off the web `NewDesktop` returns a `Desktop` with neither. Package `desktop` keeps no JavaScript type: its `services` has a `web` field (set by `servicesOf` when `foundation.IsWeb`) of the interface `webDesktop`, whose methods take and return plain Go values (`web.NotificationSpec`, a number, callbacks). The callbacks come from the browser's event, on any goroutine, and package `desktop` posts them to the UI thread with the same `Guard` as on Linux. With `web` set, `services.connect` fails with `DBusUnavailableOnWeb` without trying anything, so every D-Bus service ends with its own Unavailable tag: `OpenFiles`, `SaveFile` and `SaveFiles` with `FileChooserUnavailable` and a tray with `TrayUnavailable` (reported once through the error reporter), each wrapping that cause. `gluttertest`'s fake desktop (`desktop.InstallBus`) and the test seams `UseBus` and `UseBusOpener` clear `web`, since they put a bus in its place.
- **`OpenURI`.** The validation of Linux first (`OpenURIInvalid`), then the web's: a `javascript:` or `vbscript:` URI, read as a browser reads the scheme (leading control characters and spaces dropped, tabs and newlines inside it ignored, any case), is `OpenURISchemeBlocked`: those schemes run script in the page that opens them. Then `window.open(uri, "_blank", "noopener")` for every other scheme (`mailto:` too: the target stays as the spec said). A `window.open` that throws (a sandboxed frame without `allow-popups`) or a page without a window is `OpenURIUnavailable` (with the reason). `noopener` makes `window.open` answer `null` whatever happened, so a pop-up blocker's refusal cannot be seen and is not reported; browsers allow a window only soon after a click or key press, so an app calls `OpenURI` from the handler of one (on a goroutine, as everywhere: the goroutine runs in the same browser task as long as nothing waits for a promise).
- **`ShowNotification`.** The call (which waits, so it is on a goroutine and not the UI thread) reads `Notification.permission`: `granted` goes on; `denied` is `NotificationsUnavailable` at once; `default` calls `Notification.requestPermission()` and waits for its promise (a callback made once posts the answer; the wait ends with the context, `NotificationPermissionAborted`), and any answer but `granted` (the prompt dismissed leaves `default`) is `NotificationsUnavailable`. "Once" means once per decision: a permission that is decided is never asked for again, and one that stayed `default` is asked for at the next call, which is the browser's to allow or refuse. A page with no `Notification` (an insecure context) is `NotificationsUnavailable` too, with the reason in the metadata. A browser that refuses `new Notification(...)` is `WebNotificationFailed`. What a notification can be (a `Notification` made without a service worker has no buttons):
  - `summary` is the title; `Body` is the plain-text body (`BodyMarkup` strips the tags and reads the entities, `web.StripMarkup`); `Icon` is used when it is a URL or a path (`web.IconURL`: it has a slash or is a `data:` URI), a theme name is ignored; `Urgency` Critical and `Persistent` set `requireInteraction`, Low sets `silent`; `Category` and `AppName` have nowhere to go (the browser names the page).
  - **Actions.** Only a click exists: the action with the ID `"default"` is the click (`OnAction("default")`), the other actions are not shown and not called. No buttons without a service worker, which a Glutter page does not have (Linux's rule, "the ID `default` is the action of a click on the notification itself", is the one kept).
  - **Replace by ID.** The notification's `tag` is `glutter-<id>`; `ReplacesID` of a notification that is shown makes the new one with the same tag, which the browser shows in its place, and returns the same ID; the replaced one's `OnClosed` is not called (the servers of Linux do not report a replacement either). An ID that is not shown makes a new notification.
  - **Close.** `CloseNotification` calls `Notification.close()` and `OnClosed(NotificationClosedByApp)`; closing one that is gone is `nil`. A close that the browser reports after a click is `NotificationClosedDismissed`; any other close of the browser (its own time-out, the notification center cleared) is `NotificationClosedUnknown`, since the page cannot tell. `Timeout` is kept by the page, because the Notifications API has none: a timer closes the notification with `NotificationClosedExpired` (not for `Persistent`).
- **Not done.** Actions as buttons (service worker), `AppName`, and the permission being asked for from the UI thread (the call must be on a goroutine as on Linux).
- **Browser tests** (`internal/platform/web/desktoptest`, `TestBrowserDesktopServices`): a program that replaces `window.open` and `Notification` by stubs (a headless browser cannot answer a permission prompt; the code under test is the real one) and posts what it saw: `window.open` got `(uri, "_blank", "noopener")`, a refused permission, the one prompt, the options of the `Notification`, the click, the closes, the replacement and a page without `Notification`.
- Accessibility (an ARIA tree from the semantics tree) is not in 1.1. `Services().Accessibility()` is nil.

### 23.8 Testing and proof

- Unit tests run under Node (`scripts/test-wasm.sh`), and browser tests under the `glutter_browser` tag (§17).
- Browsers run headless with a temporary profile: Chromium with `--headless=new` and the flags that turn WebGPU on under Linux (kept in `internal/webtest`, with the reason for each), and Firefox with `--headless`.
- **Proof method D**: `glutter-web shot` builds the app, serves it, and opens it with `GLUTTER_SCREENSHOT=1` in the URL. After the first idle frame (as in step 7.4) the engine reads its frame back and posts a PNG to `/__glutter/screenshot`. The tool saves the PNG and kills the browser.
- `examples/examples_test.go` also builds every web example for js/wasm.

**Decided in step 21.7** (web examples, size and performance; the targets of §15 are not changed by it):
- **The examples on the web.** Every example except `multiwindow` and `tray` builds for js/wasm (`examples/examples_test.go` builds each directory it finds, so a new example is covered; under `-short` the builds are skipped) and draws its first frame in Chromium and Firefox (`TestBrowserEveryWebExampleDrawsItsFirstFrame`, `glutter_browser`). What a page does not have degrades through the public API: `examples/editor` keeps its notes in memory when `foundation.IsWeb` (a `memoryNotes` in `options`, set by `main`), and its status bar says so; on Linux nothing changed (its tests and golden are the same).
- **`glutter-web perf` and `scripts/perf-web.sh`.** The page is measured from outside, with no change to the app: `webtest.Perf` serves the build with the caching headers of a deployed site (`webtool.CachingHandler`; `Handler`, which `serve` uses, sends `no-store` so that a new build always loads), puts a script at the start of the app's page that counts the calls of `requestAnimationFrame`, `setTimeout` and `setInterval` and the times between two animation callbacks, runs the app in a frame of 1536x864 CSS pixels at a device pixel ratio of 1.25 (a 1920x1080 display, §15) with `GLUTTER_FRAME_STATS=1`, and reads what the loader posts: with `glutter_log_every=<ms>` the loader posts the program's new output, and sets `window.glutterTimings` (milliseconds since the page began to load, when the module was instantiated and when the first frame was presented). The start is timed in several loads one after the other, the first with an empty cache; the others are "app.wasm in the browser's cache". The CPU time is that of every process of the browser's group (and of Xvfb for Firefox), read from `/proc` at both ends of the window, and is compared with a blank page that makes the same posts. Clicks are `PointerEvent`s dispatched at the canvas by the page (a move, a press 100 ms later, a release 50 ms after that); a click's latency is the time from the release to the end of the first `requestAnimationFrame` callback that began after it, which is when the Go frame (build, layout, paint and the renderer's calls) has run: the GPU's work after that is not counted. The engine's statistics also log, with `GLUTTER_FRAME_STATS=1`, every frame over a 60th of a second on its own line (`slow frame build=... raster=...`).
- **JavaScript calls are not what is slow.** The remedy of §22 (one buffer of encoded calls) was not needed: in the gallery the calls of a render pass cost about a microsecond each (1,700 calls in 1 to 2 ms), and all calls of the device that make or write a resource come to 3 to 13 ms in a frame of 40 to 90 ms. What costs is Go on one thread: a page of the gallery built from nothing is 17 to 90 ms of build, layout and paint in WebAssembly against 3 to 16 ms native (four to seven times), and the explicit renderer's own work for a scene that is new (clip masks drawn on the CPU, flattening) is about 25 ms.
- **Garbage collection is tuned for the web** (`engine.tuneGC`, `webGCPercent` 400 and `webMemoryLimit` 768 MiB, unless `GOGC` or `GOMEMLIMIT` is set). There is one thread, so the collector's work is taken from the frames, and a click that builds a screen allocates megabytes (a gallery page: 5.7 MB in 57,000 objects). With the default 100 the click on the gallery's navigation spent about a quarter of its time in it; the price is a Go heap of up to five times what is live (the counter and the gallery use 150 to 280 MB of Go memory while animating or switching pages, against 66 to 85 MB before).
- **Framework changes the profile asked for** (all keep Linux behavior): `foundation.SameRuntimeType` in place of two `RuntimeTypeName` calls (§12.10); `ThemeData.copyWithIconTheme` (§12.10); the inherited-widget dependency maps are written only when the entry is new (`DependOnInheritedElement`, `InheritedElement.UpdateDependencies`, `InheritedModelElement.UpdateDependencies`); `min` and `max` of Go in place of `math.Min` and `math.Max` in `ui`, `internal/gpu` and `internal/text` (the functions are not inlined in WebAssembly and sat in the loops of the rasterizer; the same values); `gpu.cut` skips the rows that a mask does not reach. The gallery builds its two themes when the seed changes, not in every build, and has `GLUTTER_GALLERY_REBUILD=1` (every build in every frame, for the measurement of a full rebuild).
- **Sizes.** The counter's release build is 38 MB of WebAssembly (stripped) and 8.8 MB gzipped, the gallery's 58 MB and 11.9 MB: more than twice the targets. The bundled fonts are 1.96 MB of that gzipped (Roboto 1.4, Material Icons 0.56). The figure of §15's "on 2026-10-21, without the web platform and renderers: 2.3 MB / 2.4 MB" was not a measurement of these programs. The levers are listed in docs/PERFORMANCE.md; none is applied in this step.

**Decided in step 21.1** (web platform skeleton, `glutter-web`, the browser harness):
- **Packages.** Besides `internal/platform/web`, `internal/gpu/canvas2d`, `cmd/glutter-web` and `internal/webtest`, packaging lives in `internal/webtool` (build, `index.html`, the loader, the file server with gzip), because `glutter-web` and the browser tests both use it and an `internal` package of a command cannot be imported by `internal/webtest`. `scripts/test-wasm.sh` leaves `internal/webtool` and `internal/webtest` out, like `cmd/`: their tests run the go command, an HTTP server and browsers, which Node cannot. The files that talk to the browser carry `js && wasm`; the size and ratio math (`Geometry`), the config, `EnvFromQuery`, the detection of the operating system, the settings, the lifecycle and the event queue are plain Go, tested in the normal run and under Node.
- **Target platform.** `foundation.DefaultTargetPlatform` reads a value that the web platform sets once at startup through `foundation.SetBrowserTargetPlatform` (it exists only under js/wasm; before it is called, and in tests under Node, the host platform is Linux). `DetectOS` and the mapping (an unknown OS is Android) port `browser_detection.dart` and `_platform_web.dart`. So `foundation/web_js.go` holds a variable, not only the constant of §6.3 rule 2.
- **The canvas.** The platform makes it with the attribute `data-glutter-view="<view>"`; `gpu.SurfaceHandle` has the new kind `SurfaceKindWeb` whose `Window` is the view ID, and `canvas2d` finds the canvas by that attribute. The canvas's `width` and `height` are set to the frame's size by the renderer when it draws (in the same task as `putImageData`), not by the platform when the `ResizeObserver` fires, which would blank the canvas for a frame; the platform sets them once, when it makes the canvas. The canvas is opaque (`getContext("2d", {alpha: false})`); the scene is laid over `#6750A4`, as `gles` does.
- **Events.** One `js.Func` per kind of browser event, made in `New`. The loop is `internal/platform/web.queue`: callbacks post a function, and `Run` runs them. `Run` reports the lifecycle and the size first, then schedules the frame that `RequestFrame` asked for before `Run` began. The page's lifecycle is `Hidden` when `document.visibilityState` is not `visible`, else `Resumed` with the focus and `Inactive` without it. `Displays()` reads `screen` each time; a change of the device pixel ratio calls `OnDisplaysChanged` and then `OnMetrics`.
- **Things the web platform cannot do yet or at all.** `StartDrag` fails with `DragUnsupported`; `KeepScreenOn(true)` with `IdleInhibitUnavailable`; `Services()` is nil. A second window, a dialog or a window with a parent is `WindowUnsupported`. The app's request to exit (`RequestAppExit`) does nothing: the platform implements the new optional `platform.ExitController` with `CanExit() false`, which the engine checks (SPEC §23.3).
- **Self-capture.** `GLUTTER_SCREENSHOT=1` in the URL: the engine encodes the frame and hands it to a platform that implements the new optional `platform.ScreenshotPoster` (the web does; Linux writes the file as before). The web posts it with `fetch` to `/__glutter/screenshot` (`web.ScreenshotPath`); the answer is a promise whose `then` posts to the UI goroutine, and `Run` returns when it arrived, so the program exits with code 0. A failed post is `ScreenshotWriteFailed`.
- **The loader.** `index.html` has `globalThis.glutterConfig`, `wasm_exec.js` and the loader inline. Besides copying the `GLUTTER_` parameters (`web.EnvFromQuery` is the specification, `loader_test.go` runs both on the same inputs under Node) it reads `?glutter_report=<path>` and, when that is given, posts `{kind: "exit", exitCode, log}` to the path when the program ends, with what the program wrote to standard output and error: the browser tests learn from it why an app failed. `glutter-web build`, `serve` and `shot` also take `-host <selector>`; `shot` takes `-width`, `-height`, `-timeout` and `-query <query string>` (repeatable).
- **Web fonts.** `engine.SystemFontMatcher` on js returns a matcher that finds nothing, so the collection holds the bundled fonts and the fonts the app loads and never scans a directory.
- **The browser harness.** Chromium and Firefox have a minimum window width and show toolbars even when headless (Firefox: `--window-size` is ignored, `--width` gives a viewport 74 px shorter than the window), so an exact viewport cannot come from the window. `Shot` serves a wrapper page (`/__glutter/frame.html`) that shows the app in an `<iframe>` of exactly the size asked for; the app sees that as its window, and a resize is the wrapper changing the iframe's size once the app's canvas exists. Chromium's flags are kept in `internal/webtest/browser.go` as a table with the reason for each; `--no-sandbox` is not set because Chromium starts without it here (`GLUTTER_WEBTEST_FLAGS=--no-sandbox` adds it where the sandbox cannot start); the flags for WebGPU are in §9.5 (step 21.5). Firefox gets a `user.js` with the preferences that quiet it, each with its reason. Browsers run under `timeout -k 5s <lifetime>` in a process group of their own, with no `DISPLAY` or `WAYLAND_DISPLAY` and with `HOME`, `XDG_*` and `TMPDIR` inside the temporary profile, so nothing of the user's is reached; `Process.Stop` kills the group and removes the profile. The group code is `procgroup_linux.go` (an OS tag in `internal/webtest`, which §6.3 and CLAUDE.md list for this reason; other OSes get a launcher that kills the process only).
- **Tests of the framework on Linux and wasm do not need a browser;** the browser tests (`glutter_browser`) are in `examples/counter` (first frame equals the golden, resize, device pixel ratio 2), `internal/platform/web` (host element, bad config, renderer names) and `internal/webtest` (the real browsers start, post, and leave nothing running).

## 24. Android (`GOOS=android`, 1.6)

Android is the first target where cgo is unavoidable (D21): an Android app is a shared library that Android loads into its own process, and Go builds one only with cgo (`-buildmode=c-shared`); purego, too, needs cgo on Android. The cgo stays in one package, `internal/platform/android`, and only in files that build for Android. Linux and the web stay cgo-free. Like Flutter's Android embedding (`FlutterActivity`, `FlutterView`), a few Java classes own the activity and the view and hand everything to Go through JNI (D22). The renderer is Vulkan where the device's Vulkan is good, with GLES as the fallback, as Flutter's Impeller does on Android (D23).

**Build tags.** `GOOS=android` satisfies the `linux` build constraint, so every file tagged `linux` (and every `_linux.go` file) builds for Android too. The desktop backends that Android does not have carry `linux && !android`: `internal/platform/linux/...` and `internal/engine/backends_linux.go`. The pure-Go packages (`internal/dbus`, `desktop`) keep no OS tag, as on the web: on Android nothing connects to D-Bus (§24.11). The parts that both share keep `linux`: the purego loaders and the `gles` and `vulkan` renderers, which load `libEGL.so`, `libGLESv3.so` and `libvulkan.so` on Android as they load the Mesa libraries on Linux. Files that only Android builds are named `*_android.go`.

**Decided in step 25.1.** Every file of `internal/platform/linux/...` except the loader `dl` is tagged `linux && !android` (the `doc.go` of each package is not, so a package whose files are all tagged still exists on Android), the generators `glutter-gen wayland` and `glutter-gen keys` write that tag, and so do `internal/engine/backends_linux.go` and the Linux-only tests of `engine` and `widgets`. `internal/engine/backends_android.go` has `Run` fail with `BackendUnavailable`, no renderer and no font matcher until steps 25.3 to 25.10; `backends_other.go` stays `!linux && !js`. `foundation/host_android.go` makes `TargetPlatformAndroid` the host platform and defines `IsWeb`, which `web_other.go` (`!(js && wasm) && !android`) no longer does there. `scripts/check-android.sh` builds and vets the module for `android/arm64` and `android/amd64` with `CGO_ENABLED=1` and the NDK's clang for API 26, and fails with the `sdkmanager` command when there is no NDK. `glutter-gen errcheck` has a fourth pass, `android/arm64` with the same compiler, and fails with `ErrCheckAndroidNDKNotFound` without an NDK (the test of the whole module leaves that pass out and says so). `apicheck` reads the source of the module and does not depend on the target. `scripts/fetch-reference.sh` also fetches Flutter's Android embedding and Impeller's Vulkan backend. `TestBuildsHaveTheirOwnBackends` (`internal/engine`) lists the imports of `examples/counter` per target with `go list`: Android has none of the desktop backends, the web has none of the Linux ones.

### 24.1 Build and packaging

- An app is built with `go run ./cmd/glutter-android build [-o app.apk] [-abi arm64,amd64] [-release] [-app-id org.example.app] [-label Name] [-icon icon.png] [-permission NAME]... <package>`. It finds the tools through `ANDROID_HOME` (the SDK: `build-tools/<newest>` for `aapt2`, `zipalign` and `apksigner`, `platforms/<newest>` for `android.jar`) and `ANDROID_NDK_HOME`, else the newest `ndk/<version>` under the SDK. A missing piece is a tagged error that names the `sdkmanager` package to install (`AndroidSDKNotFound`, `AndroidNDKNotFound`, `AndroidBuildToolsNotFound`, `AndroidPlatformNotFound`).
- For each ABI (`arm64` is `arm64-v8a`, `amd64` is `x86_64`; the default is both) it runs `GOOS=android GOARCH=<arch> CGO_ENABLED=1 CC=<ndk>/toolchains/llvm/prebuilt/linux-x86_64/bin/<triple><min-sdk>-clang go build -buildmode=c-shared -o lib/<abi>/libapp.so <package>`. `-release` adds `-tags glutter_release -ldflags=-s -w`. The `.so` files are stored uncompressed and aligned to 16 KB (`zipalign -P 16`), so that the APK installs on devices with 16 KB memory pages, which Google Play requires; the ELF segments Go 1.27 and NDK 30 make are already 16 KB-aligned (checked on 2026-10-23).
- The APK holds a manifest that the tool generates (one activity, `io.github.rm4n0s.glutter.GlutterActivity`, with the `configChanges` Flutter declares, so that turning the device or changing the theme does not recreate the activity; `minSdkVersion` 26, `targetSdkVersion` 37; the permissions asked for; `android:hasCode`), the embedding's `classes.dex`, the icon (Glutter's by default), and the libraries. It is linked with `aapt2 link` against `android.jar` and signed with `apksigner` (APK Signature Scheme v2 and v3). Debug builds are signed with a key the tool makes once with Go's `crypto` packages (`$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/glutter/android-debug/`); `-release` needs `-key` and `-cert` (PKCS#8 and X.509, as `apksigner` takes them). No Gradle, Android Studio, AndroidX or Kotlin is used.
- `glutter-android install [-device serial]`, `run` (install, start, and stream the app's log lines from `logcat` until it exits or Ctrl+C) and `shot` (proof method E, §24.13) work on a connected device or a running emulator through `adb`. `run` and `shot` take `-env GLUTTER_X=v` (repeatable): they are given to the activity as intent extras, and the embedding copies the extras whose names start with `GLUTTER_` into the environment before `main` runs, as the web's loader copies the query parameters (§23.1).
- An Android App Bundle (`.aab`) for Google Play is not in 1.6.

**Decided in step 25.2.** The tool is `internal/androidtool` (the finder `FindSDK`, `Build`, `ADB`, `EnsureDebugKey`, `CompileDex`) with the command `cmd/glutter-android` (`build`, `install`, `run`). The finder picks the newest `ndk/<version>`, the newest `build-tools/<version>` that has `aapt2` and the newest `platforms/android-<level>` that has `android.jar`; the tags name the `sdkmanager` package in the metadata `install`. The default application ID of a package is `org.glutter.<last element>` with every character that an ID cannot have turned into `_`, and the label is the last element. `Build` runs, in this order: `go build -buildmode=c-shared` per ABI, `aapt2 compile` (the icon) and `aapt2 link` (manifest, `--min-sdk-version 26 --target-sdk-version 37`), the packing of the APK in Go (aapt2's entries as they are, `classes.dex`, the libraries stored), `zipalign -f -P 16 4`, and `apksigner sign --key --cert --min-sdk-version 26`. The manifest has `extractNativeLibs=false` (the system maps the libraries from the APK), `versionCode` 1, `launchMode` `singleTop`, the theme `Theme.Material.NoActionBar`, and `debuggable` in debug builds (adb's `run-as` needs it). The debug key is an RSA 2048 key in `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/glutter/android-debug/{key.pk8,cert.pem}`. `install` and `run` build only the ABI of the device (`ro.product.cpu.abilist`) unless `-abi` is given, and `install` also takes a ready `.apk` (with `-app-id`). `run` starts the activity with `am start -W` after `am force-stop`, passes `-env` as `--es` extras, and shows `logcat --pid` until the process is gone.

### 24.2 The embedding and the native boundary

- **Java.** The classes live in `internal/androidtool/java/io/github/rm4n0s/glutter/` and port the parts of Flutter's embedding (`engine/src/flutter/shell/platform/android/io/flutter/`) that Glutter needs: `GlutterActivity` (the activity and its lifecycle, configuration changes, back callbacks, results of the system dialogs), `GlutterView` (a `SurfaceView`: the surface's life, touch, mouse and key events, window insets, `onCreateInputConnection`, `getAccessibilityNodeProvider`), `GlutterInputConnection` (from `InputConnectionAdaptor` and `ListenableEditingState`), `GlutterAccessibility` (from `AccessibilityBridge`) and `Native` (the `native` methods and the calls from Go). They use only the platform's APIs of the minimum API level and check the level before newer calls. Each class says in a comment which Flutter class it ports, as the Go code's `// Flutter:` lines do.
- **The checked-in dex.** `go run ./cmd/glutter-gen android-dex` compiles the Java with `javac --release 11 -classpath android.jar` and `d8 --min-api 26` into `internal/androidtool/classes.dex`, which is checked in and embedded into `glutter-android`. So building an app needs no JDK, only the Java runtime that `apksigner` runs on; `javac` is needed only to change the Java (as `naga` is for the shaders, D20). A test checks that the dex is up to date and skips without `javac`.
- **cgo.** `internal/platform/android` is the only package with `import "C"`, and only in `*_android.go` files: the exported entry points (`JNI_OnLoad` and the `native` methods of `Native`), and small C functions for the `JNIEnv` calls and the NDK APIs (`ANativeWindow`, `ALooper`, `AChoreographer`, `liblog`). No C type, C pointer or JNI reference leaves the package; the GPU backends get the `ANativeWindow*` as the `uintptr` of a `gpu.SurfaceHandle` of the new kind `SurfaceKindAndroid`. Global JNI references are made once and dropped when the activity is destroyed.
- **`main`.** A shared library does not run `main.main` by itself. `GlutterActivity.onCreate` loads `libapp.so`, whose `JNI_OnLoad` keeps the `JavaVM`; then it calls `Native.start`, which runs `main.main` (linked as gomobile and Gio do) on a new goroutine locked to its own OS thread. That thread is the UI thread (§24.3). `main` runs once per process: when it returns, or calls `os.Exit`, the activity finishes and the process exits with that code. If Android destroys the activity while the process lives on and a new activity starts (the user came back), the new activity attaches to the running app: the view gets the new surface.

**Decided in step 25.2.** The JNI entry points are C functions in `jni_android.c` (`JNI_OnLoad`, `Native.setenv`, `Native.start`), which call the Go functions `goSetenv` and `goStart`; `JNI_OnLoad` keeps the `JavaVM` and a global reference to `GlutterActivity`. `Native.setenv` calls `os.Setenv`, because Go copies the environment when the library loads and `setenv(3)` would not reach `os.Getenv`. `main.main` is reached with `//go:linkname callmain main.main` (an empty `stub_android.s` lets the declaration have no body). The embedding sets `GLUTTER_APP_ID` (the application ID, the tag of the log lines) with `HOME` (the files directory), `TMPDIR` and `XDG_CACHE_HOME` (the cache directory), `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` (`files/.config`) and `XDG_DATA_HOME` (`files/.local/share`), then the `GLUTTER_` extras. When `main` returns, Go calls `GlutterActivity.onMainEnded(0)` (the thread attaches to the JavaVM for the call and detaches), which posts to the main thread: the activity finishes, and `onDestroy` calls `System.exit` with the code (after 2 seconds anyway if the system is slow). `os.Exit` ends the process at once, as Go does; on the emulator the system then drops the activity within a second and does not start it again (checked on API 37). A second activity in a living process does not start `main` again.
- **One view.** The activity shows one view that fills its window. A second `NewWindow`, a dialog window or a window with a parent is `WindowUnsupported`, as on the web. `SetTitle` sets the task description's label; `SetSize`, `SetSizeConstraints` and the window states are ignored, except fullscreen (immersive mode, §24.7); `SetHidden` returns false; `SetIcon` and `SetContentType` do nothing.

### 24.3 Threads, event loop and frames

- **Two threads, as in Flutter's Android embedding.** Android calls the activity, the view, the input connection and the accessibility provider on its main thread (Flutter's platform thread). Glutter's UI thread is the OS thread that runs `main`: it owns the framework, the renderer and the GL or Vulkan objects (§13).
- **The UI thread's loop** is an `ALooper` prepared on that thread: `Post` writes to an `eventfd` the looper watches; frames come from `AChoreographer` frame callbacks on that looper (the 64-bit callback where the API level has it); `RequestFrame` asks for one callback, and nothing is asked for while no frame is scheduled. The frame time is the callback's vsync time.
- **Events.** The Java side copies each event into plain values (a `MotionEvent` is recycled after its callback) and calls a `native` method, which queues it for the UI thread and returns. Nothing the main thread needs right away waits for the UI thread: unhandled keys are dispatched again later (§24.5), the input connection answers from its own copy of the editing state (§24.6), and the accessibility provider from its own copy of the semantics tree (§24.10).
- **The one wait.** `surfaceDestroyed` must not return while the renderer still draws into the surface, so it waits for the UI thread to release the surface (at most 2 seconds, then it logs and returns). **The UI thread never waits for the main thread.** What Go asks of Java (show the keyboard, a new editing state, a semantics update, haptics, the system UI) is posted to the main thread's `Handler`, and answers (a clipboard read, a permission) come back as events. The UI thread is attached to the `JavaVM` once, when it starts.
- Other goroutines use `SetState` and `Post` as on Linux.

**Decided in step 25.3.** The platform is plain Go over two small pieces, so that its tests run on any machine: a `host` (Poll, Wake, RequestVsync, ReleaseWindow; the cgo `nativeHost` makes it from an `ALooper` with an `eventfd` and the `AChoreographer`, `host_android.c`) and a `bridge` (the one queue of the process, where the native methods of the Java side put their events, and the state of the surface, the lifecycle and the focus). `ALooper_prepare` runs in `New`, which the engine calls on the UI thread; Poll is `ALooper_pollOnce(-1)`, whose callbacks only set flags in C, so no Go code runs inside the looper. The frame callback is `AChoreographer_postFrameCallback64` when `dlsym` finds it (API 29), else the 32-bit one. An event is applied on the UI thread, between frames, in order with `Post`; while no UI thread runs (before `Run`, or after it) it is applied at once on the calling thread, so nothing that came early is lost. A frame is asked for only while there is a window with a size and the activity is not stopped; requests made meanwhile, or during a frame, are one more frame (`frameRequests`). **The one wait** is `bridge.surfaceDestroyed`: the UI thread applies the destroy between frames, tells the handler (`platform.SurfaceHandler.OnSurfaceLost`, which makes the renderer release the window) and then releases the `ANativeWindow`; the main thread waits for that for at most 2 seconds, logs a warning and goes on if the UI thread is late (a frame in progress keeps using the window, which stays valid because Go holds its own reference). `OnSurfaceLost` is a new optional interface of the handler (SPEC §8.2).

### 24.4 Rendering

- **`SurfaceKindAndroid`**: `Window` is the `ANativeWindow*` of the view's surface, held (`ANativeWindow_acquire`) from `surfaceCreated` until the renderer has let it go. `surfaceChanged` gives the new size (`OnMetrics`); a destroyed surface stops the frames until the next one exists.
- **GLES**: EGL on the `ANativeWindow` (`eglCreateWindowSurface`), OpenGL ES 3.0 and the same shaders (D7). The EGL context lives as long as the renderer; only the EGL surface is made again with each Android surface.
- **Vulkan**: `VK_KHR_android_surface`. The swapchain's `preTransform` is the surface's `currentTransform`, and the frame is drawn rotated to match, so that the display does not rotate it again (Android's guidance for Vulkan). The render passes of the explicit renderer are checked for tile-based GPUs (Adreno, Mali, PowerVR): an attachment is cleared, or not loaded when the pass covers all of it, instead of being loaded from memory, and what nothing reads later is not stored.
- **`soft`**: `internal/gpu/nativewindow`, the software renderer drawing into the window's CPU buffers (`ANativeWindow_setBuffersGeometry`, `lock`, `unlockAndPost`, loaded through purego from `libandroid.so`), the last fallback, as `canvas2d` is on the web. It also makes the first frames before the GPU renderers exist (step 25.3).
- **`auto` on Android** (§9.6): Vulkan when the device runs Android 10 (API 29) or later, has Vulkan 1.1, is not on the deny list of drivers that Flutter's Impeller keeps (ported from `impeller/renderer/backend/vulkan/driver_info_vk.cc`), and presents the first frame. Otherwise GLES. Otherwise `soft`.
- A lost surface is not a lost device: the renderer keeps its device, context, textures and atlases while the app is in the background. When Android asks for memory (`onTrimMemory`), the renderer drops its caches (the raster cache, the unused atlas pages), as Flutter does.

**Decided in step 25.3.** `internal/gpu/nativewindow` is `soft` plus a `Presenter` (a copy of `canvas2d`'s design): the Android presenter calls `ANativeWindow_setBuffersGeometry` (RGBA_8888) when the frame size changes, `ANativeWindow_lock`, copies the rows (the buffer's stride is in pixels) and `ANativeWindow_unlockAndPost`, through `dl` and purego from `libandroid.so`; the sequence is plain Go over a `windowAPI` interface and tested with a fake, and the `ANativeWindow_Buffer` mirror has a layout test. The engine's Android renderer list is `soft` only; `vulkan` and `gles` are `RendererUnavailable` by name. `GLUTTER_SCREENSHOT=1` means `$HOME/glutter-screenshot.png`, which is the app's files directory (a value that is a path is used as it is). `glutter-android shot` is proof method E: it starts the harness's emulator (or uses `-device`), builds for the device's ABI, starts the app with `GLUTTER_SCREENSHOT=1` and the `-env` extras, waits for the process to end and reads the file with `run-as`; with `-screen` it waits for the log line "renderer chosen", settles and takes `screencap`. The Java side: `GlutterView` (a `SurfaceView`) sends `surfaceCreated` (the `ANativeWindow` is acquired in C), `surfaceChanged` (size, `DisplayMetrics.density`, the display's refresh rate and real size) and `surfaceDestroyed`; `GlutterActivity` sends the lifecycle (resumed, paused, stopped; step 25.9 completes it) and the window focus. `dl` also finds Bionic's `libc.so`.

**Decided in step 25.4.** `gles` draws on Android through EGL on the `ANativeWindow`: `eglGetDisplay(EGL_DEFAULT_DISPLAY)`, `libEGL.so` and `libGLESv3.so` (the loader also tries the unversioned names), the config of the Linux code, and `eglCreateWindowSurface` with the `ANativeWindow`; before the surface is made the window gets the pixel format of the config (`EGL_NATIVE_VISUAL_ID` through `ANativeWindow_setBuffersGeometry`, `windowPreparer`). The surface is made with the legacy call, since `eglCreatePlatformWindowSurface` needs a platform display Android does not have. The context outlives its surfaces: `Surface.Destroy` first makes the context current with no surface when that surface was current, and the engine's `glesRenderer.Release` (called from `OnSurfaceLost`, before the window goes) destroys only the surface, so the textures and atlases survive Home and back. The log says it: "OpenGL ES surface released; the context stays", then "OpenGL ES surface attached to the existing context" and "OpenGL ES first frame on the new surface" with `tiles_rasterized`, `images_uploaded` and `clip_masks_built`, which are 0 after Home and back. Android has no damage tracking in `gles` (`eglSwapBuffersWithDamage` is not used). The Android list is `gles`, `soft`; `vulkan` is `RendererUnavailable` until step 25.5. The parity program is `examples/dev/android-parity`: it draws every proof scene with `gles` (and with `soft`) on the device and saves the images; the device test compares the two, **both drawn on the device**, because the host's system fonts are not the device's (against the host's `soft` image, `text-tour` differs in 18,000 pixels, and with the device's own `soft` image in none). The tolerances are `gputest.ProofTolerances`, with one addition: in `gradients`, up to 200 pixels may differ by more than 96 levels, because the repeat seams of a diagonal gradient run through pixel centers, where `t` is exactly a whole number and each GPU decides which end of the ramp the pixel gets (80 pixels on the emulator's GPU, none on llvmpipe).

**Decided in step 25.5.** Vulkan on Android is the explicit renderer over `libvulkan.so` with `VK_KHR_android_surface` (`VkAndroidSurfaceCreateInfoKHR` with the `ANativeWindow`), and the device is the first that can present to the surface. **Pre-rotation:** the swapchain's `preTransform` is the surface's `currentTransform` when that is a rotation the surface supports (else the identity, as before; Wayland and X11 always give the identity), and with a quarter turn its images have the width and height swapped (Android's `currentExtent` is the window's size, and the loader gives the buffers the size asked for). `explicit.Swapchain.PreRotation()` says the turn; the renderer's blit pass (the only pass that draws into the swapchain image) draws the frame rotated clockwise by it: the rotation is the instance index of the blit draw (`firstInstance`), and `blit.wgsl` samples the frame texture with the rotated coordinates, so no uniform and no new pipeline. The damage rectangles are rotated the same way (`explicit.RotateRects`). The emulator reports all four transforms and the screen equals the software renderer\'s in each. The system reports the transform of a surface when it likes: a swapchain made before the surface is on screen can have the identity, and the first present (or acquire) then says it is suboptimal; `Swapchain.Stale()` is true when the transform the swapchain would choose now is another one, and the renderer then blits the frame texture again, once and whole, into a new swapchain (`Renderer.blitAgain`), so that an app that draws nothing more is right. A half turn changes no size, and the emulator then often never reports it: the compositor turns the frame, correctly, which is why the device test compares the screen with the software renderer\'s in all four orientations and only requires that some orientation was pre-rotated. **Tile-based GPUs:** the passes of the renderer were checked: the frame texture is cleared to the background, a layer to transparent, and a pass that comes back to a target after a copy (a blend that reads the pixels under it, a backdrop filter, a layer's composite) loads it, which no pass order can avoid; the one pass that covers all of its target and used to clear it, the blit into the swapchain image, now has `explicit.LoadDontCare` (Vulkan `LOAD_OP_DONT_CARE`; WebGPU, which has none, clears), and every store is one that something reads later (the frame texture and the layers are sampled, the swapchain image is presented). **`auto`:** Android 10 (API 29) or later (`AndroidVulkanTooOld`, from `android_get_device_api_level`), Vulkan 1.1 (as everywhere), and a driver that is not on the deny list ported from `driver_info_vk.cc` (`VulkanDriverDenied`; `vulkan.Options.DenyKnownBad`, set for `auto` only), then the first frame presented; the chooser logs the reason for each candidate it does not keep ("renderer not usable", with the tag) and the one it chose with its device. Vulkan asked for by name has none of these checks except the loader, and takes a CPU device (the emulator's gfxstream over SwiftShader). The list is `vulkan`, `gles`, `soft`. The swapchain's size, pre-rotation and present mode are logged when it is made ("Vulkan swapchain"). `glutter-android shot` has `-rotation 0..3` (it turns the device before the app starts and back after). The validation layer: `glutter-android build -vulkan-validation <dir>` copies `<dir>/<abi>/libVkLayer_khronos_validation.so` into `lib/<abi>/` of the APK, stored; the app asks for it by name through `GLUTTER_VULKAN_VALIDATION=1`. **Logging, found by the device test of a failing Vulkan:** a line that a program writes with `log.Fatal` waited in the pipe of standard error while the process ended, and was lost about one run in three; the standard logger (`log`, which `log.Fatal`, `log.Print` and the default `slog` logger write through) now writes to logcat at once, from the calling goroutine, and when `main` returns the platform writes an end marker into the pipe and waits (at most a second) until the reader has passed it, before it tells the activity to finish. Direct writes to standard output or error, and a panic, still go through the pipe.

### 24.5 Input

Port from `AndroidTouchProcessor.java`, `KeyboardManager.java`, `KeyEmbedderResponder.java` and `KeyboardMap.java` of Flutter's embedding, or the files that hold that code at the pinned commit.

- **Pointers.** `MotionEvent`s become `PointerData` with Flutter's conversion: the kind from the tool type (touch, stylus, inverted stylus, mouse), every pointer of the event and its historical samples, pressure, size, orientation and tilt, hover and the mouse buttons (`onGenericMotionEvent`), scroll from `ACTION_SCROLL` with the device's scroll factor, and the trackpad gestures Android reports. The conversion is plain Go over plain values (as on the web, §23.4), tested in the normal run.
- **Keys.** `KeyEvent`s become `KeyData` through tables that `glutter-gen keys` generates from Flutter's key data (the Android scan codes and key codes), with Flutter's synthesized modifier and lock events. As in Flutter, the view takes every key event at once; when the framework did not handle one, the embedding dispatches it again to the rest of the view hierarchy and the system (so Back, volume and the soft keyboard's keys still work).
- **Mouse cursors** use `PointerIcon` with Flutter's mapping (API 24 and later).
- The system gesture areas come from the insets (§24.7). Drag and drop with other apps is not in 1.6 (`StartDrag` fails with `DragUnsupported`).

**Decided in step 25.6.** The conversion chain is plain Go in `internal/platform/android`: the Java view (`GlutterView`) passes every `MotionEvent` (`onTouchEvent`, `onGenericMotionEvent`, `onHoverEvent`) to `Native.motionEvent` as flat arrays, `decodeMotion` (`motion.go`) drops what is malformed, `touch.go` is `AndroidTouchProcessor` and `pointerconv.go` is the engine's `PointerDataPacketConverter` (it adds the synthesized `add`, `remove`, `hover` and `cancel` events and the pointer numbers). One `MotionEvent` is one batch for `OnPointer`, in order, on the UI thread. A mouse that Android reports with no button state, which is what `adb shell input mouse tap` does, takes Flutter's path for a Chromebook's trackpad: a pan-zoom gesture, not a press. Keys: `KeyEvent`s go to `Native.keyEvent`, `keyboard.go` is `KeyEmbedderResponder` with `KeyboardManager`'s character combiner and `deadchar.go` its dead-key table; the tables (`keymap_gen.go`) are written by `glutter-gen keys -android-out`. The view answers `true` for every key at once and the platform answers the framework's decision with `host.KeyHandled`; the activity dispatches an unhandled key again (`GlutterView.keyHandled`). Mouse cursors: `window.SetCursor` maps a system cursor to a `PointerIcon` type (`cursor.go`, the table of `MouseCursorPlugin.java`), `host.SetCursor` sets it on the view from the main thread. The debug-only dev events script (§24.13) has the steps `wait:<duration>`, `tap:x,y`, `swipe:x1,y1,x2,y2,ms` and `key:KEYCODE_X`; the Java side injects each through the view's own dispatch, so they take the path of the user's input. Back is not a key event on a target API of 33 or later (the system calls `OnBackInvokedDispatcher`): it comes in step 25.9 with predictive back. Deviations are in §12.10.

### 24.6 Text input, IME and clipboard

Port from `TextInputPlugin.java`, `InputConnectionAdaptor.java`, `ListenableEditingState.java` and `ImeSyncDeferringInsetsCallback.java`.

- **The input connection.** While a text field has the focus, the view's `onCreateInputConnection` gives the IME a `GlutterInputConnection`. It keeps a copy of the editing state (text, selection, composing range) that the framework sends with `SetTextInput`, answers the IME from it on the main thread, applies the IME's edits to it and sends each change to the UI thread as `TextInputEvent`s. The contract with the framework stays the one of Linux and the web (§23.5 "Decided in step 21.3"); what Android needs beyond it is added to `ui.TextInputState` and `ui.TextInputEvent` without changing the old fields.
- **The soft keyboard** is shown and hidden with `InputMethodManager` when text input is enabled and disabled. `EditorInfo` comes from the configuration: `inputType` from `TextInputType`, obscured text, autocorrect, suggestions and capitalization; `imeOptions` from `TextInputAction`, and the action comes back as the field's action. The keyboard's height is the bottom `ViewInsets` (§24.7), and its animation is followed frame by frame where Android reports it (API 30 and later), so a `Scaffold` moves with the keyboard.
- **The clipboard** is `ClipboardManager`, text only, as on the web: reading is asked of the main thread and answered as an event (`ClipboardReadTimeout` when the context ends first). There is no primary selection (`ClipboardPrimaryUnavailable`).
- **Spell check** is `TextServicesManager`'s spell checker service, behind the framework's `SpellCheckService`, as Flutter's `SpellCheckPlugin` does (step 26.4).
- Autofill, `TextInputType.none` with a hardware keyboard, and content inserted by the keyboard (stickers, GIFs) are not in 1.6.

**Decided in step 25.8.** The contract is the web's (§23.5 "Decided in step 21.3"), and so is the model: `TextEditor` is used as it is, moved in step 25.9 from `internal/platform/web` to `internal/platform/textedit` (with `EditingState` and `Composition`) because an Android binary has no web backend (`TestBuildsHaveTheirOwnBackends`), and `web` keeps aliases (it is plain Go over UTF-16 text, a selection and a composing range, which is what the `Editable` of the Java side holds), so that the framework keeps the caret and the selection, handles Backspace and the arrow keys itself, and only text and composing text come back from the input method. Nothing was added to `ui.TextInputState` and `ui.TextInputEvent`. On the Java side `GlutterInputConnection` (a `BaseInputConnection`, the port of `InputConnectionAdaptor`) edits a `ListenableEditingState` (an `Editable` with a batch counter and change listeners, the port of `ListenableEditingState`); when the outermost batch ends, `Native.textEdit(seq, text, base, extent, composeBase, composeExtent)` sends the whole editing state with a number that counts the edits, `bridge.textEdit` queues it, and `textInput.edit` (`textinput.go`) gives it to the editor, which returns the `TextInputEvent` (`DeleteBefore`, `DeleteAfter`, `Commit`, `Preedit`); the engine takes it at once through `OnTextInputSync`. A selection that only the input method moved is not sent (a cursor moved by swiping on the space bar does not move the framework's caret). The framework's state comes back as `window.SetTextInput`: `textInput.setState` sends `opTextStart` when a field gets the focus or its configuration changes (`editorConfigOf`: the input type, the IME options and the action from the purpose, the hints and the enter key, the port of `inputTypeFromTextInputType`; a field with no action is Done, a multiline one has none), `opTextState` with the state when it differs from the editing state of the Java side (the editor says so), and `opTextStop` when no field has the focus. **The number of edits settles the race** between the input method (on the main thread) and the framework (on the UI thread): a state carries the number of the last edit that Go handled, and the Java side ignores a state when it has sent a later edit since, because that state is stale and the echo of the later edit follows. The action of the keyboard (`performEditorAction`) is an Enter key down and up dispatched to the view, which the framework turns into the field's action or a new line (`TextInput.HandleUnhandledKey`); Backspace and the other keys that the input method sends as key events go the same way. The soft keyboard is shown with `restartInput` and `showSoftInput` (or the `WindowInsetsController` from API 30) and hidden by `opTextStop`. The activity has `windowSoftInputMode=adjustResize`, as Flutter's template; the keyboard's height is `ViewInsets.Bottom` (§24.7), and from API 30 `WindowInsetsAnimation.Callback` reports it at each step of the animation. The clipboard is `ClipboardManager` (`clipboard.go`): a write is a command, a read is `opClipboardGet` with a number and the answer `Native.clipboardText`, which the UI thread gives to the function of the call (`ClipboardReadTimeout` when the context ends first, once); only text; the primary selection is `ClipboardPrimaryUnavailable` and another format reads empty and cannot be written (`AndroidClipboardFormatUnsupported`). The dev events script has `text:`, `compose:a,b,c` and `action:enter`, which the Java side sends through the active input connection as an input method does (a warning in the log when there is none). Not done, as in §23.5: autofill, a selection moved by the input method alone, content inserted by the keyboard.


**Decided in step 26.4.** The framework asks the engine through `ui.SpellCheckHost` (`PlatformDispatcher.SpellCheckSupported` and `SpellCheck(ctx, locale, text, done)`), the stand-in for Flutter's `flutter/spellcheck` channel; the engine forwards it to the implicit view's window when that has it (`internal/engine/spellcheck.go`), and Linux and the web have none, so spell check stays off there, as in Flutter. A misspelled range is a `ui.SpellCheckSpan` in UTF-8 byte offsets (Flutter's are UTF-16). On Android `SpellCheck.java` is the port of `SpellCheckPlugin`: it opens one `SpellCheckerSession` with `TextServicesManager.newSpellCheckerSession(null, locale, listener, true)` (the language of the spell checker's settings is followed), calls `getSentenceSuggestions(..., 5)` and answers with the ranges that have at least one non-empty suggestion (Flutter's Samsung workaround), as lines of `start`, `end` and the suggestions separated by tabs, in UTF-16 offsets, which `parseSpellCheck` turns into byte offsets. The command is operation 24 (`A` the number of the request, `S` the locale, a line feed and the text) and the answer is `Native.spellCheckResult(request, code, text)` (code 0 the ranges, code 1 the reason); `spellcheck.go` keeps the requests by number and answers each once on the UI thread, never before the call returned, like the clipboard. **Differences from Flutter's plugin:** a second request while one waits fails in Java with the same message as Flutter (`Previous spell check request still pending.`, tag `AndroidSpellCheckFailed`); a request that the context ends fails with `AndroidSpellCheckEnded`, as does one that waits when a new activity is created (its session is gone); a missing session (the user turned the spell checker off, or no service has the language) is an `AndroidSpellCheckFailed` instead of a null pointer. `SpellCheckSupported` is what the activity last reported with `Native.spellCheckSupport` (when it is created and each time its window gets the focus; `TextServicesManager.isSpellCheckerEnabled` from API 31, always true before); the session is closed when the activity is destroyed. The dev events script needs no new event: `text:` types the word and `tap:` opens the toolbar. Device tests use `examples/dev/android-spell` and the spell checker of the Google images (Gboard's `AndroidSpellCheckerService`, selected on `glutter-37` and `glutter-26`).

### 24.7 View, insets, system UI and settings

- **Edge to edge.** The view draws behind the status bar, the navigation bar and the display cutout, as Android 15 requires of apps that target it. `ViewPadding` is the union of the system bars' and the cutout's insets, `ViewInsets` is the keyboard's, `SystemGestureInsets` the system gestures' (all in physical pixels, from `WindowInsets`, through the older calls below API 30). So `SafeArea` and `MediaQuery` work as in Flutter.
- **Metrics.** The physical size is the surface's; the device pixel ratio is `DisplayMetrics.density`. `Displays()` comes from `DisplayManager`, with the refresh rate, and changes call `OnDisplaysChanged`.
- **System UI** (port the framework side of `services/system_chrome.dart`, and `PlatformPlugin.java` for the platform side): `SystemChrome.SetPreferredOrientations` (the activity's requested orientation), `SetEnabledSystemUIMode` (leanBack, immersive, immersiveSticky, edgeToEdge, through `WindowInsetsController`), `SetSystemUIOverlayStyle` and `SetSystemUIChangeCallback`; the overlay style that the app bar's `AnnotatedRegion` gives is sent after each frame, as Flutter's `RenderView` does. Fullscreen (`SetState`) is immersive mode. `KeepScreenOn` sets `FLAG_KEEP_SCREEN_ON`.
- **Feedback.** `services.HapticFeedback` (port `services/haptic_feedback.dart`) uses `View.performHapticFeedback` with Flutter's constants; `SystemSound` uses `View.playSoundEffect`. On Linux and the web both do nothing, as in Flutter.
- **Settings**: brightness from the night mode of the configuration; the text scale from `Configuration.fontScale`, non-linear on Android 14 and later as Flutter's `TextScaler` makes it; the locales; the 24-hour clock; animations off when the animator duration scale is 0; bold text (`fontWeightAdjustment`, API 31); high contrast text. Changes arrive through `onConfigurationChanged` and the settings observers, and call `OnSettings`.

**Decided in step 25.7.** The framework side is a host interface, `ui.SystemChromeHost` (`ui/system_chrome.go`): the engine implements it for every platform and forwards each call to the view's window when the window implements it too (only Android's does), so that the desktop and the web do nothing, as in Flutter. `services.SystemChrome`, `services.HapticFeedback` and `services.SystemSound` are instances (no statics: `WidgetsBinding.SystemChrome()` and the others) on the dispatcher, which stands in for the `flutter/platform` channel. `RenderView` sends the overlay style of the layer tree after each frame through a hook that the view widget sets. On the Android side the window sends one command, `GlutterActivity.command(op, a, b, c, d, s)` (`systemui.go`), to the main thread, where `SystemUI.java` (a port of `PlatformPlugin.java` that uses the platform's `WindowInsetsController` and not AndroidX) does it: the requested orientation (the set of orientations is turned into one `ActivityInfo` value by `requestedOrientation`, a port of `PlatformChannel.decodeOrientations`), the modes of the bars (the modes that hide them use `WindowInsetsController.hide`, because an app that targets API 36 or later has the old `SYSTEM_UI_FLAG_*` flags ignored; below API 30 the flags), the overlay style, haptic feedback with Flutter's constants, `playSoundEffect(CLICK)` (the other sounds do nothing, as in Flutter), `FLAG_KEEP_SCREEN_ON`. The window is edge to edge from `onCreate` (`setDecorFitsSystemWindows(false)` from API 30, layout flags below, transparent bars, the cutout mode `ALWAYS` from API 30 and `SHORT_EDGES` below). The insets are worked out in Java, as `FlutterView.onApplyWindowInsets` does (the bars and the cutout and waterfall edges for `ViewPadding`, the input method for `ViewInsets`, the system gestures; below API 30 the heuristics of `guessBottomKeyboardInset`) and passed as twelve numbers to `Native.insets`; a change of them is a change of the metrics. Go keeps the state and reports `OnMetrics`. The settings are one text of `key=value` pairs (`SystemSettings.describe`, read by `parseSettings`), sent before `main` starts, on `onConfigurationChanged` and from `ContentObserver`s (the animator duration scale, the transition animation scale and the clock setting) and the contrast listener: the night mode of `uiMode`, `fontScale`, `LocaleList` (the locales come as BCP 47 tags), `DateFormat.is24HourFormat`, animations (off when the animator duration scale **or** the transition animation scale is 0), bold text (`fontWeightAdjustment` of 300 or more, API 31), high contrast (`UiModeManager.getContrast() > 0`, API 34, as Flutter's `AccessibilityBridge`). From API 34 `platform.Settings.ScaleFontSize` is set: the dispatcher scales a font size by asking `GlutterActivity.scaledFontSize` (`TypedValue.applyDimension` with `sp`, divided by the density) once for the whole sizes next to it and interpolating, with the results remembered until the settings change, as `PlatformDispatcher.scaleFontSize` does; `SystemTextScaler` of `MediaQuery` uses it. `Displays()` and the metrics follow a `DisplayListener` (`Native.displayChanged`). The system UI change callback is told when the bars appear or disappear (API 30 and later, from the insets' visibility). Flutter's `ZeroSides` heuristic for the navigation bar's side in landscape below API 30 is not ported: those versions keep the side padding. `Window.SetState(Fullscreen)` is the immersive sticky mode and `SetState(Normal)` edge to edge. The app's debug builds log each command (`GlutterJava`), which is how the device tests see the haptic feedback and the sounds.

### 24.8 Lifecycle, back navigation and exit

- **Lifecycle**, ported from Flutter's `LifecycleChannel` and `FlutterActivityAndFragmentDelegate`: `Resumed` while the activity is resumed and its window has the focus, `Inactive` while it is resumed without the focus or paused, `Hidden` and then `Paused` when it is stopped, `Detached` when it is destroyed. The platform reports the lifecycle itself, so the engine follows it instead of deriving it from the windows (§13). While the app is `Paused` or has no surface, no frame is drawn.
- **Back.** On API 33 and later the activity registers an `OnBackInvokedCallback` while the framework wants the back gesture (`SystemNavigator.SetFrameworkHandlesBack`, which `PopScope` and the `Navigator` call), and on API 34 and later an `OnBackAnimationCallback`, whose start, progress, commit and cancel become the framework's predictive back events (`HandleStartBackGesture`, `HandleUpdateBackGestureProgress`, `HandleCommitBackGesture`, `HandleCancelBackGesture`). Below API 33 `onBackPressed` is a pop. A back that the framework does not handle finishes the activity, as Android's default does (measured, step 25.9).
- **`SystemNavigatorPop`** finishes the activity, and `RunApp` returns. `RequestAppExit` asks the framework, then finishes the activity.
- **Memory.** `onTrimMemory` at the levels Flutter uses calls the framework's memory pressure handlers (`WidgetsBindingObserver.DidHaveMemoryPressure`) and the renderer's (§24.4).

**Decided in step 25.9.** The platform reports the lifecycle with the window's focus: `Resumed` only while the activity is resumed **and** has the focus (`appState(focused)` in `lifecycle.go`), `Inactive` while it is resumed without the focus or paused, `Paused` when it is stopped and `Detached` when it is destroyed (the framework makes `Hidden` on the way, as it does for Flutter); a change of the focus is a lifecycle change too. Back reaches the framework through the engine's `platform.BackHandler` (the window and the engine forward optional interfaces by type assertion) as `ui.PlatformDispatcher` callbacks (`PopRoute`, `StartBackGesture`, `UpdateBackGestureProgress`, `CommitBackGesture`, `CancelBackGesture`, `NotifyMemoryPressure`); the framework tells the host whether it wants back through `ui.BackHost.SetFrameworkHandlesBack`, which `SystemNavigator.SetFrameworkHandlesBack` (called by `WidgetsApp` when a `PopScope` or a `Navigator` can handle a pop, on the Android target platform only) sets, and the activity registers its `OnBackInvokedCallback` only while it is true (`BackNavigation.java`: an `OnBackAnimationCallback` on API 34 and later, a plain callback on API 33, and `onBackPressed` below). **A back that the framework does not handle is the system's**: the callback is unregistered, and Android finishes the activity (`Detached`, the process stays cached and a new start is a new activity in a living process, §24.2); this was measured on `glutter-37` and `glutter-26`. A back from a button or a key arrives on API 34 and later as a gesture whose edge is `EDGE_NONE`: the platform drops its start and progress, and the commit is a plain pop. The activity's position in a gesture is in pixels of the view and goes to the framework in logical pixels. `onTrimMemory` from `TRIM_MEMORY_RUNNING_LOW` (level 10), once a frame has been drawn, calls `PlatformDispatcher.NotifyMemoryPressure`; **the renderer is not trimmed** (no cache is released on memory pressure: only the framework's handlers run, recorded as a follow-up). A new activity in a living process (`Native.activityCreated`) asks again for the back callback and the text input the framework wants. The dev events script has `back:pop` (the system's back, through the registered callback or `onBackPressed`), `back:gesture` (start, two steps of progress and commit) `back:cancel` (start, one step and cancel), and, from step 26.3, `back:hold` (start and two steps up to one half, the finger stays down) and `back:release` (cancels a held gesture), for the proof image; below API 34 `back:gesture` is a plain pop and `back:cancel` does nothing. **Device tests** use `examples/dev/android-back` (a home page, a second page and a third page under a `PopScope` that cannot pop) instead of `examples/navigation`, which needs taps on a layout the tests would have to know; `examples/navigation` is a manual check (it makes its own `PageRouteBuilder` pages, which have no predictive transition, so the step 26.3 proof and manual check use `examples/dev/android-back`, whose pages are `MaterialPageRoute`s). Whether a route animates with the system's predictive back animation depends on the theme's page transitions builder, not on the platform.

### 24.9 Fonts, assets, files and logging

- **Fonts.** The system fonts are in `/system/fonts` and are described by `/system/etc/fonts.xml` (and the `fonts_customization.xml` of `/product/etc`, where it exists). The Android font matcher reads them as Skia's Android font manager does: the families and their aliases (`sans-serif` is Roboto), and the fallback chain with its languages, so that CJK text uses the font of the locale. go-text's `fontscan` is given its cache directory in the app's cache directory. The bundled Roboto and Material Icons stay the first fonts, as on Linux. The emoji font of Android 13 and later is COLRv1, which Glutter draws (§9); older versions have a CBDT emoji font, whose bitmaps are drawn (step 25.10).
- **Assets** come from `RunAppOpts.Assets` (usually an `embed.FS`, which is inside `libapp.so`), as on Linux. Network images need `-permission android.permission.INTERNET`.
- **Directories.** Before `main` runs, the embedding sets `HOME`, `TMPDIR`, `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, `XDG_DATA_HOME` and `XDG_CACHE_HOME` to the app's private files and cache directories, so that `os.UserConfigDir`, `os.UserCacheDir` and `os.TempDir` work and the examples keep their data where the app may write.
- **Logging.** An Android process has no terminal, so standard output and standard error are sent to `logcat` line by line, with the app ID as the tag and Info, Warn and Error priorities from the `slog` level of the line. `glutter-android run` shows them.

**Decided in step 25.10.** The Android font matcher (`internal/text/androidfonts`, plain Go) reads `/system/etc/fonts.xml` and the `fonts_customization.xml` of `/oem`, `/product` and `/vendor`, as Skia's Android font manager does (`reference/skia/src/ports/SkFontMgr_android_parser.cpp` and `SkFontMgr_android.cpp`, at the revision Flutter's `DEPS` pins; its BSD-3-Clause license is in `third_party/skia`). It builds the named families (with their aliases and weighted aliases), the unnamed fallback families (with `lang`, `variant` and `fallbackFor`) and a style set per family. `Match` returns the requested family's nearest-weight font first, then the fallback chain in Skia's order: elegant variants before compact ones, then the families written for the requested language and its more general tags (the script is inferred, so `zh-TW` takes the Traditional collection index), then the named family and its `fallbackFor` families, the families with no `fallbackFor`, the other named families, and the rest, in file order. `Fallback` returns the first font of that chain that has a glyph for the character. An unknown non-generic family gives no fonts and no error, as Skia gives no typeface; the generic `emoji` puts the `und-Zsye` family first (on API 26 the family whose file name has "emoji"). `New` fails with `AndroidFontsXMLInvalid` or `AndroidFontsUnavailable` when nothing usable was read, and then `engine.SystemFontMatcher` logs it and returns nil, so the collection scans `/system/fonts`. The language of a request is the locale of the text (`Text.Locale`, else `Localizations`), as in Flutter, so a Han text with no locale gets the file order (Simplified Chinese first); `Options.Locale` is not set by the engine. The bundled Roboto and Material Icons stay first. Android 8 to 12 have a CBDT emoji font (Noto Color Emoji as a bitmap font), which the layout engine draws (`internal/text/layout/bitmap.go`, on Android and on Linux): the strike for a size is chosen as Skia does (`chooseBitmapStrike`: the exact ppem, else the smallest strike that is at least the size, else the largest), the PNG is decoded once into a `ui.Image`, and each glyph is a `ui.Picture` cached per face, glyph and strike, placed by the strike's bearings and scaled by size/ppem with a medium filter, on the drawing path of COLR (one `OpDrawPicture`), so every renderer draws it with no change; the text color does not matter. A glyph with no valid bitmap draws nothing, and an image that cannot be decoded is reported once through `OnFontError` with the tag `BitmapGlyphInvalid`. sbix (PNG and JPEG) goes through the same code but has no test; SVG glyphs, TIFF strikes and monochrome bitmaps are not drawn. The test font of the unit tests is built in memory by the test (`cbdtfont_test.go`: a small sfnt with CBLC and CBDT and PNG glyphs), because no font tool is installed and a subset of Noto Color Emoji is not needed; the device tests use the real fonts of the emulators.

**Decided in step 25.2.** The pipe replaces file descriptors 1 and 2 when `Native.start` runs, a goroutine cuts the text into lines, and the priority of a line is Warn or Error when it has the level of an `slog` record in the text form (`level=WARN`), the JSON form (`"level":"ERROR"`) or the default logger's (`2006/01/02 15:04:05 WARN msg`), Debug for DEBUG, and Info otherwise (`linePriority`, plain Go).

### 24.10 Accessibility

Port from `AccessibilityBridge.java` (and the parts of `semantics` that its messages carry).

- `Services().Accessibility()` is the Android bridge. TalkBack and the other accessibility services turn it on (`AccessibilityManager`'s state and the touch exploration state, as Flutter reads them), and the framework then makes the semantics tree.
- The UI thread sends each `ui.SemanticsUpdate` to the main thread; `GlutterAccessibility` keeps its own copy of the tree, answers `createAccessibilityNodeInfo` and `findFocus` from it, and sends actions (tap, long press, scroll, increase, decrease, copy, cut, paste, set selection, set text, dismiss, focus) to the UI thread as `SemanticsActionEvent`s. The events Flutter sends (window content changed, accessibility focus, live regions, announcements, scrolled) are sent too.

**Decided in step 25.11.** `Services().Accessibility()` is the bridge of `accessibility.go`.
